Introduction

*Uyun al-Akhbar wa Funun al-Athar* ("The Springs of Reports and the Arts of Antiquities") is the major historical work of Idris 'Imad al-Din ibn al-Hasan al-Anf (794-872 AH / 1392-1468 CE), the nineteenth absolute da'i of the Tayyibi Ismaili da'wa in Yemen. Written in seven volumes, each of which the author calls a *sab'* ("seven"), it is the only comprehensive history of the Ismaili Imams -- from the earliest times through the Fatimid era -- written by an Ismaili author. This first volume covers the life of the Prophet Muhammad.

Idris led the Yemeni da'wa for nearly forty years, from 832/1428 until his death, and as its head he had access to the Ismaili literary heritage preserved in Yemen, much of which has since been lost. Beyond this history, he also wrote *Nuzhat al-Afkar* and *Rawdat al-Akhbar* -- covering later Ismaili history in Yemen and his own era -- along with theological and polemical works.

This edition is a critical edition of the Arabic text, prepared jointly by the Institute of Ismaili Studies (London) and the French Institute for the Near East (Damascus), and published in the Ismaili Texts and Translations series. The volumes were edited by a team of scholars -- Ahmed Shalilat, Ma'mun al-Sagherji, Yusuf Fattoum, Mahmoud Fakhoury, and Ayman Fouad Sayyid -- under the academic direction of Farhad Daftary.

**[Foreword of the Book]**

Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds, Creator of all mankind, who raised up the heavens and the surface of the two earths without support or helper, and who saturated the presses with certain water, and who chose His Messenger Muhammad(2) from among the messengers who call to the reward He has prepared in the Gardens of Bliss,(3) bearing glad tidings and warning those who disobey Allah of His punishment. It was His pleasure to be well pleased with the Prophets, and He chose them to guide all worshippers and followers.

The Eyes of News and the Arts of Archaeology — by Imad al-Din Idris bin Hassan (The First Seven)

I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, the One and Only, who chose His prophets and guardians to be guides, and who delivered His servants from destruction and rebellion through them. He then distinguished among them the one He placed above all the rest by what He alone bestowed upon him, and guided humanity — through the Torah, the Gospel, through Muhammad and Ahmad — after they had been wandering in their misguidance, their associationism and their ignorance,(1) like scattered rays, worshipping idols [1/z] in place of the Most Merciful, straying in their disbelief and tyranny. They bowed to other than the Creator of mankind and jinn, disobeying Allah (Glory be to Him) and obeying the devil.

[2] So Allah (Glory be to Him) guided him — the best of all to be guided — and sent him (Glory be to Him and exalted be He) as the Seal of the Messengers, calling him to the most excellent laws and ways, and guiding through him with wealth those who had gone astray from the Hereafter.

I bear witness that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, in whom the finest of religions was established, to whom the verses of al-Furqan were revealed, and whom He sent to guide both mankind and jinn, addressing them on his tongue — may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Allah (Glory be to Him) said: *"O assembly of jinn and mankind, if you are able to pass beyond the regions of the heavens and the earth, then pass — you will not pass except by authority."*

I bear witness that he is the Messenger of Allah, sent as a guide, a bearer of glad tidings, a warner, and a caller to Allah with His permission, and a shining lamp — a prophet through whom Allah destroyed disbelief(2) utterly, and upon whom He bestowed favor above all His Prophets,(3) bequeathing to his followers gardens and silk, with rivers of goodness and bliss flowing through them. And He prays against his enemies and those who are ungrateful to him.

(1) "B": "their ignorance."
(2) "B": "the disbelievers."
(3) "B": "a great favor."


<!-- page 37 -->
He designated Ali ibn Abi Talib as his helper, supporter, and partner — his vizier and brother, as Aaron was to Moses. I will descend with the Prophet into it, and with his wife and their two sons: "Allah only wishes to remove impurity from you, O People of the House, and to purify you thoroughly" (al-Ahzab: 33–43). Allah made His bounty known through the mouth of His Messenger and rendered it famous. And it was revealed concerning them: "They feed the poor, the orphan, and the captive out of love for Him" (Verse 8). May Allah bless him and grant him peace — the guardian who strove against the disbelievers and the hypocrites — and may Allah fulfill through him His grace and the completion of religion. He made His appointed ones, who hold the Imamate until the Day of Resurrection, the inheritors, and chose them as those aware of the two worlds. Among his pure sons and his two luminous heirs: Abu Muhammad al-Hasan and Abu Abdallah al-Husayn (may Allah be pleased with him), it was said: "Do not take two gods" (al-Nahl: Verse 51). And the pure Imams from the descendants of Husayn ibn Ali, whom Allah has placed in every age — each chosen by God in his time for the community of his grandfather — upheld the law of their grandfather without deviation. They inherited, generation after generation, the supreme station of the Imamate. Among them, those through whom Allah has made the light of His clear proof manifest, and some who have concealed themselves as the sun is hidden in the darkness of night, patient with whatever afflicted them. The heir to their pure station and their enduring successor will carry the word of the Imamate until the Last Day — from his son, the master of the age and time, the guardian of the people of religion and faith.

<!-- page 38 -->
And salvation for those who follow him from torment, and safety — for they offer the finest prayers and peace. The night deepened in darkness, a shining star rose, the sky was adorned with stars and constellations, and the sun illuminated the barren land.

[On the Will and the Imamate]

After this: when Allah (exalted be He) sent His Prophets and Messengers, He did not establish His religion on the strength of the first two alone. Rather, in His wisdom, justice, mercy, and virtue, He appointed guardians, successors, and guides for His prophets to follow them — prophets and guardians whom God selected and chose. He freed the community of Muhammad (may Allah bless him and his family) through those who guide them and succeed him after Allah took him — even though Allah (exalted be He) had sealed prophethood with his prophecy and made him the last of His messengers, guided to salvation. The interruption of prophethood passed to successors who themselves are in need of guidance, who know the way and stand upright in accordance with the purified Shari'ah and its method. This was not something the community could determine on its own; it does not fall to them to make or settle matters in the religion of Allah. The Imamate is the foundation of religion, by which it is upheld, and through the Imams of Truth, obedience to Allah the Living, the Self-Sustaining, is made valid. It was not possible for the community to converge on a single person by their own accord, nor could such a matter be known through reports alone, without a clear trace — and who would determine it, and from where? Disagreement arose among the Companions after their Prophet, and they fought one another in grief.

(1) An addition from "B".
(2) An addition from "B".

<!-- page 39 -->
The Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) said, in a hadith affirmed in sound transmission:

"The nation of my brother Moses divided into seventy-one sects, of which one band survived and the rest are in the Fire. The nation of my brother 'Isa divided into seventy-two sects, of which one company survived and the rest are in the Fire. My nation will divide into seventy-three sects, of which one division will survive and the rest are in the Fire."

To begin with, they sold their Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) on the question of what they disagreed about — even though, had they obeyed him in those whom he appointed over them, they would not have deviated from the truth, nor gone astray. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) had forbidden this, saying: "A Muslim should not sleep two nights without his will written at his bedside." He would not have left the community in a disordered and neglected state, or failed to entrust those who would manage its affairs and raise a guiding banner for the people of religion. Nor would his guardian and the Imams of truth after him have abandoned the community to wander without raising anyone to guide creation. Since the guardianship extended from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) to the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, and from him to one guardian after another — in accordance with clear designations, as is well known — even though many people, generally and specifically, denied it, and most of the community rejected it out of rebelliousness.

(2) See: Da'a'im al-Islam, 345/2.

<!-- page 40 -->
They were as the Almighty said: "They denied it, though their own souls were convinced of its truth, out of wrongdoing and arrogance" (al-Naml: Verse 14). From the righteous scholars and the rightly guided, much has been narrated and written on this subject — classifications so numerous they cannot be counted in a month of sustained effort, great as that effort may be.

[Plan of the Book]

I resolved to enter their field with full determination, to exert my effort, and to acknowledge my own shortcomings. I am writing this book, beginning with a summary of the biography of the Prophet — may Allah bless him and his family — and then turning to mention the guardian (peace be upon him) and what befell him after the Prophet: his patience until Allah fulfilled His command to him, and his jihad against the renegades, the transgressors, and the deviants. We will then confine ourselves to an account of the Imams of guidance — from the God of the rightly guided — those who follow in their example, those who appear by the command of Allah and those who remain hidden, those who draw their swords and those who take refuge in piety, those who are afflicted and patient, and those who dispute and oppose them. The mourners for them, and those who abandoned their call and departed from it — all this in a concise manner; I have not taken the path of excessive length, and the best of speech is that which conveys its meaning with precision. Allah is sufficient for me, and He is the Trustee; from Him I seek help and success.

[5]

(1) "B": "By His Command."

<!-- page 41 -->
And guidance to the right path — from the lights of the blessings of His saints I draw, and through them I hope for guidance along the path of fulfillment and pursuit. I have titled this book *The Eyes of the News and the Authenticity of the Traces* — a record of the biography of the Chosen Prophet and his guardian Ali ibn Abi Talib, the vanquisher of the disbelievers, and the pure ones — may the prayers of the Almighty, the Forgiving, be upon them. It has been transmitted alongside accounts of those men who are a barrier between them, and their guardian al-Ridha al-Kitti is referred to among them as "Haidar." I wished to seek blessing through their remembrance, and I did not want this book of mine to be incomplete in honoring them. At its outset, I mention the virtues of the fathers of the Chosen One, and what Allah has bestowed upon them — that which the people of ignorance cast against them by following the path of the disbelievers. We seek refuge in Allah from ignorance. And the people of virtue dealt with their slaughter, rebelliousness against them, and transgression. There is no power save with Allah the Almighty, and from Him I seek success and recompense. Thanks be through His Prophet Muhammad and his pure family — may prayers and submission be upon them — and I ask Him to make my reward for what I have intended abundant, and to grant me striving in what pleases Him and in what pleases His guardians. For I have rendered this pure for His noble face, and I seek no reward from any of His creation, and no thanks.

**Mention of Some of the Virtues of the Fathers of the Prophet**
*Peace be upon him and his family*
*From Ishmael*

And what Allah has bestowed upon them of great bounty, and what He has singled them out for of purity — as mentioned in texts and references. We say, with the help of Allah the Almighty, that it is known to those versed in private and public knowledge alike, and that all of Islam converges on the authenticity of the narration from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) in the saying:

"You and I, O Ali ibn Abi Talib, have been carried from the noble loins to the purified wombs."

Allah (exalted be He) said: "Surely the most honored of you in the sight of Allah is the most pious" (al-Hujurat: 13). The Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) said: "Nobility is piety." Allah (exalted be He) said: "Indeed, the polytheists are impure" (al-Tawbah: 28). The testimony of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) to his fathers regarding nobility is disclosed because they were

— upon piety and purity, and their freedom from associating partners with Allah — what the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) made plain. As for those who attribute shirk to them and repeat such words against the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family and his God) — clearly phrased — they have committed a grave slander and calumny, and such a person deserves punishment unless he purifies himself through repentance and disciplines himself in the next life. Allah (exalted be He) said through His Prophet Ibrahim (peace be upon him):

"Our Lord, I have settled some of my offspring in a valley with no vegetation, near Your Sacred House, our Lord, so that they may establish prayer. So make the hearts of people incline toward them and provide for them from fruits" (Ibrahim: 37).

This indicates that the call of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family) ensures that the Sacred House has never been empty of descendants of the Friend of Allah deserving of the honor of the Exalted — even if some among them were worshippers of idols who concealed their faith, as the believer in the household of Pharaoh concealed his, while manifesting praiseworthy qualities and good traits that others lacked. It is sufficient as a source of pride for them that they were among those through whom the light of pure lineage was transmitted, and that they were singled out for the birth of the Seal of the Messengers from their pure origins. Ibrahim al-Khalil commended to his two sons — Ishmael and Isaac, peace be upon them — that Isaac and his descendants dwell in the Holy Land, while Ishmael and his family settle at the Sacred House of Allah, around Zamzam and the Maqam. That Sacred House was the qibla of Ibrahim, toward which he prayed, to which he summoned people to perform the Hajj, and which he made known — he being the father of its custodians.

(1) An addition from "B."

<!-- page 44 -->
This is amply sufficient as clarification, proof, and demonstration of what they have been distinguished with. Allah (exalted be He) has granted them His bounty and blessings, even though mixed among them were people of polytheism who worshipped idols while taking pride in their lineage from Ibrahim (peace be upon him). From the pre-Islamic era there were ignorant people more astray than camels, and polytheists of Quraysh and other Arabs who could accompany the believer alongside the disbeliever, and the righteous alongside the wicked. Allah (exalted be He) said in His Holy Book, describing a believer speaking to one who disbelieved: "I disbelieve in Him who created you from dust" (al-Kahf: 37). He made Moses, Jesus, and the other prophets (peace be upon them) among the heirs of Isaac, and singled out Ishmael — the one designated for sacrifice (peace be upon him) — for the Prophet Muhammad (may Allah bless him and his family) to descend from his noble line and the best of his noble sons.

[7] Ishmael was the guardian of Abraham and his eldest son. Allah (exalted be He) said in His Holy Book: "And mention in the Book Ishmael — he was true to his promise, and he was a Messenger, a Prophet. He used to enjoin his family to prayer and zakat, and he was pleasing to his Lord" (55). His sons followed him in uprightness in the religion of their ancestor Ibrahim, and the mark of distinction passed from one to another until it reached Muhammad, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family). The children of Ishmael (peace be upon him) were twelve, the best of whom was Qaythar ibn Ishmael, whose mother was Ra'la, daughter of Madad ibn 'Amr al-Jarhami. It is said that when Ishmael died, he was buried in Mecca alongside his mother Hajar.

(1) Addition from Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 51/1.

<!-- page 45 -->
His son Qaythar grew up, and Qaythar himself fathered a son. The mark of distinction upon Qaythar was evident; the son of his paternal uncle, Ya'qub ibn Ishaq ibn Ibrahim (peace be upon him), saw in him the light of prophethood. Ya'qub gave him the glad tidings that the Prophet Muhammad (may Allah bless him and his family) would be among his descendants, and that the beginnings of his light were visible in the contours of his face. The mark of distinction continued to pass through the children of Ishmael, born to the Prophet — may Allah bless him and his family — while others branched away from the line of Ishmael's son. Muhammad (may Allah bless him and his family) was among his offspring and holds the right owed to him by his brothers — an honor well known to those who look carefully.

[Mention of the Origins of the Arab Tribes of the Hijaz, Tracing Back to Adnan]

This continued until it came to Adnan, who lived at the time of Musa ibn 'Imran. His story is well known, and his virtue is great. It is said that Shu'ayb, Moses, and al-Khidr (peace be upon them) met, from which there came preparation and readiness, and he was prepared for the best. Al-'As ibn Mardas al-Sulami says, concerning Ibn Adnan who had dealings with Ghassan until they expelled every expelled one: this is because 'Ak had married among the Ash'arites and belonged to them — not as those we do not wish to mention in order to examine them. But we have mentioned the fathers of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and his family and his God).

(1) See: his Diwan 62.
(2) An addition from "B."


<!-- page 46 -->
Ma'ad was born of four sons: Nizar ibn Ma'ad, who was their chief, along with an otter, a sniper, and a hand.

[8]

The children of the otter belong to the donkeys, and they have a story that is not the subject of this discussion.

The children of Nizar include: the Harmful, who was the dam of his children — Rabi'ah and Anmar.

[5/7] The children of Nizar are called "al-Hamra," while Rabi'ah is called "Rabi'ah of the Persians," and Anmar is called "Anmar the Donkey." This is because it is said that when Nizar gave his commands, he assigned the horse and the donkey to Rabi'ah.

Honor has never ceased to be tied to, and goodness transmitted through, the child of the Harmful line — running through the fathers of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family — until it culminated in the one called "Quraysh," to whom the Quraysh are attributed.

It has been said that their name is derived from the word for a shark. It is said: the shark is a sea creature that devours the smallest of whales and gathers them into the deep. A poet said:

*It was called Quraysh — Quraysha,*
*and Quraysh are those who dwell in the Bah.*
*You eat the wheat and the fat, and do not eat*
*in it for the two wings of feathers.*

(1) It is stated in certain manuscripts:

*And there was the name of the Quraysh Quraysha.*
*And Quraysh in the sea may eat the water —*
*riding from them to the two wings of feathers.*
*The ghee and the fat are hoarded, and there is no ease.*

Perhaps this is a Quranic allusion from the sources, confirming what most authorities have said. The poet is al-Mushmarj ibn 'Amr al-Humayri. See: Dictionary of Poets 504, Akhbar al-Dawla al-Abbasid 73, al-Mu'jam al-Kabir of al-Tabarani 240/10, Rabi' al-Abrar 438/4.

<!-- page 47 -->
Rather, the name Quraysh is derived from this because they prevail over other Arab tribes

[Quraysh and the Arabic Tongue: An Opinion by al-Farabi]

and hold authority over them.

Abu Nasr al-Farabi said in the first book of words and letters (1):

"The Quraysh were the finest of the Arabs in their command of words — easiest on the tongue [7/z] when pronounced, most pleasing to the ear, most precise in conveying what is held in the soul. Those from whom Arabic was transmitted, whom others imitated, and from whom the Arabic language was drawn, among the Arab tribes, were: Qays, Tamim, and Asad. These are those from whom (2) the greater part of it was taken, /and upon whom I rely for Arabic, syntax, and conjugation. Then, alongside these, certain metaphors [9] and usages were taken from still others, but nothing was taken from the remaining tribes.

In short, nothing has been taken from the city dwellers, nor from those who dwell in the wilderness, nor from those in border regions adjacent to foreign nations. Nothing was taken from the Lakhm, nor from anyone of the Judhām, for these were neighbors of the people of Mudhar. Nor from the otter, nor from Ghassan, nor from Iyad, for they were adjacent to the people of the Levant and in close contact with them, and most of them were Christians who recited in their prayers in a tongue other than their own. Nor

<!-- page 48 -->
from those who overcame the tiger, for they were adjacent to the Greeks on the island and were Christians. Nor from the firstborn, for they were adjacent to the Persians and the Nabataeans and in close contact with them. Nor from 'Abd al-Qays, for they were the inhabitants of Bahrain, in contact with the Persians and India. Nor from the people of Yemen originally, because of their contact with India and Abyssinia, the mingling of Abyssinian blood among them, and then their contact with the shark. Nor from Banu Hanifah and the inhabitants of al-Yamamah, nor from Thaqif, nor (1) from the inhabitants of Ta'if, because they all mingle with the nations of the world. Nor from those of the Hijaz region; for those who transmitted the language, when they first began transmitting the Arabic tongue, found that these people had already come into contact with foreign nations and their speech had become corrupted.

The Arabic language was transmitted from those who preserved it and recorded it in books. The scholars and craftsmen of this language were exclusively the people of Kufa and Basra, from among all the remaining Arabs. Their occupations were hunting, herding, and raiding. They were the strongest of souls, the hardest of hearts, the most resolute in spirit, the most resistant to subjugation, the most devoted to those they love, the least willing to submit to kings, the most upright in character, and the least prone [8/z] to abjectness and humiliation." This is what al-Farabi said.

He said: [Mention of Qusay ibn Kilab]

Then the lineage of the "fathers" (3) of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — converges, noble upon noble, all the way back to Qusay ibn Kilab.

(1) An increase from "B."
(2) "2": "Who." What has been confirmed is from "B."
(3) An increase from "B."

<!-- page 49 -->
His father Kilab ibn Murrah died when Qusay was a small child. His brother Zahra was older than him, and their mother was Fatima bint Sa'd ibn Sa'il.

Rabi'ah ibn Haram ibn 'Athra ibn Sa'd ibn Zayd ibn Manat came from Quda'ah to [10] Mecca after the death (1) of Kilab ibn Murrah. He married Fatima, the mother of Zahra and Qusay, and she went with him to his people — the Banu 'Udhra — taking Qusay with her. (2) Zahra, however, stayed behind with his people, and by then he was already a grown man.

Qusay's name was Zayd, and his mother named him Qusay — meaning "far away" — because he had been taken far from his homeland. Qusay grew up in the household of Rabi'ah, and though he was raised far from Mecca, he yearned to return and follow his origins. He set out with the pilgrims of the Qudha'ah tribe, and when he reached Mecca he stayed there, growing into a man of honor, righteousness, and noble character.

He was a handsome man — slender, composed, wise, commanding reverence, chaste, and generous. When Allah honored him by making him the forefather of His Messenger and caused the good lineage to flow through his descendants, He brought his qualities to their fullest perfection in virtue and honor.

Al-Harith ibn Zalim said of Banu Qusay:

*And their brothers were attributed to Loay —*
*should Tha'labah ibn Sa'd ever die.*
*And they are the honorable among all living people,*
*of lineage generous and free from folly.*

(1) "B": "Death."
(2) Increasing the virtues and defects [Leaf 5/z].
(3) The verses in: Ansab al-Ashraf 49/1, Al-'Iqd al-Farid 149/5, Virtues and Defects [leaf 5/z–6/f].

<!-- page 50 -->
Offerings of God to Banu Qusay

If he grows angry with them, then he is of them. He made them reciters through whom one draws near to Allah, for they are the custodians of the House — the people of the watering, al-Rafada, al-Siyada, al-Riyas, and al-Liwa'.

They upheld by inheritance the religion of their fathers Ibrahim and Isma'il, peace be upon them: welcoming guests in the villages, providing for the pilgrim, honoring the Haram, protecting it from injustice and impiety, suppressing the oppressor, and defending the oppressed.

[Razah's Poetry in Support of Qusay]

Qusay's authority had grown great in Mecca, and the city had placed its reins in his hands. He fought Khuza'ah and Banu Bakr; some of the otter tribes were killed alongside him, and his brother Razah ibn Rabi'ah came to his aid with his people [11] from the otter. He had called upon the Quraysh to join him in war, but they hesitated and held back. His brother Razah then came to his support, gathering his own people and those who obeyed him from the otter tribes. On this occasion Razah said:

*The prophet said: answer the call of the dear one,*
*and lift the heavy burden from us.*
*We rose to him, driving the horses,*
*while the day hid itself, lest it descend.*
*We marched through the night until morning —*
*for they love us: from Qusay a messenger came.*
*They were swift in curdling the cat,*
*making the path of the Prophet easier to tread.*
*When they passed by the prostrate ones —*
*Razzah (2):*

*And when from Qusay a messenger came* [9/00]

*March on — a thousand strong, the messenger's companion.*
*You have an arena to camp through the night,*
*and they walked with the living, loosing their bonds.*

*We drove them from the land of the King,*
*and from every direction we restored Galilee.*
*So their captivity became iron.*

The Quraysh answered him when the otter had passed on, and Qusay prevailed over the affairs of Mecca. He expelled Khuza'ah [10/f] from the Haram, for they had belittled its sanctity and wrought (1) destruction within it.

[Qusay Takes Over the Affairs of Mecca]

He was the first to command Mecca and to oversee the pilgrims. He took charge of al-Sadana, the watering, and all [12] the affairs of Mecca. He received the pilgrims, fed and watered them, and honored them with generosity and dignity. Some said: (2)

<!-- page 52 -->
August Pilgrims Eating Meat

*The most generous of them is Qusay — noble in bearing,*
*with pure milk and crumbled bread.*
*He grows weary not — his eyes always open.*

He fed the pilgrims bread and meat.

[The Reason for Qusay's War against Khuza'ah]

The reason for Qusay's war was that the key to the Ka'bah was in the hands of Abu Ghabshan al-Khuza'i, who had been the custodian of the House. Qusay met him in Ta'if and purchased the key from him for a skin of wine. He then went to his people and said: "This is the key of your father Isma'il, which Allah has returned to you without injustice or treachery." A woman came and blamed her brother, saying: "I had pledged it to him — I did not sell it." War broke out between them over this.

From this episode comes the proverb: "More regrettable than the deal of Abu Ghabshan." (1) A poet said about this:

*Darker than the Banu Fahr is Khuza'ah —*
*Abu Ghabshan is darker still than Qusay.*
*Blame your elder if he sold what he held —*
*do not press Qusay in his pursuit.*

Qusay became engaged to the daughter of Halil ibn Habashiyyah ibn Salul al-Khuza'i — a woman named Hubba, daughter of Halil. Halil had been the custodian of the Ka'bah and the commander of Mecca. When Halil recognized Qusay's stature

(1) See: Ansab al-Ashraf 58/1, Majma' al-Amthal 1/385, Sawa'ir al-Amthal 117–118, al-Mustaqsa 100/2, Thamarat al-Qulub 135.

<!-- page 53 -->
and gave him his daughter in marriage, she bore him: 'Abd al-Dar, 'Abd Manaf, 'Abd al-'Uzza, and 'Abd — the sons of Qusay.

[Mention of the Virtue of 'Abd Manaf]

'Abd Manaf was the finest of them, for Allah had singled him out to be the forefather of the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family.

"If a stone were found inscribed with the words: I am the jealousy of the son of Qusay — commanded to be pious — in the hadith (1): [13] may God bless you."

Indeed, he only commanded the fear of Allah — the Almighty — to be feared by those who truly feared Him and believed in Him. And this is among the proofs of what we have mentioned regarding the fathers of the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — their great station and their noble standing.

Ibn al-Zuba'ari said of 'Abd Manaf:

*A pure sign for 'Abd Manaf —*
*the Quraysh were white and she was split.*
*And those who say: Come to the guests —*
*the feathers and not the feathers.*
*They embraced you from Hawa and from Iqraf.*
*Your mother was violated if you went to their house.*

Al-Khuza'i said (3):

(1) See: Ansab al-Ashraf 60/1.
(2) See: Diwan 53.
(3) The verses in: Ansab al-Ashraf 1/68, Al-Sira al-Nabawiyyah 1/178, Amali al-Murtada 2/268. Attributed to 'Abdullah ibn al-Zuba'ari, see: The collected edition of his poetry, 54.

<!-- page 54 -->
*O nomad who has turned aside — journey now, you are in the family of 'Abd Manaf.*
*And those who departed for the Ilaf journey* (1) —
*those who took the covenant across its horizons,*
*the journey of desire and the journey of summers —*
*both journeys were enacted for him.*

Among them is what was said (2) of Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf, who was called 'Amr (3):

*And the men of Mecca are lean —*
*'Amr, who broke the Tharid for his people.*

[11/f] The Banu 'Abd Manaf are called "al-Tayyibun," because the presidency rested with them, and they sought to take the brigade and the veil from Banu 'Abd al-Dar. Banu 'Abd al-Dar formed a pact with other clans to prevent Banu 'Abd Manaf from taking what had been theirs.

Umm Hakim, daughter of 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim, took a handful of perfume and put her hand into the stone, then said: "Whoever has been kind to him is one of us." Thereupon Banu 'Abd Manaf, Banu Asad, Banu Zuhra, Banu Taym, and Banu al-Harith joined together; they were called "al-Mutayyabun."

When Banu Sahm saw this, they slaughtered a camel and plunged their hands into its blood, saying: "Whoever licks its blood is one of us." Those who did so were: Banu Sahm, Banu 'Adi, Banu 'Abd al-Dar, Banu Jumah, and Banu [14] Makhzum; they were called "al-Ahlaf." Then nothing further passed between them.

On this al-Fadl ibn 'Abbas said:

(1) An increase from "B."
(2) An increase from "B."
(3) See: Diwan 53 [first stanza], Biography of the Prophet 1/136.
(4) See: Diwan 16.


<!-- page 55 -->
And we called the good ones from the Quraysh —
What good had we ever failed to do?
On the vineyard of Falat, Banna, and Taba,
We never closed a door upon the people.

Concerning 'Abd al-Manaf, the poet says:
More honorable than 'Abd Manaf in lineage —
No mother bore a child of finer stock.

Qusay gave 'Abd Manaf the watering rights, the rifada, and the house of the council. Qusay took the council house and set its door facing the Ka'bah, then divided Makkah into four quarters among the Quraysh.

Qusay was called "the Gatherer," for — as the poet says — Allah gathered the tribes through him:

Your father Qusay was called the Gatherer —
Through him, God assembled the clans of Fahr.

Muhammad ibn Jubayr ibn Mut'im ibn 'Adi ibn Nawfal ibn 'Abd Manaf said: "Qusay ibn Kilab was the first to reign as a king among the sons of Ka'b ibn Lu'ay." With all his glory, that distinction passed to 'Abd Manaf ibn Qusay and became his. Allah bestows His bounty upon whom He wills, and He placed the seed of prophethood in Qusay's line and caused it to take root through his heirs.

**[Mentioning the Virtues of Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf]**

The honor of 'Abd Manaf then passed, by Allah's grace, to his son Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf.

(1) See: Al-'Aqd al-Farid 5/485; Al-Iqa'a by Sahib ibn 'Abbad, 43; Al-Qistas fi 'Ilm al-Awzan, 99; Al-Wafi by the Tabrizi scholar, 107. The verse is unattributed in the cited sources.
(2) Addition from manuscript "B."
(3) The verse is by Hudhayfah ibn Ghanem al-'Adawi, found in: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/71; Ansab al-Ashraf 1/58; Sirat Ibn Hisham 1/126; Tarikh al-Tabari 2/265.
(4) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/70.

<!-- page 56 -->
Hashim's given name was 'Amr, and he was called "Amr al-'Ali" on account of his high standing. He was named Hashim because he was the first to break bread, crumble it into broth, and feed it to the people — and so he was called Hashim, "the Breaker."

Allah honored him and gave him distinction through his closeness to the Prophet ﷺ and his family — the best of all Arabs and non-Arabs, the master of the sons of Adam.

**[Hadith: "Allah the Almighty created the creation in two parts"]**

It is reported that the Prophet ﷺ and the Imams of his household commanded Bilal ibn Hamamah to call the noon prayer one day before its time, during the month of Rajab, thirteen nights into it. Bilal did so, and the people stirred, saying, "Something has happened." They gathered in the mosque, and the Messenger of Allah ﷺ came walking toward the mosque until he reached Bab al-Shamiyyin — one of the mosque's gates — where he took hold of both sides of the gate. The mosque contained a place called al-Saddah. He then called out: "Do you hear, O people of al-Saddah?" They answered, "We hear and we obey, O Messenger of Allah." He said, "Do you wish to know?" So the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said:

"Allah the Almighty created the creation and divided it into two parts, and I was placed in the better of the two parts. This is what He said: ﴿وَأَصْحَابُ الْيَمِينِ مَا أَصْحَابُ الْيَمِينِ﴾ [Al-Waqi'ah: 27] — the Companions of the Right Hand, how excellent are the Companions of the Right Hand. I am of the people of the right, and I am the best among the people of the right hand.

(1) See: Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib [folios 7/Z–8/F].
(2) Addition found in Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib.
(3) Manuscripts "[" and "B" read: "And the rest are among the people of the north." What has been established here follows Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib.

<!-- page 57 -->
Then He divided those two parts into three, and I was placed among the best of them. This is what He said: ﴿فَأَصْحَابُ الْمَيْمَنَةِ مَا أَصْحَابُ الْمَيْمَنَةِ ۝ وَأَصْحَابُ الْمَشْأَمَةِ مَا أَصْحَابُ الْمَشْأَمَةِ ۝ وَالسَّابِقُونَ السَّابِقُونَ﴾ [Al-Waqi'ah: 8–10] — I am of the foremost, and I am the best of the foremost. Then He divided the third into tribes, and I was placed among the best of them. This is what He said: ﴿يَا أَيُّهَا النَّاسُ إِنَّا خَلَقْنَاكُم مِّن ذَكَرٍ وَأُنثَىٰ وَجَعَلْنَاكُمْ شُعُوبًا وَقَبَائِلَ لِتَعَارَفُوا﴾ [Al-Hujurat: 13] — My tribe is the best of all tribes. I hold them in awe before Allah the Almighty, and I am the master of the sons of Adam, and I say this without pride. Then He divided the tribes into clans, and He placed me in the best of them. This is what He said: ﴿إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ اللَّهُ لِيُذْهِبَ عَنكُمُ الرِّجْسَ أَهْلَ الْبَيْتِ وَيُطَهِّرَكُمْ تَطْهِيرًا﴾ [Al-Ahzab: 33].

Allah then singled out three from my household for distinction, and I am the master of all three, and I hold them in awe before Allah — without pride. He chose me above 'Ali, Hamza, and Ja'far. We were once resting in al-Abtah, and every one of us had drawn his garment over his face: 'Ali to my right, Ja'far to my left, and Hamza at my feet. What roused me from my sleep was the rustling of angels' wings and a coolness upon my chest from 'Ali's arm. I looked around, and I found Jibril standing before three angels. One of them asked: 'O Jibril, to which of these four have you been sent?' He nudged me with his foot and said, 'To this one.' The angel asked, 'And who is this?' He replied, 'This is Muhammad, the master of the messengers.' Then he gestured toward 'Ali and said, 'This is the master of the guardians.' He gestured toward Hamza and said, 'This is the master of the martyrs.' He gestured toward Ja'far and said, 'This one — in Paradise he has a door with two wings,

(1) Addition from Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib.
(2) See: Tafsir al-Furat al-Kufi, 340; Sharh al-Akhbar 1/120–121.

<!-- page 58 -->
two gilded wings with which he flies.' "

This is evidence of the virtue of the Prophet's fathers ﷺ — and in particular of those nearest to him among them. We have mentioned this hadith in our discussion of his grandfather Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf because it is through his lineage that the best of all households descends — and this is an undeniable virtue, established on sound authority.

We now return to the account of Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf.

Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf held the guardianship of his father's rights in the watering and the rifada, and he held the presidency in Makkah above all his father's other sons. On the first of Dhu al-Hijjah, he would address the Quraysh with his back to the door of the Ka'bah, commanding them to do good and exhorting them to noble character. In his sermon he would say:

"O gathering of Quraysh, you are the masters of the Arabs, the finest of them in face and the greatest of them in forbearance. You are the heartland of the Arabs in lineage and the closest to them in bonds of kinship. O gathering of Quraysh, you are the neighbors of the House of Allah the Almighty — may Allah honor you with its guardianship and single you out from among the sons of Ishmael for nearness to it, and may He preserve in you the best that any neighbor has preserved. Honor the guests of Allah and the visitors of His House, for they come to you travel-worn and dusty from every land. As for my wealth, if all of it were sufficient for you I would give it freely — but I am a steward of my wealth, lawfully directing it, so long as no kinship tie is severed, nothing is taken unjustly, and nothing forbidden is mixed into it."

(1) Addition from manuscript "B."
(2) See: Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib [folio 8/F].

<!-- page 59 -->
"And whoever among you wishes to give from his own wealth what he is able to give — I ask you by the sanctity of this House that none of you hold back from providing for the dignity and sustenance of the House's visitors, so long as what is given has not been taken unjustly and no kinship tie has been severed in acquiring it."

The Banu Ka'b ibn Lu'ay responded earnestly to this call and spent freely of their wealth. Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf would collect these contributions and store them in the Dar al-Nadwa until the pilgrims arrived, and then he would feed them. He would bring large waterskins from the vicinity of Zamzam, fill them with water, and set them out for the pilgrims to drink. He fed them in Makkah before the day of watering, and on the day of watering he fed them in Mina, at 'Arafah, and at the gathering-place — bread was distributed to them — along with meat, clarified butter, sawiq, and dates. He watered them with milk and crumbled bread for them; it is for this that he was named Hashim. He was also called "the Moon" in honor of his noble bearing and beauty.

In this regard, Matrud al-Khuza'i says:

The men of Makkah were gaunt and lean
Until 'Amr broke bread for his people.

He was the first to make the roads of Makkah safe. The Quraysh were a mercantile people, yet their trade had not extended beyond Makkah — until Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf rode to the Levant, where he came before Caesar. Each day he slaughtered a sheep, prepared food, and invited those around him to eat, for he was among the finest of people

(1) Addition from Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib.
(2) Addition from manuscript "B."
(3) Addition from Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib.
(4) See: The poetry of 'Abdullah ibn al-Zuba'ari, 53 (noting the disagreement among sources as to whether the verse is attributed to Matrud ibn Ka'b al-Khuza'i or to 'Abdullah ibn al-Zuba'ari).

<!-- page 60 -->
in character and appearance. Word of his generosity and fine bearing reached Caesar, who summoned him, honored him with special distinction, and showed him great favor.

Hashim said to him: "O king, my people are the merchants of the Arabs. If you see fit to write them a letter of safe passage, they will bring you the finest goods from across the Hijaz." Caesar agreed and wrote him a letter guaranteeing their safety. Hashim then took that letter, and whenever he passed through the territories of Arab tribes on the road to the Levant, he would halt them on the grounds that the Quraysh were carrying goods for them; he would arrange for those goods to be delivered, and he would return the tribes' capital along with their profit.

Hashim thus secured the compact of Ilaf for all those between him and the Levant. He returned to Makkah bringing with him more blessings than his people had ever known, and they set out with a great caravan. Hashim led them, and they continued to benefit from the arrangements of Ilaf until they reached the Levant. He died on that journey, in Gaza, in the land of the Levant.

In this regard, Matrud al-Khuza'i said:

A grave in Gaza holds Hashim — he does not pass away;
The generous one died in Damascus on the day of his mourning.
His absence is a loss to all who dwell in his yard;
Hold firm to his memory, with tongue and with hand.

**[Mention of the Virtue of 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim]**

'Abd al-Muttalib inherited from his father Hashim his bounty, generosity, and noble qualities. 'Abd al-Muttalib was the grandfather of the Prophet ﷺ. His given name was "Shaybah al-Hamd" — he was named Shaybah because he was born with a streak of white hair on his head. He came to be called 'Abd al-Muttalib because his mother Salma bint 'Amr ibn Zayd ibn Labid ibn Khaddash ibn

(1) The two verses are found in: Ansab al-Ashraf 1/73; Al-Muhabbar, 163; 'Uyun al-Akhbar by Ibn Qutayba 1/33; Al-Munammaq, 44; Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib, folio [9/F].

<!-- page 61 -->
'Amir ibn Ghannam ibn 'Adi ibn al-Najjar — and al-Najjar's name was Taym Allah ibn Tha'labah ibn 'Amr ibn al-Khazraj. Hashim married her and brought her to Makkah, but her father made it a condition of the marriage that if she became pregnant, she should return to give birth in her own people's home. She conceived 'Abd al-Muttalib in Makkah, and when her pregnancy became heavy, Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf took her to Yathrib on the very journey during which he was heading to the Levant — and he died there.

Salma gave birth to 'Abd al-Muttalib in Yathrib, and she named him "Shaybah" for the reason already mentioned. As he grew, he became a youth of perfect bearing, beauty, virtue, and goodness. A man from Banu al-Harith ibn 'Abd Manaf happened to pass by him while he was wrestling with two other boys. He saw that the boy was the most handsome of them in appearance and the most formidable in contest, and whenever 'Abd al-Muttalib threw someone and won, he would call out: "I am the son of Hashim! I am the son of the master of al-Batha'!" The man was struck with wonder at what he witnessed. When he returned to Makkah, he told al-Muttalib ibn 'Abd Manaf the boy's story. Al-Muttalib went to Yathrib and brought the boy back with him. When the people saw them arrive, they asked, "Where did you come from?" — and the boy said: "From Yathrib." They then asked, "Who is this with you?" Al-Muttalib replied, "A slave I purchased." He then brought him home, dressed him in a new garment, and presented him to the Banu 'Abd Manaf. They asked, "Who is this?" He said, "He is the son of your nephew Hashim." He told them the boy's story and described his generosity, and they said: "This one whom we called your slave just yesterday!"

And so he was given the name 'Abd al-Muttalib. Al-Muttalib later died in the land of Yemen,

(1) Addition from manuscript "B."
(2) Addition from manuscript "B."
(3) Addition from Al-Fada'il wa-l-Manaqib, folio [10/F].

<!-- page 62 -->
in a place said to be Radman, in the land of Yemen near Hajar.

**[The Elegy of Matrud al-Khuza'i for 'Abd al-Muttalib]**

Al-Khuza'i composed these verses mourning him and his brothers, the Banu 'Abd Manaf:

I wait at the head of the road for those who come and go —
O eye, weep with flowing tears and abundant grief!
Weep, my beloved, and do not hold back your blame;
O eye, shed tears and celebrate his memory.
Mourn the great al-Dasiyah, the mother of the noble ones;
Weep for every generous brother who was a pillar of trust.
The skin of nobility is far from the base;
Lofty in resolve, his ambitions fashioned from cream.
He never shrank from what was difficult, nor turned away —
Drawn by the love of the noble and the fragrance of high-minded women.
A mediator, pure, if they call his lineage;
He dug deep after the floods with generous hands.
The tip of generosity and its excess were his demand —
How my soul yearns for him among the dead!
Yesterday in Radman he was an exile; today he is here no more.

**[The Praise of Hudhayfah ibn Ghanem for Banu 'Abd al-Muttalib]**

'Abd al-Muttalib was raised on the virtues of noble character, generosity, and goodness. He was given the title "Shaybah al-Hamd."

In this regard, Hudhayfah ibn Ghanem ibn 'Awf ibn 'Ubayd ibn 'Uwayj ibn

(1) See: Mu'jam al-Buldan 2/773–774.
(2) See: Mu'jam al-Buldan 2/212.
(3) See: Sirat Ibn Hisham 1/139–140.
(4) Addition from manuscript "B."


<!-- page 63 -->
'Uday ibn Ka'b praises Bani 'Abd al-Muttalib:

The darkness of the night shines bright as the full moon,
upon the elder of praise whose face was radiant.
I speak of the best among the ancients and their offspring,
the pilgrims ever generous — and noble Hashim.

The descendants of kings neither deceive nor dissemble,
and 'Abd Manaf, the full moon of the Fihri tribe.
He will not fail them in his youth;
you find him walking his father's path.

They filled al-Batha' with glory and honour,
while we remained mere goats of Bani Bakr.
They abandoned foolishness and baseness,
and they pardon the offence for which another seeks revenge.

Give them thanks until you vanish into the grave —
go, for among your own people you need not linger.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn 'Ali ibn al-Husayn narrated from his forebears (peace be upon them): "The reason Hudhayfah composed these verses was that two lepers fled their colony and kidnapped a man from the households of Mecca. They seized Hudhayfah, bound him, and set out with him. 'Abd al-Muttalib then encountered them as he was returning from Ta'if. He had lost his sight and his son Abu Lahab was leading him. When Hudhayfah saw 'Abd al-Muttalib, he cried out. So 'Abd al-Muttalib ordered Abu Lahab to ransom him with twenty ounces of gold, ten camels, and a mare."

(1) See: Ansab al-Ashraf 355/3.
(2) See: The virtues and defects [folio 10/z].
(3) Increasing virtues and defects.
(4) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 64 -->
[The Account of Abraha al-Ashram]

Among the traditions that speak to 'Abd al-Muttalib's virtue, honour, and unshakeable trust in his Lord is the following account — recorded by the biographers — concerning the Abyssinian campaign against Yemen. Abraha al-Ashram, the Abyssinian viceroy of Yemen, marched with the intent of destroying the Sacred House of God, bringing with him an elephant named Yaksom.

A Yemeni nobleman named Dhu Nafr rose against him, calling his people and whatever Arabs answered his appeal to wage jihad in defence of the Sacred House. He was one of Yemen's great lords and kings. He engaged Abraha in battle, but Abraha defeated him and his companions, took Dhu Nafr captive, and marched on with him in his train.

Abraha then confronted Nafil ibn Habib al-Khath'ami at the pass of Khath'am, along with two tribes. He routed them and captured Nafil. When Abraha intended to kill him, Nafil said: "Do not kill me — spare me and I will guide you wherever you wish." So Abraha kept him as a guide and marched on.

As Abraha's armies drew near the Haram and approached Mecca, the Quraysh realised they had no means to resist him by force. They withdrew, while 'Abd al-Muttalib stood firm and declared: "By Allah, I shall not abandon the Haram. I seek no glory elsewhere." He remained in the city while the Quraysh dispersed.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 46/1.
(2) An increase from "B".
(3) An addition of the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 65 -->
'Abd al-Muttalib said:

No, let them not think the Ra Yum concerns us —
we shall settle it, so guard what is lawful to you.
Though their prayers beat down and their encampments become your sanctuaries,
do not let them overpower your Qurhan.

The word rendered "impossible" here is al-Qurrah, as in the Almighty's saying: ⟪ar:«مُسْتَقَرٌّ»⟫ [al-Ra'd: 13].

The Quraysh then departed, and 'Abd al-Muttalib rose greatly in their esteem when they witnessed how firmly he forbade himself to Allah and placed his complete trust in Him — a mark of his sound and penetrating judgment in divine affairs.

When the army of the Elephant drew near the outskirts of Mecca, they struck 'Abd al-Muttalib's camels and drove them off. Word of this reached Abraha, along with news of 'Abd al-Muttalib's standing and his position among his people. Abraha sent for him, and when he came, Abraha marvelled at his bearing, his dignity, and his commanding presence. He honoured him, seated him beside himself, and asked whether he had a need to present. 'Abd al-Muttalib said: "Your army has seized my camels — I ask that you return them to me." Abraha was astonished and said: "You have diminished yourself in my eyes and lowered your standing with me. You come to ask about your camels, yet you say nothing of this House that I have come to demolish — does it mean nothing to you?" 'Abd al-Muttalib replied: "Those camels are mine, and I am their owner. As for the House, it has a Lord who will protect it from you if He wills."

(1) See: Al-Sira al-Nabawiyyah 51/1, Ansab al-Ashraf 79/1, Tarikh Makkah 145/1, 42/2, Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/92, Al-Ansab 1/219, Al-Munmaqi 76.
(2) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 66 -->
This reply made 'Abd al-Muttalib only greater in Abraha's eyes, and his words carried enormous weight with him. Abraha ordered the camels returned.

Then Allah vindicated the words of 'Abd al-Muttalib: ﴿ وَأَرْسَلَ عَلَيْهِمْ طَيْرًا أَبَابِيلَ ﴾ ﴿ تَرْمِيهِم بِحِجَارَةٍ مِّن سِجِّيلٍ ﴾ [al-Fil: 3–4].

It was said that these birds resembled swallows or martins; each bird carried three stones — one in its beak and one in each claw — roughly the size of chickpeas or lentils. Any man struck by one of them perished, though not all were struck. The army broke and fled, scattering along the road back to Yemen.

When Nafil witnessed the divine wrath descending upon them, he cried out:

Where is the refuge for one whom God pursues? The victorious has no escape.

Nafil also said:

We greet you with the morning — eyes that saw
what your wing-tips spared us.
I have with me a hedgehog we never saw before;
grieve not for what has befallen us.
Stones were cast upon us
as though the Abyssinians had become our creed —
did you love us? We gave no answer.
We shall dine with you.
Rudeina, if you see or find abundance,
then excuse me and praise my conduct.
I thanked God when I saw the bird,
while all the people ask about the gheeel.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 53/1.
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 53/1.
(3) See: Biography of the Prophet, 53/1.
(4) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 67 -->
The army fled, falling in every direction, perishing along every road. Abraha himself was struck in his body; they carried him away as his limbs fell from him piece by piece, each inch dropping away and leaving behind a trail of pus, until they finally brought him into San'a' in a state like a fledgling chick. He did not die until his chest split open.

'Abd al-Muttalib said concerning these events:

His heart, as they claim, was in what it harboured.

His building demolishes the lyric —
his fathers rushed toward the House of God,
guided through the age of sin.
You are firm, yet your wealth is not firm;
you change grey hair after youth —
so what do you wear as your alleged garment?
Let your remembrance of the matter of union be forbidden;
you are the one I dream of recalling —
an army that came to you in al-Ashram,
as though they were a people laden with spoils.
The next day they were left like al-Battah,
overwhelmed by what afflicted them.
An elephant they led to the lowest ground —
its Creator honours His close friend.
Where is he who stood in his era?
Their eyes were not shielded from the elephant.
God repented of its destruction —
as though its fountains overflowed with remorse.
The Ababeel birds flew to pelt them;
the stones lodged fast in Hama.
He honoured the people of the sanctuary as the books record,
and despite Abraha's arrogance he was compelled.
With victory from God, the Lord of all servants —
a promise rhyming true with right —
the eagles became the scavengers of their remains,
circling as mourners do.
A land whose Zamzam was dug,
and may God bequeath us the finest of lands.

(1) See: The virtues and defects [folio 10/z–11/f].

<!-- page 68 -->
'Abd al-Muttalib also has these verses on the same subject:

We are the family of God in His town, as it has ever been since the age of Abraham.
Whoever seeks to corrupt it will be silenced.
The House has a God who defends it;
even in the ancient days, it was cast into strife — and was preserved.
I have watched the wicked dragged to their ruin.

There was indeed a clear sign and a lasting lesson in the destruction of the Companions of the Elephant, through which Allah protected His Sacred House and forbade its violation. He manifested the virtue of its guardian and vindicated His prophet 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim — confirming his promise to him and answering his words: "The House has a Lord who protects it." And He answered his supplication.

That same year — the Year of the Elephant — saw the birth of al-Mustafa Muhammad, master of all humanity and seal of the prophets, may God bless him and grant him and his family peace. This was among the blessings heralding his birth, and his religion — peace and blessings be upon him — was thereby blessed.

[The Account of the Excavation of the Zamzam Spring]

Then 'Abd al-Muttalib received a vision in his sleep in which he was commanded: "Dig Zamzam — the hidden place of the Grand Sheikh." He awoke and said, "O Allah, make it plain to me." He returned to sleep, and the vision came again, saying: "Dig the troubled woman — none shall be able to withhold her from the people but you." Again he said, "O Allah, make it plain to me," and returned to sleep. The vision came a third time, saying: "Dig Zamzam, between al-Farth and the blood, where the crow alights, hottest above the village of the ants, toward the red monuments."

(1) See: Al-'Aqd al-Farid 313/3, Akhbar Makkah 96, Al-Munmaqi fi Akhbar Quraysh 27, Al-Hur al-'Ayn 173.

<!-- page 69 -->
He rose and went to the Sacred Mosque, where he sat and waited for the sign he had been shown.

A group of people then slaughtered a cow, and it broke free from its tether and bolted into the Sacred Mosque, falling in the very spot of Zamzam. The tether was recognised, and its owner made for that place, slaughtered the cow there, and skinned it. A crow then came and settled until it landed in the manure, where 'Abd al-Muttalib found an anthill. He rose and began digging there for Zamzam.

The Quraysh came to him and said: "O 'Abd al-Muttalib, what do you think you are doing — digging in our mosque without our knowledge?" He replied: "I have been commanded to do this, and He who commanded me will repel anyone who tries to stop me." They left off when they understood that a divine charge had been laid upon him, and they were safe from His ignorance of their affairs. He continued digging until he uncovered swords and shields that had long been buried in Zamzam. When the Quraysh saw this, they said: "Give us a share of what you have found." He replied: "It belongs to the House of God." He dug on until the water welled up; he then contained it so that it would not overflow, and built a basin over it.

Zamzam is the well of Ismail ibn Ibrahim, peace be upon him. It is narrated that during a year of drought, Ismail was suffering from thirst with his mother Hajar; she sought refuge in God and called upon Him, and Allah caused the spring of Zamzam to well up before them. When it later fell into disuse, it was buried, and what had been placed in it was buried with it — until the excavation of 'Abd al-Muttalib.

Concerning 'Abd al-Muttalib's excavation of Zamzam, Khuwaylid ibn Asad ibn 'Abd al-'Uzza says:

I say — and what shall I say of him because of this? —
to you, O Ibn Salma: you are the digger of Zamzam.
The excavation of Ibrahim on the day of Hajar's son,
and the running of Jibril in the age of Adam.

(1) Increasing the virtues and defects.
(2) Al-Baytan in: Ansab al-Ashraf 96/1, Akhbar Makkah 22/2, al-Ikti'a 1/161.

<!-- page 70 -->
It is said that 'Abd al-Muttalib found a golden gazelle among the buried items and greatly admired it. He cast lots over it with arrows — some for the House and some for himself — and the arrow of the House came out, so he adorned the House with it. It was the first ornament with which the House was ever adorned.

It is also narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's blessings be upon him):

When the Quraysh saw what 'Abd al-Muttalib had brought forth from Zamzam, they gathered around him and said: "O 'Abd al-Muttalib, this is the well of our father Ishmael, and we have a right to it — let us share in it and in whatever you find." He said: "I will not do that, for this is a matter that was entrusted to me alone and not to you, and He granted it to me from among you." They refused to accept this unless he shared it with them, saying: "Our judge in this shall be such-and-such." He agreed to take the matter before the soothsayer of Bani Sa'd ibn Hudhaym. He set out with them, and each tribe of the Quraysh sent one man. The land at that time was waterless; as they made their way through one of those parched stretches between the Hijaz and the Levant, the water ran out for 'Abd al-Muttalib and his companions. He asked the other tribes of Quraysh for water, but they refused, saying: "We are ourselves in want, and we fear for ourselves what has befallen you." Seeing no other course, they dismounted. Each man dug a pit and sat in it, agreeing: "Whoever among us dies first shall be buried by those who remain." Then 'Abd al-Muttalib said: "By Allah, it is weakness and surrender to simply sit here and wait for death without striking the earth in search of water." He rose, mounted his camel, and when it got up from beneath him, water burst forth from under its resting place — a sweet spring. 'Abd al-Muttalib dismounted, gave thanks and drank deeply.

(1) See: The virtues and defects [folio 12/z–].
(2) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 71 -->
He and his companions drank and filled their water-skins. Then he called to those tribes of Quraysh who were with him and said: "Come to the water — Allah has provided us with water." They came and drank, and then they said: "By Allah, Allah has already given His judgment for you against us, O 'Abd al-Muttalib. We shall never dispute with you again over Zamzam. Whoever gave you water from this camel in the wilderness is your waterman of Zamzam — go back and tend your watering as its rightful master." So he returned, and they returned, and he was set apart from them in this matter.

This is among the virtues that one mentions not to boast, but because it speaks to the truth of his visions — for what he saw concerning Zamzam was a clear sign of Allah's favour and of what Allah had singled him out for. As the Prophet — may God bless him and his family — said: "A true vision is one of twenty-six parts of prophecy."

And what he was then shown — the appearance of the water in Zamzam, the well of Ishmael, peace be upon him — was a blessing for him: Allah provided him with water, caused it to spring from the earth for him, and made it a miracle witnessed by all his people, one that none among them could deny or diminish.

'Abd al-Muttalib was the finest of the Quraysh, their chief, and their master. In him the light of prophecy was visible, along with the majesty of kingship, the awe of virtue, and his qualities are beyond enumeration. He was called "Sayyid al-Wadi" — the Master of the Valley — meaning Mecca and its surroundings.

(1) An increase in virtues and defects [folio 12/z].
(2) An increase in virtues and defects [folio 12/z].
(3) An increase from "B".
(4) See: Whoever is not attended by the jurist 585/2, Amali al-Saduq 121: "The true vision is part of the seventy parts of prophecy."


<!-- page 72 -->
'Abd al-Muttalib had ten children, the best of whom were 'Abd Allah and Abu Talib. Abu Talib, the eldest of them by birth, was raised on generosity and noble conduct, and it was clear that these two were superior to their brothers.

[Abdul Muttalib's tenth son vows to sacrifice]

When 'Abd Allah grew up and was still young, his father 'Abd al-Muttalib held him very dear. When he had nine children, he had vowed that if a tenth son were born to him, he would sacrifice him — and many narrations have been transmitted on this matter.

Al-Qaadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad (may Allah be pleased with him) said:

The most authentic report, transmitted from the Ahl al-Bayt (peace be upon them), is this: ['Abd al-Muttalib] held back from the slaughter because of what Allah had cast into his heart — a reluctance that was felt also by those who saw the boy. He took 'Abd Allah by the hand and set out to slaughter him, whereupon the Quraysh rose to stop him, imploring him to release the boy. They said, "If you slaughter him, men among us will follow your example with their own sons." He said, "What am I to do? I have vowed to do this." They said, "Stand by your vow, yet find another way." His brothers from Banu Makhzum rose in this matter as well, and al-Ghayrah ibn 'Abd Allah ibn 'Umar ibn Makhzum said:

And love — who killed 'Abd al-Muttalib
And slaughtered him in rags like a statue of gold.

(1) See: The Virtues and Defects [folio 20/f].
(2) An increase in virtues and defects.
(3) See: Biography of Ibn Ishaq 12–13.

<!-- page 73 -->
Mention of some of the virtues of the fathers of the Prophet:

O elder, do not rush us in wonder —
for what is our son among the loving people?
Our son stands against the humiliated usurper.
So he ransomed him with wealth, that the wealth might be spent.
I will give my wealth, and let it be taken —
Ossouf threw without him the bitterness of harm.

Abu Talib defended him, as did his brother al-Zubayr ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib. Abu Talib said in this regard:

No, by the Lord of the House with its fallen —
may God not abandon those who pass through it.
Every kinsman of the House, every pilgrim
who visits the House of God beneath the veil —
'Abd Allah was not to be slaughtered with ease,
among the youth of high lineage,
among the women of noble descent.
I am drawn between the enmity of rivals
and the people of ancestry,
the people of standing and the domed sanctuaries.
You are not like that in the eyes of the Lords.

(1) See: Al-Durrat al-Ghara', 73–74.

<!-- page 74 -->
In long hair.

'Abd al-Muttalib said:

O God, my God, I have made a vow,
and I fear God if I disobey His command.
This is my son — I meant to slaughter him.
If He delays him and accepts his excuse,
I bind myself to thank the Almighty God.

[Mention of the fidya of 'Abd Allah]

'Abd al-Muttalib then brought out ten camels and said: Cast the lots and slaughter these in his place, then release him. He said, "I do not know whether Allah is pleased with me or whether my vow is fulfilled — I will keep casting until He is." He cast the lots, and the arrow fell to 'Abd Allah; he added ten more camels and cast again, and the arrow fell to 'Abd Allah again. He continued adding ten at a time, and the arrow kept falling to 'Abd Allah, until the total reached a hundred — at which point the arrow fell to the camels. He said, "No, by Allah, I will not accept this once — the arrow must fall on the camels three times." He cast again, and the arrow fell to the camels. He cast a second time, and again it fell to the camels. He cast a third time, and once more it fell to the camels. He said, "Now I know that my Lord is pleased." He slaughtered them and said to the people, "Take." And they rushed to take the meat.

(1) See: Biography of Ibn Ishaq 13.
(2) An increase from "B".
(3) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 75 -->
Mention of some of the virtues of the fathers of the Prophet:

On this matter, 'Unays ibn Khalif al-Fahmi said:

He also distributed among them the blood-price of Ibn Hashim, in the place where they had been struck.

This act of 'Abd al-Muttalib — his intention to sacrifice 'Abd Allah — was an inspiration from Allah, following the example of Ibrahim (peace be upon him), who said: "O my son, I see in a dream that I am slaughtering you, so tell me what you think." He said: "Father, do what you are commanded; you will find me, God willing, among the patient." (Al-Saffat: 102) 'Abd Allah was thus ransomed with a hundred camels, just as Isma'il ibn Ibrahim was ransomed with the great sacrifice in the way of God and in His honor — for God followed that act of grace toward Isma'il, and Allah bestows His bounty on whomever He wills.

Among the greatest of those bounties is what Allah granted me by extending my life until I brought forth Muhammad — may Allah bless him and his family — and honored him through his birth. His forebears were not among those who worshipped idols or rejected prophecy; they held fast to the law of Ibrahim (peace be upon him), with all its virtues, as their transmitted accounts and what the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family have testified of their generosity and purity make clear.

'Abd Allah ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib was raised on the finest moral character, purity, generosity, prosperity, and honor. He was beautiful and handsome, and between his eyes shone a radiant light — the clear sign of the imminent birth of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him).

(1) Al-Bayt in: Al-Manaqib wa al-Maqalib [folio 14/z], Tarikh al-Ya'qubi 292/1.
(2) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 76 -->
[News of a priestess who sought Fatima bint Murr]

When 'Abd Allah married Amina bint Wahb, the mother of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), a priestess from Khatam looked upon him — she was known as Fatima bint Murr, one of the people of Tabala. She had studied the scriptures, and the light of prophecy was unmistakably visible in his face. When she saw it, she longed to conceive a son by him, so she called him to herself, but he refused. She offered him a hundred camels, and still he refused, saying:

As for the forbidden — death stands before it,
and the lawful has not come that you might soften it.
The generous man guards his honor and his religion —
so how could I do what you are asking?

She then asked him to marry her. When he later went to his wife Amina bint Wahb, he intended to marry Fatima in the proper manner according to the religion of Ibrahim (peace be upon him), but she refused and said: "I saw a light in your face, and I know of a prophet who is to be sent near the time of his birth. I had hoped that I might be the one, but now I see that light

(1) "[" and "b": "once".
(2) An increase from "B".
(3) See: Dictionary of Countries, 816/1–817.
(4) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/96, Ansab al-Ashraf 1/97, Al-Manaqib wa al-Naqalib [leaf 17/z], Tarikh al-Tabari 244/2, Al-Jinan 173, Tayseer al-Mutalib 71.
(5) "B": "By order of the Atta".

<!-- page 77 -->
has departed from you." He told her what had passed with his family, and she said:

I saw a dazzling vision, glittering like the shimmer of lightning —
in his face a light shines,
and around him it spreads like the light of the full moon.
So I asked to be given him.

We will not show every man alive
a stallion-light within her, unknown to her.
I swear by God, no vessel was withheld —

She said of Banu Hashim:

Banu Hashim have left your brother
as a lamp is left when it goes out.
Or all that the boy contains —
Amina, as the father is bereaved —
wicks that proved full of fat,
devoted to purpose, and what he lost for my sake.
Seven grandparents shall be recompensed.

It is more beautiful if you seek something,
or a hand glad with generous fingers —
a whale of pride from which there is no surpassing.
And when Amina bore him, she did not bear
anything less than a hand that overwhelms,
and the protection of Allah — the Almighty — for His servant, as the protection of Yusuf ibn Ya'qub (peace be upon him).

He entered his family and was moved by them, and Allah preserved that light, founded as it was on purity. She conceived and bore the Messenger of Allah — Amina bint Wahb ibn 'Abd Manaf ibn Zahra ibn Kilab ibn Murrah ibn Ka'b ibn Lu'ayy ibn Ghalib ibn Fihr ibn Malik ibn al-Nadr ibn Kinana.

(1) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 97/1, Al-Manaqib wa al-Muqalab [folio 17/z], Tarikh al-Tabari 2/245, Al-Jinan 173, Tayseer al-Muttalib 71.
(2) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 97/1, Al-Manaqib wa al-Muqalab [folio 17/z], Tarikh al-Tabari 2/245, Al-Jinan 173–174.

<!-- page 78 -->
These are virtues that none deny or dismiss except those who reject the prophets and turn away from them — those who know their inner reality through the prophets' guardians and the disciples whom Allah has chosen from among them.

[The death of 'Abd Allah ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib and the laments for him]

'Abd Allah ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim died after the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had been born — two months later, according to one report; another report states that he died while his mother, Amina bint Wahb, was still pregnant with him. She said:

Toda weeps in a corner, in profound grief —
Ibn Hashim was a master among his people.
Mourned aloud upon the back of the plain —
when the clouds descend and do not fall,
when in the open land and when the horizon gathers,
and the frozen palanquin melts.
He raised trouble against those who harmed his journey —
he was a man of great virtue.

She also said:

A mountain that exposed the wicked in a corner.

She also said:

Forgive the side of al-Batha' the son of Ibn Ghalib,
beside a place recorded among the clouds.
I did not leave among the people anyone like Ibn Hashim.
Al-Manaya called him with a call, and he answered.

On the eve they came to carry him on his bier —
his companions bore him with tenderness.
If you are a fool of death and its uncertainty —

(1) See: The Virtues and Defects, leaf [17/z].
(2) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/100, Ansab al-Ashraf 1/105, Al-Manaqib wa al-Maqalib [leaf 17/z].

<!-- page 79 -->
Mention of some of the virtues of the fathers of the Prophet:

In a hollow among the stones of the pit,
I saw the end for one who uproots poverty —
Ibn Hashim was, in their verses,
from the generous lineage bearing news.
The course of his aims in every endeavor —
I sang the verses in the branches with the trees.
He gives abundantly of the original in Mudar,
and the mind, when the mind is measured by its worth —
forbearance and wisdom were of his nature.
The axes of steadfastness at all times lead to destiny.
If he had been in a dark pit,
Farb Shehana would have dug and brought forth —
the opening of ports is a return to travel
for the mind when the mind is in hardship.
The steel of resolution has been fed, purely, in difficulty,
and all the embers are marble.

A rain for the anxious — corn and oil from the rain —
water the sides of a grave in which you dwell.
Do not dictate to the noble for us a support —
O sorrowful eye, let tears fall forever.
There is pain and grief within my heart;
I complain to those around me of my deep affection for him —
he was the dwelling-place of the soul, an object of envy.

Had he lived, all of it would have been known —
Ibn Hashim would have been a guiding light in the darkness.
If you forget him, you have not forgotten —
Al-Arika plain, Qarma, this land —
I gave it to her without pretense or hesitation.

His brother Abu Talib said:

(1) See: The Virtues and Defects, leaf [17/z–18/f].
(2) See: His Diwan, 98.

<!-- page 80 -->
[The vision of Amina]

It was said that Amina, the mother of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), when she was pregnant with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), saw in her sleep someone coming to her who said: "You are pregnant with the master of this nation. When you give birth to him, say:

I give him refuge by the One,
from the evil of every envious —
Wasihi, Muhammad."

She saw that a light had come forth from her, illuminating the palaces in his sight. She told his father 'Abd Allah of this, and he in turn told his father 'Abd al-Muttalib, who awaited the birth eagerly. When she gave birth, she said what she had been told to say. 'Abd al-Muttalib came, received him with joy, carried him to the Ka'ba, and prayed for him, saying:

Praise be to God who gave me
this boy — the remedy of the distressed.
He has prevailed in the cradle over other boys;
bring him back to the house of the corners,
until I see him fully grown.
I seek refuge from all evil —

(1) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 98/1, Biography of the Prophet 1/158.
(2) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 103/1, Al-Manaqib wa al-Miqalib [folio 18/z], Muruj al-Dhahab 3/13.

<!-- page 81 -->
Amina's pregnancy and the birth of the Prophet:

Then he went out to her mother and returned the child (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) to her.

[Mention of the birth of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him)]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was born on a Monday, ten days into the month of Rabi' al-Awwal, in the Year of the Elephant. What Allah thereby granted the Quraysh was His protection — His blessing shielded them from any enemy who might have appeared while the Prophet was still a child or even before his birth.

A woman from Banu Sa'd ibn Abi Bakr nursed him — she was known as Halima bint Abi Dhu'ayb. The name of her husband, in whose care the Prophet was nursed, was al-Harith ibn 'Abd al-'Uzza ibn Rifa'a, also from Banu Sa'd ibn Bakr ibn Hawazin. They continued to see in him the marks of virtue and the signs of prophecy; they were blessed through him, and they witnessed wonders attesting to his great distinction.

[The Prophet's description of himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace]

In a hadith, some of the companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to him: "O Messenger of Allah, tell us about yourself." He said: "I am the answer to the prayer of my father Ibrahim, and I am the good news brought by my brother Jesus. My mother saw, when she conceived me, that a light came out of her and illuminated the palaces of Syria. I was nursed among Banu Sa'd ibn Bakr, and while I was with a brother of mine tending our flock, two men came to me wearing white garments, carrying a vessel filled with snow. They split open my belly, extracted my heart and split it, then removed

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 1/166–167.


<!-- page 82 -->
From it they extracted a black leech, washed my belly, and sealed it with that ice until I was satisfied. One of them said: Weigh him against ten of his ummah. He outweighed them all ten. Then he said: Weigh him against a hundred of his ummah. He outweighed them. Then he said: Weigh him against a thousand of his ummah. He outweighed them too. Then he said: Leave him, for even if you weighed him against his entire ummah, he would outweigh them all.

[His pride in his Quran, and his nursing among Bani Sa'd]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) used to say to his companions: "I am the most eloquent of you — I am of Quraysh, and I was nursed among Bani Sa'd ibn Bakr."

The people of the Sira said: What prompted his foster-mother al-Sa'diyya to return him to his mother (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) was that, when she came back with him after the weaning, people saw him with her and gathered around to look at him. They asked her about him, examined him closely, and said to her: "Let us take this boy to our king and our land — this is a child of great importance." It was said that she only escaped them with difficulty, and she brought him to Mecca, returning him to his mother and to his grandfather 'Abd al-Muttalib.

[The Death of Amina bint Wahb]

His mother, Amina bint Wahb, died when he was four years old. It was also said that she died when he was

<!-- page 83 -->
six years old.

[34]

['Abd al-Muttalib's honour for him — may Allah bless him and grant him peace — in his childhood]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) lived in the care of his grandfather 'Abd al-Muttalib. 'Abd al-Muttalib used to have a mattress laid for him in the shade of the Ka'ba, and his sons would sit around it, waiting for him to come out — none of them would sit upon it, out of respect for him. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) would come as a young boy and sit down upon it, and his uncles would try to move him away. But whenever 'Abd al-Muttalib saw this, he would say: "Leave my son — Allah has a great matter destined for him." Then he would seat him beside him on the mattress, pat him on the back, and look upon him with great affection.

When 'Abd al-Muttalib died, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was in his lap. He had already entrusted his care to his son Abu Talib ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib, and he composed these verses about him:

I entrust you, O slave of Manaf, after I am gone —
I shall be beneath the earth, a man departed.
He is different, and I shall light the cradle —
so be to him as a mother in her tenderness,
lowering him from her very entrails and liver,
even when the span of care is long to its end.

<!-- page 84 -->

I commend to you the care of his family, that they may flourish —
what I have relinquished, now beneath the grave.
Let no hatred come upon you, for it is not by design.
He said to me, and the saying is trustworthy:
My nephew — I have not lived among Ma'ad
save as the most devoted of my sons in affection.
Muhammad — please the most steadfast,
and everything in affairs —
the most knowledgeable of the people of the covenant:

[35]

that this boy is the master of the people of Najd.

[What was said in lamentation of 'Abd al-Muttalib]
