It is narrated that when death drew near to 'Abd al-Muttalib, he gathered his daughters together and said: "Let each one of you recite to me her lament before I die." Each of them composed verses, and this is recorded in the Sira.

When 'Abd al-Muttalib died, his son Abu Talib mourned him, saying:

I weep — the elder of the House of religion and generosity —
my eyes weeping, their tears falling like pearls.
He had virtues that surpassed the masters of nations;
the brave and singular one — I shall mourn him.
The one who relieved distress in times of hardship and vengeance —
Abu al-Harith al-Ma'la, the exalted one, has departed.

<!-- page 85 -->

He protected his protectors from heroes with honor and knowledge;
he was the leader whom no one could rival in character.
He was the light that dispelled the darkness of oppression and injustice;
the guardian of the House — the House of God — who filled it
with the virtues of a people of pride and nobility.
Lord of the assembly, generous to his guests,
and we have mentioned the assembly that was prepared for 'Abd al-Muttalib, going back to the time of Qusayy ibn Kilab, for him and for the great men among his sons.

Abu Talib also said about his father:

7 — The Quraysh wept, all of them, for their fathers,
and you brought joy — O Umayma — today mixed with grief.
8 — Describe my weeping and my tears shed for him,
while the flowers bloom — a spring for Arabs and non-Arabs alike.
9 — The women of Rahat from Bani Asad love you,
and the protection of creation, from those who return and those who cast.
10 — Was he not the adornment of all the people of the earth?

[Hudhayfa ibn Ghanem's poem in lamentation of 'Abd al-Muttalib]

Hudhayfa ibn Ghanem, the brother of Banu 'Adi ibn Ka'b ibn Lu'ayy, wept for 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim and spoke of his virtue and of Qusayy's virtue over the Quraysh and his virtue over his sons:

My eyes pour forth tears upon my chest,
an outpouring of tears and the grief of every sorrow —
the weeping of a man whose loss no age can replace.
The spade and the strike, and the sajma are gone;
gone is the one who was the pride of Quraysh and its shield.

<!-- page 86 -->

The beautiful life — no regression or pretense —
for the man of strong resolve, who was swift when roused,
the one who was the arbiter and the defender,
the spring of Lu'ayy in hardship and plenty,
noble in nature, generous in hospitality and slaughter,
fine in his bearing, whether unshod or shod.
I blessed them with honors and remembrance,
and their best — original in root, branch, and stock —
and thanks from the oppressed, saved from the dust;
and the first of them: measured judgment, forbearance, and God.
The darkness of night shines from him like the full moon —
upon the elder of praise, whose face was radiant —
and 'Abd Manaf, the sayyid of Fahr,
and the pure of the pilgrims, then the bread of Hashim,
a pillar whom every proud man takes pride in.
He folded a zamzama at the maqam, and it became:
may every hardship be relieved for him in distress,
and the family of Qusayy from the very eyes —
the falcon's egg set apart from among them.
Their fathers are the keepers of their dignity in old age and youth,
and the link of the House of God in ease and hardship.
Qusayy — whose memory is in all a metaphor —
long live the auspicious in character and in bearing,
prayers like al-Radniyya of the dark complexion.
I am drawn by the people of the cave,
Abu 'Utba al-Mulki, to his beloved ones —
pure of garment and pledges, free of treachery.
And Hamza is like the full moon, trembling with dew;
the one of Du al-Qurmi, merciful with this son-in-law.
And 'Abd Manaf — no one bears more nobility —
the kings are not to be scrutinized or challenged.
The elders are the best of elders, and he entertained them;
you find him following his father's path.
When did you find the noblest of them emerging
if he preceded good deeds in the early afternoon?
They filled al-Batha with glory and pride,
and 'Abd Manaf — their grandfather — the mender of the broken,
builders of the highest, and builders of honor.

[28/f]

If a precious wish is cherished and fulfilled —
and men of nobility remain without isolation,

<!-- page 87 -->

if they set out in the morning of the slaughter
so that al-Hamaj drinks from it, and others too,
hidden between wood and stone —
three days their travelers shelter there —
and we draw only from the heat or the hollow springs.
And they gave us a song before all that,
and they forgave the foolish words of the ignorant,
and they forgave the wolf to avenge without taking vengeance themselves,
and those who led Bani Bakr to consume us,
and they gathered the alliance of all the houses —
give them thanks until they vanish into the grave.
So go forth — your family awaits, do not tarry —
he gave his daughter in marriage to confound us;
we explained the Tihama land and found it
counted among our enemies, as Banu Fahr made us submit.

[37]

With his security, until the Ear waded into the sea,
and they came while the people wanted their assembly,
and there was nothing in it but the devotion of Banu 'Amr.
Bathara sweeps the water from the darkness of the sea;
they built it as a great house and sealed it.

<!-- page 88 -->

[Abu Talib's guardianship of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him)]

When 'Abd al-Muttalib died, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was eight years old. Abu Talib fulfilled what his father had entrusted to him: he took charge of him, sheltered him, singled him out with special care, adopted him as his own, and kept him close. He was the most devoted of all his children to him, and the most loving toward him.

When Abu Talib wished to travel to the Levant with a group of Quraysh merchants, he began to prepare his provisions and equipment. He noticed signs of profound sadness on the face of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), so he asked him how he was. The Prophet conveyed his grief at being left behind and confided how deeply he felt the separation. He asked Abu Talib to take him along. Abu Talib relented and took him with him.

[The story of the monk]

They stopped near the hermitage of a monk in the land of Busra — known as Bahira. The Quraysh caravans had often passed by and rested near his hermitage, but he never came down to them nor spoke to any of them. When they stopped on this journey, however, the monk came down to them and inquired about their well-being. He was a learned man. He looked intently at the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and studied him carefully, then asked about his situation and his story. He was told about him, and then he drew Abu Talib aside — having confirmed that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was indeed with them — and said to him privately: "This is the affair of your nephew — he will be a prophet, and this is his time.

<!-- page 89 -->

His sign is clear and his proof established. Beware of the Jews on his account, and return — out of love for him — to your land, for I fear for him from them."

While he was still speaking with him, some Jews arrived. They caught sight of Bahira with Abu Talib, looked closely at the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), and recognized in him the signs of prophethood. They grasped it, so Bahira turned them away from him, warned them against the Quraysh, and cautioned them that they would not be able to harm him. Abu Talib then took him back to Mecca.

It was also said that Bahira saw the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) with a cloud of light above him, shading him and moving with him wherever he went. Bahira came down from his hermitage, prepared food for them, and invited them to eat, gathering the Quraysh caravan together. Three Jews opposed him in this — Driss, Tamam, and Zarir — intending to lie in wait for the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). But Bahira chose a different course of action.

In the noble lineage of free men are those who are honored —
I shall depart having bade him farewell in peace.

Abu Talib said:

Did you not see me — after my anxieties —
with Ahmad, when I drew the reins tight,

<!-- page 90 -->

while he spread his palms to grasp the reins?
When he wept, the wicked were driven from us.
I recalled his father, and from that a lesson was drawn —
the eyes shedding tears upon the chest.
I said: You depart as a grown man in the company of others —
there is comfort in misfortune, beyond mere reproach.
My spirit is with love; my intent is not apart from it —
we have a difference of view, though it looms great.
We have drink and food,
and he came to the lake of Nafisi, and they gathered.
He said: Bring your companions to our meal.
We said: We have gathered everyone — no one is excluded.
Many — today there is no prohibition.
The heat of the sun is shaded by clouds,
and they sat together, exalted and serene,
a covering spread over them for their shelter,
while all the people were unable to sleep.
Driss and Tamam — and he was among them —
he turned them away from him with skillful argument.
They came with intent to kill Muhammad,
and he said to them: You are not unjust men —
by the interpretation of the Torah, so that you may be certain —
and no day has ever been seen like this one.
This is from his signs and his distinction;
we were glad for the man who carried that burden.
When we alighted upon that land, they shone brightly.
He said: Call him — for the food is ours.

[39]

When he saw him approaching, over his head
was the neighborhood of Dhahira, bowing in semi-prostration, clinging to it,
to his throat and chest — every manner of attachment.
Bahira — the seer of the eye — stood in the midst of the tents,
and accepted with open palms what he had seen.

[30/f]

He also said in a similar vein:

He wept in dread when we departed with Muhammad,
as though he would never see me returning to Ma'ad.


<!-- page 91 -->
Abu Talib's Sponsorship of the Prophet:

So he was wakeful, stirring his tears,
And I poured out to my bed all my grief.
You will find rest from me in this land —
I shook his head: Draw your seat close, and heed
The resolution of our command and our guidance,
And leave the reins of the matter, and leave us,
For He who shows mercy to people is no stranger.
There will be a scent among those who return in mourning,
Coming from the valley of the land of Iyad.
We were glad of the one who rode that mount —
Accounts that sweeten the grief of every heart.
They did not return until they had seen in Muhammad
Signs that deceived a group, both many and few.
They even beheld the traces left in every city,
Studied, yet all of them were deceived by corruption.
Perfect and complete, and he was present —
Why after lying and such long distance?
So he said to them, "Bahira," and they were certain,
And their striving in God's cause is the right of Jihad.
As he said to the monk who had come —
He said: he had every right to say it.
Wrap the books written for him with ink,
And I fear the envious, and that —
I hold the same as the dwellings of boys:
The son of Amna, the Trustworthy, Muhammad,
And the eagles have been brought low by the darkest ones.
When you take hold of his reins, his mercy flows
Like the meeting of warm and cool waters.
So I wipe the tears from my eyes
And upheld the will of the forebears within it.
I tended to him with the closeness of near kinship,
Bright of face, praying for salvation,
And she was bid to walk among his cousins.

He also said in this regard:

(1) An addition from "B".
(2) See: Diwan, 164–165.

<!-- page 92 -->
The Eyes of News and the Arts of Archaeology — by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

The visitor's fold has parted ways;
They walked to the farthest reach of knowledge.
They met in honour at the lookout,
Even when the people looked upon me.
A companion is a ward. Envy
Ink, and tell them a truthful hadith —
In it is the substance of heart and liver,
And the people of Judah came to him and saw him,
About him, and I am greatly pressed.
They came to kill Muhammad, and he destroyed them.

[The Standing of the Messenger of Allah with His Uncle Abu Talib]

The Messenger of Allah was with his uncle Abu Talib like the most beloved of his own children — condemned, glorified, honoured, and esteemed — on account of the signs Abu Talib had witnessed in him: his virtue, the signs of his prophecy and message, and the bequest of Abd al-Muttalib his father to him. In the Messenger of Allah there was nobility of character, purity of lineage, and in him were found forbearance, truthfulness, sound reason, and piety, asceticism, and virtue. He was the most righteous of people in his dealings with his uncle Abu Talib, and he held with him an elevated and firmly established standing. Fatima bint Asad was his uncle's wife; she preferred him above all her own children, honouring him, serving him, and glorifying him.

[The Birth of the Commander of the Faithful, Peace Be Upon Him]

When Fatima bint Asad became pregnant with the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him —

[41]

(1) (2): "Knowledge," and what has been established is from "B".
(2) See: Kitab al-Azhar, 226; Katar al-Fawa'id, 1/116.

<!-- page 93 -->
Abu Talib's Sponsorship of the Prophet:

Peace be upon him — she saw in a dream as though a pillar of iron had been wrenched from the earth; then a light blazed in the sky until it reached the heavens, then descended back, and stood for a time before rising again from her feet. She said, "What is this?" It was said to her: This is the destroyer of the people of disbelief, the bearer of the Charter of Victory, strong in his troops — he who is God's aid to His Prophet and His support against His enemy. He is the beloved and the pious, a parable in the exalted heavens; the earth is set by him, and the majestic mountains, the vast seas, and the shining stars.

When the time of labour came, her husband Abu Talib instructed her to circumambulate the Ka'bah, to wipe her hands along its interior, and to offer as many supplications as she was able. She did so, and gave birth to him inside the Ka'bah. When she wished to depart, she quickly gathered him and took him home, and began to raise him.

Allah placed the love of him in the heart of His Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him and his family — who was at that time in the fullness of his youth and his splendour — and he said to Fatima bint Asad: "O his mother" — for so he used to address her — "place his cradle beside mine." He, peace be upon him, took charge of much of the child's upbringing until he grew strong, his guidance became clear, and he was rightly directed.

(1) Addition to the text of benefits.
(2) An addition to the severed chain of transmission.
(3) "2": "Crack," and what has been established is from "B".
(4) An addition from "B".

<!-- page 94 -->
The Eyes of News and the Arts of Archaeology — by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

[The Story of the Rebuilding of the Ka'bah and the Messenger of Allah's Judgment Among the Quraysh]

The Ka'bah had been demolished, and the Quraysh set about rebuilding it. The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, was at that time a young man raised upon the purity and noble character of the prophets, and the people called him al-Amin — the Trustworthy. When the tribes had finished their work and reached the place of the Black Stone, every clan of the Quraysh wished to have the honour of carrying it to its position, and they fell into dispute and quarrel over this. They then agreed to submit the matter to the judgment of the first man who should appear before them.

The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, appeared, and they said: "The Trustworthy man has come." They told him the matter, so he took his garment and placed the stone upon it, then said: "Let one man from each clan of the Quraysh take hold of the hem of the garment, and let them all lift it together." They were pleased with his ruling and accepted it, and they did so until they brought it to its place.

[42]

The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, then set it in its position with his own hand. The Quraysh called him "al-Amin" because his grace, honesty, and chastity had become manifest to them.

[The Account of the War of the Ungodly]

When the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, reached the age of fourteen and entered his fifteenth year, the War of the Ungodly broke out between the Quraysh and their allies from Kinana on one side, and Qays ibn Aylan on the other. It was called "the War of the Ungodly" because the two parties did not hold sacred what was sacred —

(1) "B": "al-Azar".
(2) "Let each tribe take hold of a portion of the garment and then lift it all together" (Biography of the Prophet, 1/197).

<!-- page 95 -->
The Eyes of News and the Arts of Archaeology — by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

[The Account of the Marriage of the Messenger of Allah to Khadijah bint Khuwaylid]

When Khadijah bint Khuwaylid ibn Asad ibn Abd al-Uzza ibn Qusayy ibn Kilab ibn Murrah ibn Ka'b ibn Lu'ayy [ibn Ghalib ibn Fahr] beheld the virtue of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family — and what shone forth from his signs and was evident from the marks of his prophethood and the glad tidings of it — her slave Maysarah had accompanied him on some of his journeys and had seen the cloud shade him, while monks spoke of his virtue and informed him of the nearness of his mission.

Khadijah was a resolute woman, held in the highest esteem among her people. She sent word to the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, saying: "O my cousin, I have desired you on account of your kinship, your standing among your people, your honesty, your good character, and the truthfulness of your speech." She then offered herself to him in marriage. At that time she was the foremost of the women of Quraysh

[43]

in honour and nobility of lineage. Abu Talib stepped forward and conducted the marriage on behalf of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family.

The scholars of biography said:

Abu Talib attended the contract along with the leading men of Banu Hashim and the notables of the Quraysh. The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, was wed, and Abu Talib said:

(1) An addition from "B".
(2) See: Man la Yahduruhu al-Faqih, 3/397–398; al-Kamil of al-Mubarrad, 3/1362; I'jaz al-Qur'an of al-Baqillani, 153; Nathr al-Durr, 1/396; al-Tadhkirah al-Hamdaniyyah, 6/253–254; Rabi' al-Abrar, 4/299–300; Sharh Nahj al-Balagha, 14/70.

<!-- page 96 -->
Abu Talib's Sponsorship of the Prophet:

— Kinana and Qays Aylan — in which sacred prohibitions were violated between them.

The Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, was present at some of those battles when he was twenty years old. He said: "I was — on that day — defending my uncles," meaning he would return upon their enemy the arrows they shot at them. When he came — peace be upon him and his family — the Quraysh and Kinana gained the upper hand. They knew this, and they would ask his uncle Abu Talib to bring him out with them. The War of the Ungodly has a story that we have summarised here.

(1) "B": "The two tribes," and what has been established is from "B".
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 1/186.

<!-- page 97 -->
"Praise be to Allah, who made us the descendants of Abraham, peace be upon him, and planted from Ishmael a flourishing root, an honoured lineage. He made him from the heart of His house, and placed in him reverence, and made it a sanctuary of refuge for us, a safe haven, and He made it a gathering place for people."

"And this nephew of mine, Muhammad ibn Abdallah — no man is weighed against him in merit but that he surpasses him in vision and grace, in what is permitted and what is sound, in piety and in nobility — though when it comes to wealth, say: wealth is a fleeting shadow, a transient thing. And Muhammad is a man whose lineage you know, and whose kinship, truthfulness, [33/f] and honesty are plain to all. He seeks the hand of Khadijah bint Khuwaylid, and she has agreed with what he has put forward and deferred from my wealth, the amount being such-and-such — and by Allah, after this he shall have great standing and momentous affairs."

It was said: the dowry was twenty she-camels, ten waqi of gold, a male slave and a female slave.

Khadijah was the best of the wives of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family. He did not marry another woman while she lived — may Allah have mercy on her and be pleased with her — out of reverence for her rank, elevation of her status, and honour of her memory. No woman among his wives could equal her in virtue or surpass her, and all of his children, peace be upon him, were from her,

(1) More than "B." (2) "Boy" in the Spring of the Righteous. (3) An addition from the Spring of the Righteous. (4) An addition from the Spring of the Righteous. (5) An addition from the Spring of the Righteous. (6) An addition from the Spring of the Righteous.

<!-- page 98 -->
The Eyes of News and the Arts of Archaeology — by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

except Ibrahim, peace be upon him, who was from Maria the Copt.

The children Khadijah bore to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, were, firstly, al-Qasim — by whom he was given his kunya, may Allah bless him and his family — then al-Tayyib, then al-Tahir.

[44]

His daughters were: Ruqayyah, Zaynab, Umm Kulthum, and Fatimah, peace be upon her.

[The Account of Khadijah with Waraqah, and Waraqah's Confirmation of the Prophethood]

After the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, married her, she continued to observe from him signs of prophethood and marks of the message, and she would recount these to her cousin Waraqah ibn Nawfal. He would hear her gladly and say: "By Allah, he is the awaited Prophet." Waraqah ibn Nawfal died before the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, was sent. He was a poet, and whenever Khadijah told him what she and the Messenger of Allah had witnessed, he would be moved and say: "When he is raised up, I will believe in him and follow him." In this vein he said:

For them, as long as memory endures,
I have been in remembrance of longing.
Description upon description from Khadijah,
I have waited long, O Khadijah.
Your words show me a way out of it —
Of monks who refuse to be led astray.
What I have found in the words of a shepherd
In the midst of two peoples weighing upon my hope.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 1/191–192.

<!-- page 99 -->
The Account of the Marriage of the Messenger of Allah to Khadijah:

And reckon those who are on pilgrimage —
The wilderness made ready for him to ripple.
He finds the one who greets him welcome,
And the one who fights him shall be vanquished.
I have witnessed, and I was among the first of them —
Would that it were not hidden from you.
And if she had stayed within her house, she would have been foolish,
Along with those who hold the Quraysh in contempt.
By the Lord of Thrones, if they bow down in humility,
I hope for what they all once despised.
Who among those chosen from among the people of towers —
Is this matter of folly anything other than disbelief?
The disbelievers cry out against it.
"If they are purified and abide, things will be such
That Muhammad shall prevail among us,
And the light of justice will appear throughout the land —
Or if he should perish, every son cast down
From the fates destroyed by chains." [34/f]

[45]

(1) An addition from "B".


<!-- page 100 -->
[Mention of the Islam of Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him)]

Ali (peace be upon him) was raised in the household of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). He was cared for and brought up in an atmosphere of purity and piety. Among the Arabs of noble standing, it was customary that when a man had a son and wished to discipline and cultivate him properly, he would entrust the child to a respected elder of his people, who would raise him and employ him in his household so that the boy might develop properly and find his role.

Abu Talib had several sons: Talib, Aqeel, Ja'far, and Ali (peace be upon him), with roughly ten years between each of them. He gave Aqeel to al-'Aas, Ja'far to Hamza (may Allah be pleased with him), and Ali to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). It was also said that he kept Aqeel with him and told the Messenger of Allah: take whomever you wish — so Ali came to live with him.

Thus Ali (peace be upon him) grew up in the household of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). He never worshipped an idol, never committed a sin; he was raised entirely in purity and piety, keeping away from vice and immorality. His character was molded by the Prophet's own character — may Allah bless him and his family — and his conduct followed the Prophet's way of life. Khadija, who knew the love the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) had for Ali, would honor him and hold him in the highest esteem.

Then Allah, Exalted be He, chose His Prophet Muhammad (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) —

<!-- page 101 -->
— Allah, Lord of all people, Seal of the Covenant, and the best of the sons of Adam — and sent him as a Prophet and a Guide. When the revelation descended upon him and Gabriel (peace be upon him) brought him the message, he called Khadija to Islam on that very day. This took place on the twenty-seventh of the month of Rajab, and she accepted Islam at that moment, bearing witness to the Oneness of Allah and affirming his prophethood. That was on a Monday.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) then called Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) on Tuesday — the day after — and told him what Allah had assigned to him and to what He had called him, and that He had sent him to all people. Ali said: "By my father and my mother, tell me more every hour." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) replied: "I will tell you as you wish; but what I have told you in truth is not yet known to anyone else."

Ali (peace be upon him) said: "I wished to put this before my father first, but even as I was saying that, I declared: I bear witness that there is no god but Allah and that you are the Messenger of Allah."

The prophethood of Muhammad (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) came on Monday, and the Islam of Ali (peace be upon him) came on Tuesday — the second day — making him one of the forerunners and the very first of the true believers. This is a point of consensus across the entire community, uncontested and unopposed. The most that those who wish to diminish

<!-- page 102 -->
the virtue of Ali (peace be upon him) and what Allah has bestowed upon him can say is: "His Islam came too early — he had not yet reached the age of legal accountability, so it should not be counted." Yet they forgot the words of Allah, the Almighty, concerning Yahya ibn Zakariyya (peace be upon him): "O Yahya, hold fast to the Book with strength — and We gave him wisdom as a child" [Maryam: 12]. And His saying concerning the Messiah (peace be upon him): "He will speak to the people in the cradle" [Al-'Imran: 46]. These are in the context of prophethood — so how much more do Islam and faith, which are below that rank and lesser than that station, apply? In truth, their attitude is nothing but ingratitude toward the virtue of his guardianship and denial of Allah's grace — they have rejected what is plain to the eye and turned away from the proof.

The arguments on this matter are extensive, and this is not the place to set them all out; whoever wishes to investigate will find them in numerous books and compiled refutations. It is reported from 'Abdullah ibn 'Umar ibn al-Khattab — and he is among those from whom they themselves narrate — that he said: "When a person reaches the age of seven, his faith and disbelief are recorded."

The arguments on this matter are extensive, wide-ranging, and go beyond the scope of this book. A little is enough for one who governs himself by reason, is fair to himself, and listens to the word of Allah his Lord — one who is not driven by whims or blinded by hatred of those whom Allah has chosen and been pleased with.

Ali (peace be upon him) remained for several years praying alongside the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). They would go out together and pray in the valleys of Makkah, and they continued in this way for eight years. During all that time, on the face of the earth,

<!-- page 103 -->
no one had entered Islam except them and Khadija bint Khuwaylid (may Allah be pleased with her).

Abu Talib came upon them one day and said to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family): "What is this you are doing, nephew?" He replied: "O uncle, this is the religion of Allah with which He has sent me. This is the faith of our forefather Ibrahim. You, uncle, are the most deserving person before whom I should call aloud and invite to the religion of Allah, and you are the most deserving of those who should answer and support Him."

The narrators say that Abu Talib replied to him: "As for abandoning what I follow, I find no way to do that. But as for supporting you — by Allah, no one shall harm you with something you hate, as long as I remain." Then he turned to Ali (peace be upon him) and said: "Follow what Muhammad says to you. Do not leave him and do not forsake him, for he has called you to nothing but the religion of Allah."

It is narrated from Abu 'Uthman, the judge of Mosul, with a chain of transmission going back to Abu Ayyub al-Ansari, who said:

"I heard the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) say: Ali ibn Abi Talib and I worshipped Allah for seven years before anyone else believed in Him. Allah, the Almighty, says: 'Those who bear the Throne and those around it glorify their Lord, believe in Him, and seek His forgiveness' ['Umar: 7] — for those on the earth. And that was for me, Ali,

<!-- page 104 -->
and Khadija bint Khuwaylid — and then came those who believed afterward."

It is also narrated: "Abu Talib passed by the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) while Ali (peace be upon him) was with him as they prayed. Ja'far ibn Abi Talib was there with his father as well. Abu Talib turned to his son Ja'far al-Tayyar (peace be upon him) and said: Go and join your cousin's wing. So Ja'far (may Allah be pleased with him) came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family), accepted Islam, and prayed with them — peace be upon them — and that was the first congregational prayer the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) led."

[The hadith of the clan at the beginning of the call]

We have narrated from Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) that he said:

"When Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) revealed to His Messenger (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family): 'And warn your nearest kinsmen' [Al-Shu'ara': 214], the Messenger of Allah gathered the men of Banu 'Abd al-Muttalib with a haunch of mutton and a cup of milk. At that time they numbered

<!-- page 105 -->
forty men, among whom there was not one who could not eat an entire haunch and drink a full skin on his own. Yet they all ate until they were full and drank until they were satisfied.

On that day, the Messenger of Allah said to them: 'O sons of Abd al-Muttalib, hear me out. Be the kings and rulers of the earth, for Allah does not send a messenger except that He appoints for him a guardian, a minister, an heir, and a brother. So which of you will be my brother, my guardian, my minister, and my heir?'

Not one of them spoke up or accepted.

Ali (peace be upon him) said: 'There was no one left among them except me — and I was the youngest of them on that day — but I stepped forward and offered myself. He presented the matter to me and I said: Yes, I am, O Messenger of Allah.' He said: 'Yes — you are my brother, my guardian, my minister, and my heir.' Then he said to them: 'Listen to him and obey him.'"

When they had gone, Abu Lahab said to them: "If your companion had not bewitched you, you would not believe what you saw — that he brought you a sheep's haunch and a cup of milk and filled you all." And they mocked and said to Abu Talib: "Your son has been appointed over you today."

<!-- page 106 -->
[The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) openly proclaimed the call]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) continued calling to Allah (swt) and conveying His message. Allah (swt) commanded him to do so openly, saying: "So proclaim what you have been commanded and turn away from the polytheists" [Al-Hijr: 94]. He came forward — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — and invited the Quraysh and the people of Makkah to Islam. He told them what he had been sent with, warned them of Allah's punishment, and spoke against their idols and what they attributed to them instead of Allah. His uncle Abu Talib stood up to defend him against those who sought to deny and obstruct him, and forbade the Quraysh from harming him. Yet the Prophet's mission grew ever mightier, for it challenged their cherished beliefs and condemned their gods.

[Mention of what passed between the Quraysh and Abu Talib]

The leaders of Quraysh gathered in resentment at what he had called them to and in defiance against him. Those who took the lead in this — who stood up and sat down together in opposition and deliberation — were Abu Sufyan, who is Sakhr ibn Harb ibn Umayyah ibn Abd Shams ibn Abd Manaf; and his kinsmen from Banu Umayyah; Utbah and Shayba, the sons of Rabi'a ibn Abd Shams; Abu al-Bakhtari — who is al-'Aas ibn Hisham — al-Walid ibn al-Mughira; al-Aswad ibn Abd al-Muttalib; al-'Aas ibn Wa'il; al-Hakam ibn Abi al-'Aas; and Abu Jahl ibn Hisham.

They gathered, deliberated, and concluded that they should go to Abu Talib and put their grievance before him. They came to him and said: "O Abu Talib, your nephew Muhammad has insulted

<!-- page 107 -->
our forefathers, denounced our gods, declared our fathers to have been misguided, and you have shielded him from us. Either you restrain him, or leave us to deal with him." Abu Talib replied to them gently and with dignity, and they departed.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) went on fulfilling his command, calling to Allah his Lord and conveying what Allah had sent him with. Many of the Quraysh accepted Islam, and this weighed heavily on Banu Umayyah and those among the Quraysh who followed them. So they came again to Abu Talib and said: "O Abu Talib, we have come to you before and thanked you for what you granted us, and you hold honor and standing among us. We have endured what your nephew has brought upon us — the cursing of our gods, the disparagement of our fathers, the dismissal of our dreams as foolishness — and now a group of Quraysh have gone over to him. Either you stop him from us, or we shall make war on him and on you and those who stand with him, until one of the two sides is destroyed." Abu Talib replied to them with a fair and measured answer and said: "I am considering what is best in this, God willing."

He then called the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) privately and told him what his people had resolved — that they had decided it was easier for them to strike first than to wait — and said to him: "O nephew, your people have come to me with what you have heard. Spare me and spare yourself, and do not burden me with more than I can bear."


<!-- page 108 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) assumed that Abu Talib was wavering, and said: "O uncle, if you wish, do not be overwhelmed. But I, by Allah — even if they placed the sun on my right hand and the moon on my left — I would not abandon this matter until Allah caused it to prevail or I perished in its pursuit." At that, Abu Talib stood firm for him, called him back, and said: "O son of my brother, go and say what you love and do whatever you will — for by God, I will never abandon you for anything." He said in this regard:

*I swear they will not reach you while I am buried in the dirt — by gathering all their forces against you.*
*Preach and let that not diminish you — work hard at your matter, and have no anger.*
*You believed, and you were truthful, and you called — claiming to be a sincere adviser.*
*The best of wilderness religions is our religion, and yet a religion has been offered that must inevitably come.*
*Were it not for blame and my fear of reproach — I would have found myself free to follow it.*

This too is testimony from Abu Talib to the truthfulness of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah bless him and his family — and that his religion is the finest of religions. What he endured in suppressing his own acceptance of that religion stands as the greatest proof of his conviction.

<!-- page 109 -->
[Quraysh Approached Abu Talib a Third Time, Sending 'Amarah ibn al-Walid]

When the Quraysh learned that Abu Talib would not abandon the Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — nor surrender him, and that they had united in their hostility toward him, they came to Abu Talib again. They said: "O Abu Talib, this is 'Amarah ibn al-Walid — the finest and most vigorous young man among the Quraysh. We offer him to you: take him as your son, for he is yours. Hand over to us your nephew, who has opposed your religion and the religion of your fathers, sown division among your people, and made a mockery of their cherished beliefs — for he is but a man like any man." Abu Talib replied: "By Allah, what a terrible bargain you offer me — that you give me your son to clothe and feed, while I hand over my son to be killed! That, by God, will never happen." Al-Mut'im ibn 'Adi ibn Nawfal ibn 'Abd Manaf then said to him: "Your people have been fair to you, O Abu Talib, and have gone to great lengths to spare you what you dislike. Yet I see you unwilling to accept anything from them." Abu Talib replied: "By God, they have not been fair to me — rather you have all abandoned me and united against me. Do then whatever seems right to you."

[Abu Talib's Poetry Rebuking al-Mut'im and Those of Banu 'Abd Manaf Who Abandoned Him]

Abu Talib then composed verses rousing Banu 'Abd Manaf to support him while rebuking Banu 'Abd Shams:

*Do not tell 'Amr and al-Walid and al-Mut'im — that I would ever wish to be under your protection.*
*From the poplars I love, great froth*
*sprinkles on the legs from a drop of urine.*

<!-- page 110 -->
*If he stood above the heights, it was said to him: well done.*
*If they were asked, they deferred to another.*
*As though I were swept from the head of one who wore a desert cloak.*
*Yes, they hold authority — yet they are dragged along.*
*They cast us out as embers reject their own fire —*
*most of all 'Abd Shams and their feeble ones.*
*Some of them have amounted to nothing.*
*They brought into the matter those of uncertain lineage.*
*No one is mentioned among the people but that he is named —*
*al-Walid, whose father was but a slave alone.*
*They came to a lush meadow brought by the sea,*
*yet they were our lords when we sought victory.*
*And Tim, and Makhzum, and Zahrah among them —*
*they were like cisterns I did not fill.*
*Their dreams and reason have gone silent,*
*and some of them were not of our line — they were bound.*
*By God, enmity will not cease from us —*
*lagging behind fragrant blossoms, not following after.*
*I see our brothers from our shared mother and father*
*as the most settled of people in their dwellings.*

[The Persistence of Banu 'Abd Shams in Their Enmity toward Banu 'Abd Manaf]

This only deepened the enmity of Banu 'Abd Shams and their allies toward the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him. As for what came from the Banu Hashim and Banu 'Abd al-Muttalib, their hearts turned fully to supporting the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — with none among them holding back except Abu Lahab. Banu al-Muttalib and Banu Hashim, as we have noted, had from ancient times been as one. Abu Talib said to them:

*How long must we, O Hashim, remain while the people stand in one party*

<!-- page 111 -->
*and we in fear and isolation?*
*They call their horses by the neck —*
*speeds unknown,*
*like the black one you exaggerate,*
*like the cat that covers the opening.*
*They must kick against the rock*
*with every sharp blade upon a sable.*
*O people, protect your masses —*
*the finest inheritance from the finest.*
*Iron, five-fold, to shake its limit,*
*protected by the swift in the cradle.*
*How many times has war been witnessed in it,*
*at the time of rebellion in the wastelands,*
*and in the fury of war like a cub —*
*do not bow if they are rebuked.*
*Wide, six flames.*

[Abu Talib's Poetry in Support of the Prophet — Peace Be upon Him]

When Abu Talib saw the sons of Hashim and the sons of al-Muttalib rally around him, his confidence grew and his resolve in supporting the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — was renewed. He declared openly to Banu 'Abd Shams and those bellies of Quraysh who were with them in their enmity, saying:

*Flashing like lightning —*
*beware the whistle and the signal.*
*We have protected the Prophet, the messenger of the King.*
*Strike and melt without plundering —*
*the protection of a tender uncle.*
*I throw and shield the messenger of the King.*
*The wolf of the herd — beware the phoenix.*
*And though he be courteous to his enemies,*
*the lion too visits the narrow pass.*
*But I shall raise them high —*

<!-- page 112 -->
*following from among Banu Dhu Habb.*
*I swear I have not abandoned the Prophet, nor*
*when things grew heated and fierce.*
*'Ali and Ja'far are my trust,*
*summit and belonging to Takab.*
*I see them ready to meet the encounter —*
*my brother, son of my mother, among them, and my father.*
*Do not let them down, and support your cousin.*

He also said:

[Abu Talib's Poetry Praising His People for Their Noble Deeds]

When Abu Talib saw what pleased him in his people — finding them steadfast at his side — he praised them, recalled their ancestors, and spoke of the virtue and standing of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — so that some among them might strengthen their resolve and unanimously commit to his cause. He said:

*The slave of Manaf is its secret and its core.*
*If the Quraysh gather one day in pride,*
*in Hashim lies its honor and its antiquity.*
*And if you take the honors of every tribe —*
*he is the chosen one of her secrets and generosity.*
*And if you depart one day — then Muhammad:*
*over us she did not triumph, and swept away her illusions.*
*Quraysh have squandered their wealth and their fullness.*
*If they bend the stiff necks of the borders, we shall set them right —*
*for we were not ones to accept darkness.*
*And we protect its protectors every day like a crane,*
*and we strike with our stones those who hurl at it.*
*With our shoulders it calls and its throws grow —*
*we have revived the oudh of the people.*

<!-- page 113 -->
[Al-Walid ibn al-Mughirah Is Confounded by the Qur'an]

Al-Walid ibn al-Mughirah then gathered a group of Quraysh around him — he being one of their leaders — as the season of pilgrimage approached. He said to them: "O assembly of Quraysh, the season has come and the Arab delegations will arrive before you. They have heard of this matter concerning your companion, so agree on one position — do not differ, lest you give the lie to one another and your words contradict each other." They said: "O Abu 'Abd Shams, speak — tell us what opinion we should voice." He said: "No — you speak first, and I will listen to what you say." They said: "We shall say he is a soothsayer." He said: "By Allah, he is no soothsayer — we have seen soothsayers, and what he says bears no resemblance to the murmuring or the manner of soothsayers." They said: "We shall say he is mad." He said: "We have seen madness and know it well — what he does has nothing of madness's interference or its whispering." They said: "We shall say he is a poet." He said: "We have known all manner of poetry — its metres, its forms, its recitation — and what he brings is not poetry." They said: "We shall say he is a sorcerer." He said: "We have seen sorcerers and their sorcery — what he does has nothing of their breath or their knotted cords." They said: "Then what shall we say, O Abu 'Abd Shams?" He replied: "By Allah, his speech is sweet — and none of what you have said fits it. The closest thing you can say is this: he is a sorcerer who has come with a magic that divides a man from his father, from his wife, and from his brother and his clan." So they dispersed in agreement on that, stationing themselves along the roads to warn people about him.

<!-- page 114 -->
Concerning al-Walid ibn al-Mughirah, Allah — may He be exalted — revealed: ﴿ذَرۡنِي وَمَنۡ خَلَقۡتُ وَحِيدٗا ۝ وَجَعَلۡتُ لَهُۥ مَالٗا مَّمۡدُودٗا ۝ وَبَنِينَ شُهُودٗا ۝ وَمَهَّدتُّ لَهُۥ تَمۡهِيدٗا ۝ ثُمَّ يَطۡمَعُ أَنۡ أَزِيدَ ۝ كَلَّآ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ لِأٓيَٰتِنَا عَنِيدٗا ۝ سَأُرۡهِقُهُۥ صَعُودٗا ۝ إِنَّهُۥ فَكَّرَ وَقَدَّرَ ۝ فَقُتِلَ كَيۡفَ قَدَّرَ ۝ ثُمَّ قُتِلَ كَيۡفَ قَدَّرَ ۝ ثُمَّ نَظَرَ ۝ ثُمَّ عَبَسَ وَبَسَرَ ۝ ثُمَّ أَدۡبَرَ وَٱسۡتَكۡبَرَ ۝ فَقَالَ إِنۡ هَٰذَآ إِلَّا سِحۡرٞ يُؤۡثَرُ ۝ إِنۡ هَٰذَآ إِلَّا قَوۡلُ ٱلۡبَشَرِ ۝ سَأُصۡلِيهِ سَقَرَ ۝ وَمَآ أَدۡرَىٰكَ مَا سَقَرُ ۝ لَا تُبۡقِي وَلَا تَذَرُ﴾ [Al-Muddaththir: 11–29] — and on to the end of those verses.

[Abu Talib's Lamiyyah Poem Concerning the Quraysh]

When Abu Talib feared the provocations of the Arabs, he composed his celebrated poem in which he sought the protection of the sanctuary of Mecca and his standing within it, appealed to the nobles of his people, and made clear in his verses — as in other poems of his — that he was not a Muslim in the sense of formally following the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — yet that he would never abandon him for anything, even unto death. This poem, which I have cited here and in other versions of his poetry, was narrated by Abu Hafan 'Abdullah ibn Ahmad al-Mahrami. He said: Khalid ibn Harb recited to me from 'Abdullah ibn al-'Abbas ibn al-Husayn ibn

'Ubayd Allah ibn al-'Abbas al-Saqqar ibn 'Ali ibn Abi Talib — may Allah be pleased with the righteous among his descendants. Abu Talib said:

<!-- page 115 -->
*Mukhaysa — between Sudais and Basil —*
*tattooed, or shortness in the sinews.*
*Listening to truth or to falsehood —*
*my dear friend, I do not lend my ear to the former.*
*And there is no plunder when matters pile like hills.*
*My dear friend, opinion is of no use in company.*

*They have severed all bonds and means,*
*and when I saw the people, I misliked them —*
*they obeyed the enemy's command to cast us down.*
*They opened upon us enmity and harm,*
*biting in anger behind our backs with their fingers.*
*They made an alliance — or so I think —*
*and white fury from the contractor's heritage.*
*I bore them patiently — my soul, a patient beauty —*
*and I held fast to their garments with the reins.*
*I brought my house and my shelter —*
*I have where every supererogatory act is spent.*
*Standing together and receiving its rejection*
*with floods from Isaf and Na'il.*
*And where the Ash'arites are their passengers,*
*with their necks knotted like a snail.*
*You see the trust within it, and the marble and its adornment —*
*upon us with evil, or seasoned with falsehood.*
*I seek refuge with the Lord of people from every stabber,*
*and from whoever is beguiled in religion unless we are tried.*
*And from whoever is a man who seeks us with harm —*
*a man who would rejoice in Hira' as he descends.*
*And by Him who established a thunderbolt in its place —*
*by God, God is not heedless.*
*And in the House of God, from the belly of Mecca —*
*if they are satisfied with the sacrifice and the sacred rites,*
*and with the Black Stone when they wipe it,*
*on his feet, barefoot, without sandals.*
*And the footprints of Ibrahim in the open ground —*
*and the image and its likeness within them.*
*And runs between the track to al-Safa,*
*and from all who depart and from every traveler,*
*and from the pilgrims to the House of God from every journeyer,*
*except the free ones, unshackled.*
*And in al-Masjid al-Aqsa when they consecrate it.*


<!-- page 116 -->

They lift the bodies of the dead with their hands
And halt them on the ropes at evening
And the night of the gathering, and the houses of Mina —
Is there any sanctity or house above it?

He added that if relatives were near, he would reward them,
Swiftly, as they emerge from a torrent.
They pray that she will be burdened with the loads,
And with the great redness when they hold fast to it.
They are allied with Hajjaj, Bakr ibn Wa'il,
And Kinda, when they camp in the gravel on the eve —

In answer to him, the emotions of the means,
And their resolve gathers every gain.
Samar cut them down with the blade of the antelope,
Peacefully, and the ostriches unite.
They are like him, surrounding Basal and his hair.

Is there anyone who fears God rightly?
Is there anyone after this who is a refuge for the seeker?
We block the doors of Turk and Kabul;
The enemies submit to us, and if they do not —
We shall be safe, except by your command in Bulbul.

You lie — by the House of God — we do not abandon Mecca,
And when we obey him and fight.
You lie — by the House of God — in disgracing Muhammad,
And you marvel at our children and our women.

Hand it over until we insist in circling it,
The rise of generations under the same clay,
From the stabbing — the act of the partisan.

And by the life of God, I truly see
In the palm of a boy like a shooting star,
From the lineage of the two branches of Luay ibn Ghalib —
Do not let our bonds be severed.

My brother is Thiqa, the guardian of truth, the steadfast,
Warding off calamity when the idle man goes without.
Upon us comes an argument, after he met —
He surrounds Dhamar without withholding provision.

Two allies who bound the covenant they pledged;
And a people clad in iron will rise against you.
Until you see this grudge mounting as a deterrent —
Months, days, and years held sacred.

And what remains of a people who hold you, master, in no regard?

<!-- page 117 -->

The Prophet's word of the Call —
The protection of orphans is a shield for widows,
And white clouds watering from his face.
They are in mercy and virtue for him;
Destruction takes refuge in him from the House of Hashim —

For one another, and our reward for all.
By my life, I have run through hardship, and tomorrow
Brings an abiding punishment that does not delay the urgent.
May God have mercy on us, as a master and as Khalid.

But they obeyed the command of those tribes
And did not watch us with an article to their name.
They obeyed any, and the son of a slave will aid them,
And everyone who took a pledge did not bear it —

As we have received, from seven and beyond.
We give them a measure for a measure of the vessel.
If they are cast, or God is able to do so,
Let us be among the people of flock and camel.

And as for Abu Amr — he is my father, but he hates us;
Abu Amr came to us, then became an uncle.
Yes, we may yet hear a voice other than one of welcome,
From the ground between the trees, and he argued.

We have a grievance like that of the wronged.
The path of Abu al-Walid — what did you love in us?

And his mercy is upon us, and I am not ignorant;
I know a woman who lives by his opinion —
An envious liar who hates the deceitful.

Threshold, do not hear us utter a blatant word,
So live gently, cousin, not bereft.
And I do not care about him for his own sake.

You will part from us and receive one of the nightingales,
And I fear that if they do not increase their grazing,
It will be as was said of the bones of the contracted.

Abu Sufyan passed me with a grievance
And claims that I am not unaware of you;
He flees to a place where the water is cold.

As the enemy is both right and wrong,
And Uthman did not grieve for us, and he was negligent.
We are greeted every evening and morning,
And he swears by the Merciful that, as soon as he gives us life,
Each of us is constrained by him —
And I know that he remains unaware through the evening.

<!-- page 118 -->

Whether upon us, and the wind brings rain,
Compassionate, yet concealing what lies within —
Nor are most affairs easily resolved.

First dispute among litigants —
And when I eat, I shall not be foolish.
May God reward us, Abd Shams and Tofla,
With punishment of evil sooner rather than later.
He has a witness from within himself who provides no sustenance.

Bani Khalaf, the leaders of the structure and al-Ghaital,
And the family of Qusay in the first engagements,
And the pride of the lineage from Ghalib and al-Kahl —
They swore nothing but sparks among the tribes,
So they did not harm you, and no blood was shed.

Bu Hamah — Ubayd Fa'is ibn Aqil,
Bani Ummah, gathered — Hind Kiya.
We must shed all idleness and sloth,
And an arrow — a piercing arrow, twisted and swift.

Understanding where we stand in every small matter,
And Shayez was among Luay ibn Ghalib,
And the hardship is barefoot, from stomach to sandals —
So do not bring into your affairs every passerby.

"So you are the best of your people —
Be as Wa'il's gatherings were.
I fear that God will not set your affairs right,
And you have gone astray with a misguided command."

By my life, you have grown weak and helpless.
Hattab is more capable and more resolute,
And you were a woodcutter when he completed the task,
And he let us down and left us in the strongholds.

Why do the sons of Abd Manaf defy us?
Lean on all of you, if your inclination holds —
And the counsel of advisors tells us:
A provision I have never withheld from you for a single day,
And not a day of shortage, if I brought you a child.

I sense that the people have a plan,
Weighed with a premium that observes no ritual.
The dreams of a people have changed,
And we are the core of Hashim's wolf.

We had a watering basin among them,
And he repelled the evil of one who trod upon the pebbles.

<!-- page 119 -->

Dragging our elders through public forums —
By God, if I were not alive, bound by a covenant,
From a time long past, beyond mere mockery,
But we sold it nonetheless.

We have — and I do not mean to speak falsely —
They knew that our word is no lie;
They will make her fruitful only through a family.
If a people keep secret what you have done,

Then proclaim it to Qusay after us, with vigor —
So he told Qusay to spread our matter.
If we do not take refuge in their words at the entrances,
Or I struck at night — Qusay — a great blow.

But we grieved for the women left childless,
And if they trusted the strike within their homes,
Then it must be so in ignorance —
The heel of many peoples;
One day it must be removed.

And if you are a remnant of Luay, gathered,
They slaughtered us with ranges and terrors.
And we were sound before the darkening of the mat —
We found it, in our time, useless folly.

Every friend and nephew we count
Is absolved from the disabled and the deceitful,
Except one who is among the dogs of Ibn Murrah —
If he does not speak truth, he speaks falsely.

Bani Asad — do not be extremist in the dust;
Zuhayr Hasama is one of the bearers.
The uncle of the son of Ahat — the people do not lie —
In the reckoning of Houmat al-Muhad, is foremost.

I sense something stronger than the scent of the Bahalil belonging,
And contain it — the devoted lover who persists.
By my life, I have been committed to Ahmad,
And we are, despite the enemy, like ropes.

So beauty still abides in the world for its people,
If rulers measured it when distinguishing —
And who among the people is like him, for any hopeful soul?
He is loyal to her, and nothing about him is strange.

Forbearing, rightly guided, just — not reckless,
And Dina showed his right without it being withheld —
So the Lord of all servants supported him with His victory.

<!-- page 120 -->

To glory — honorable fathers of the gifted —
A youth from al-Muteen and Hashim,
Who rebukes every seller and the ignorant,
Like the bright gleam of swords in the hands of the refined.

Black predator over the mustard herd —
By striking, you see the boys within it as though
The people rise among them when they are transgressed,
And we found the descendants of the noble men —

It rises and rises within a few nights.
The people of the grudge will come to know which is which;
He finds, when the time comes to yield,
Who among me and them stands with his sword.

And he praises the horizons with the saying:
"And who is weary of war, from me and from them?"
The long poem is drawn shorter from it,
So Ahmad came among us in honor.

And he was defended with shield and clay;
I stood with him and protected him,
And what harm is upon him in this world and on the Day of Solidarity?
There is no doubt that God has elevated His command.

His father saw them as no worse than they were,
Just as yesterday and today his grandfather saw —
Honorable men other than their inclination.

We halted them until they scattered.

---

[Abu Talib's faith and his role in supporting the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him]

Abu Talib's statement is like that of the believer of Pharaoh's household, who concealed his faith. It suffices to recall what Abu Talib said and did, his struggle on behalf of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, his protection of the Prophet's family, his shielding of the Prophet from his enemies, his striving without respite, and his testimony to the Prophet's prophethood and truthfulness.

<!-- page 121 -->

This is because of the signs that had become clear to him, and of the virtues he had come to know. Had Abu Talib openly declared his Islam, he would have found no supporter among his people; he would have become merely one of those who accepted Islam while his people rejected it. But God, Almighty, supported His Messenger through him and established His religion by means of him.

When the religion began to appear openly, and the disbelievers, in God's sight, saw what God intended for His religion and the victory of His Messenger — Abu Talib's poetry spread among the Arabs. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, was supported by the rise of Bani Hashim through him, and through the rise of Bani Abd Shams and those loyal to them. Those Arabs who had been tempted away from the Messenger of Allah and his family were thereby checked; some of them submitted to him, while most of them denied what was said about him.

The people of Yathrib recalled what the Jews had been telling them since the time the Prophet appeared — that a Messenger had been sent, and their eyes turned to him and their hopes inclined toward him. The polytheists of Quraysh roused those who had promised them victory over the Messenger of Allah and his family, urging them to halt and to treat the matter as momentous — the sanctity of the Haram — and the poetry of Abu Talib reminded them of this. He sent some of them to address them in preaching, and the war with their people would bring them to an end, so what was in their hands was relinquished when their plot found no justification and their plan could not be carried out.

<!-- page 122 -->

[The poem of Abu Qays ibn al-Aslat warning the Quraysh against division]

Abu Qays ibn al-Aslat was a relative by marriage to the Quraysh; he had a wife, the daughter of a lion — Ibn Abd al-Ar'i — and he would bring her to Mecca and stay there for a period with the followers of her people. He had close ties with them, and he was a poet. He sent them a poem urging them to cease from war, exhorting them to follow the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, reminding them of what God had done for them and how He had turned away from them the army of the elephant and its cunning stratagem. He said:

Luay ibn Ghalib is not unaware of me —
Any traveler, whether he offered or reported.

Concerning the distant ones — they grieve that we stand
With the article of a man who has taken care of his own among you.

And I did not free myself of my needs and views of it,
And I had a groom burdened with worries.

She has the finest of endings and the woodcutter —
You silence two on every kiss.

And the evil of your deceit and the treading of scorpions —
May God protect you from the evil of your deeds.

The pain of the healers has struck it rightly,
And the morals are shown and the soundness of private counsel —
And the replacement of the sacred garment of the antelope.

So he reminded them of God at first glance —
War is departing from you in greetings.

And tell them, for God rules with His wisdom —
It is the dependence for the two allies or for the kin.

Hopes — send them, send them reproach,
And acquit the Sadeef from the hump and the stranger.
It severs a soul and destroys a nation —
Shella, and the echoes of the warrior's garb.

Or replace it with warming afterward,
As though the eyes of locusts had purchased it,
With musk and the dust of kagur.

<!-- page 123 -->

In its days, and knowledge is the knowledge of warfare,
And a basin and tents of water — the bitter stripes —
With the outcome of a loyal companion's mother.

The people among you are on the right path —
Do you consider it, or was there a war of wood?
Did you not know that there is no war
For the one of great provision, a guest who is not turned away?

How many a nobleman, Maswad, has been wounded by it,
And this is pure charisma — the cream of the deliberate.
Great are the embers of fire — he is praised,
The vitality of youth broadcast with him,

And the water of the rocky stream in the crags, as though —
Concerning war, in its advance and its aftermath,
He tells how many people are right about her —
Your reckoning, I swear, by the finest of reckoners.

So they sold the spears to a warrior and remembered:
You have a guardian other than the Lord of the piercings.
A man confused by a religion — it must not be so —
We have a purpose that the melts may guide.

Establish for us a true religion, for you
Are the faithful, and dreams are not isolated things.
And I pay tribute to these people — light and infallibility —
Bands guided by bands.

The seeker sees needs drawing toward your homes
In any case — the finest people of the brow.
The nations have known that your secrets
Are what I state truly in the midst of the processions,

And I favor it in my view, and above it is a year's standing
In the corners of this House among the trees.
So arise, single out your Lord and be anointed —
The day after Abu Yakhsum, the guide of the battalions.

You have a warning from him — believe it —
So do not worry, for war will not ensnare you;
It is adorned for people, and then they see it.

Burn — do not grill the weak — and step down.
If you do not know war, then ask,
And I shall be rewarded if people do not grasp the essence.

You have the navel of al-Batha', smelling of rabbits,
A polished lineage without flaw —
You preserve the ancient dignities of nobility.


<!-- page 124 -->
On the slingers at the heads of the virtues —
his battalion is easy to touch and his leg,
Jude al-Malik between Saf and Hasib.
When the victory of the Lord of the Throne came to you, he saw them:
to his family there is no one but Asaib.
Hurry, Harin — and he does not repent.
He lives by it, the saying of a man who is no liar.
If you perish, you will perish, and the seasons will perish.

[62] [Banu ʿAbd Shams and their tongues turned against the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him)]

When the Quraysh were discouraged from rallying the Arabs to support him, they refrained from speaking directly against the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) — resorting instead to harming him with their tongues, even as his uncle Abu Talib stood in his defense. Meanwhile, the Prophet called them to Allah (the Almighty), reciting to them the verses of His Book that had been revealed to him. Yet they persisted in their disbelief and denial, stubbornly rejecting the signs that were shown to them.

[A sermon of the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him]

The following is narrated from the Commander of the Faithful (peace be upon him) in one of his sermons. He said:(2)

"I was with him (may Allah bless him and his family) when the Quraysh came to him. They said: 'O Muhammad, you have made a great claim that neither your fathers nor any of your kinsmen ever made. We will put a matter to you, and if you show it to us and answer it, we will know that you are a prophet and messenger. If you cannot, we will know that you are a lying sorcerer.'

(1) An increase in virtues and defects [Folio 29/Z].
(2) See Nahj al-Balagha, 406–408.

<!-- page 125 -->
He said to them: 'What do you ask?'

They said: 'Call this tree to come to you, so that it uproots itself and stands before you.'

He said: 'Allah is Able over all things. If Allah does this for you, will you believe and bear witness to the truth?'

They said: Yes.

He said: 'I will show you what you ask, and I know full well [48/f] that you will not turn to what is good — for among you are those in whose hearts it has been sealed, and those who are partisan.'

Then he said: 'O tree, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day, and you know that I am the Messenger of Allah, then uproot yourself and stand before me.'

By Him who sent him with the truth, it uprooted itself with a great rushing sound,

[63]

thundering like the beating wings of a bird, until it stood in the hands of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family),(4) and flung its upper branch toward him, while one of its branches grazed my forehead — for I was standing at his right hand.

When the people saw this, they said: 'Arrogance and sorcery!'

They asked that half of it return. He commanded it, and half of it came back as though it had never moved. Then it drew closer still until it nearly encircled the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family), and they said:

(1) "2" + "B": "I" — and what has been established is from Nahj al-Balagha.
(2) A guide from Nahj al-Balagha.
(3) "2" + "B": "Then he said to the tree" — and what was established is from Nahj al-Balagha.
(4) An increase in Nahj al-Balagha.
(5) An increase in Nahj al-Balagha.

<!-- page 126 -->
Command it to return the other half as well. So he commanded it (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family), and it returned.

I said: 'There is no god but Allah. I am the first to believe in you, O Messenger of Allah, and the first to acknowledge that the tree did what it did by the command of Allah (exalted be He), as a testament to your prophethood and in reverence [48/z] for your word.'

Yet all the people said: 'He is a lying sorcerer — and sorcery is well-suited to his kind. Is there no one who will believe you [in your claim](1) except someone like this?' — meaning me.

And I am among those people whom you do not blame regarding Allah, especially the righteous — those whose words are the words of the righteous, who spend their nights in worship and their days in journey, holding fast to the rope of the Quran. They follow the Sunnah of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger; they are neither arrogant nor conceited nor haughty. Their hearts are tender and their bodies are at work!"

This is the saying of the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him.

[The Prophet's message spreads among the tribes]

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) showed them mighty signs — among them the splitting of the moon, and other things beyond the power of all humankind — yet they only grew more arrogant,

(1) An increase in Nahj al-Balagha.
(2) An increase from "B".

<!-- page 127 -->
and their denial and disbelief only deepened.

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) reached out to those in the Arab lands — the Jews, the Christians, their scholars, monks, and rabbis — and they came to him to hear his arguments. Some of them recognized in him the evidence of prophethood from what they found in their books: they submitted to him and to what pointed to his nature,(1) [49/f] and their knowledge of him. Yet when they returned,(2) most of them spoke of him only under the shadow of envy and enmity, which prevented them from acknowledging his prophethood (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family).

[Abu Talib warns the Quraysh]

Islam spread and news of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) became known across the horizons, and hearts inclined toward it. Many things happened in this period that would take long to recount. Abu Talib warned the Quraysh sternly against their denial of him and said, in part:

Wake up, O Banu Ghalib, and be forewarned —
outstanding men from your own house will face you.
Otherwise, I fear what may come upon you.
By the Lord of the West and the East,
let your forebears be a lesson to you:
Thamud and ʿAd — who remains of them
after they tasted what came before you?
The she-camel of the Throne when she was watered —
the next day they hamstrung her.

(1) "B": "Falsehood".
(2) An increase from "B".
(3) See: Madwana 88–89.

<!-- page 128 -->
From Allah came a blow from the blue,
Hasama from India — the Rotaq.
So wrath descended upon them.
The next day he severed her hock —
wonders in the desert laid bare —
for those who are patient, truthful, and pious.
And I am more awed by that than you,
by the hand of one who has risen through hardship
in spite of this foolish opposition.
So Allah held him in His palm:
He dissolved the seduction and they would not believe it —
I will outfox your obstinate one when he is beguiled.

[What happened to Abu Jahl when he threw a stone at the Prophet]

It is related by "the Sticky Stone":(1) "When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) had made his command firm and openly proclaimed the worship of his Lord, he would come to the House and pray there. Abu Jahl ibn Hisham al-Makhzumi came upon him one day while he was praying, stood up,(2) and seized a stone, filling both hands with it. He advanced toward the Messenger (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) as he was prostrating,

[65]

intending to strike his head — all this after the disbelievers of the Quraysh had incited him against him, and they sat watching to see what he would do. But when he drew close to the Prophet while he was prostrating, he recoiled, his face drained of color, and he cast the stone away from his hand. The people stood up to him and said: 'What is the matter with you, O Abu al-Hakam?'

He said: 'When I drew near him, a great stallion camel loomed before me, opening its mouth — and I fled from it.' They said to one another: 'By Allah, Muhammad has been safeguarded against us, as no man among a people before him was ever safeguarded.'"

(1) See: Virtues and Defects [Folios 29/Z–30f].
(2) "2": "So he advanced" — and what was established is from "B", which accords with what is in Virtues and Defects.
(3) An increase in Virtues and Defects.

<!-- page 129 -->
[The migration to the land of Abyssinia]

The Quraysh saw that the more they pressed those who had followed him, the more determined those people became. So they made every clan(1) torment the Muslims among them: they tortured and beat them. The Muslims complained of this to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family), and he gave them permission to migrate to the land of Abyssinia.

A group of them set out to emigrate [50/f] to Abyssinia, and among them was Jaʿfar ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him). The number of those who emigrated(2) — Muslims(3) — to Abyssinia was eighty-three men, not counting the women and children who accompanied them.

[Abu Talib's letter to the Negus, urging him to protect the emigrants]

Abu Talib sent a letter with his son Jaʿfar al-Tayyar (peace be upon him) to al-Najashi, the king of Abyssinia, informing him of the state of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family), of the hostility of the Banu ʿAbd Shams and their Qurayshi allies against him, of the hardships his followers had suffered at their hands, of what had been taken from them and what of their wealth had been withheld — and asking al-Najashi to shelter and protect them. He wrote to him:(4)

Know — may I be your ransom — that Muhammad is the counterpart of Moses and the Messiah, son of Mary.

(1) An increase in Virtues and Defects.
(2) An increase in Virtues and Defects.
(3) An increase in Virtues and Defects.
(4) See: Diwana 259.

<!-- page 130 -->
By the command of Allah he guides and is infallible —
truly, a hadith for the hadith of translation.
Please receive them with kindness;
the path of truth is never dark.
That is: by a guidance like that which has been brought,
and which you recite in your books.
Do not let a faction from among us
turn you from calling to Allah — and receive them well.

[66]

Jaʿfar (peace be upon him) delivered his father's letter to the Negus, and this was among the reasons why the Muslims received righteousness and generosity from him.

[The Quraysh send messengers to the Negus to reclaim the emigrants]

When the Quraysh saw the honor(1) that the companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) had received — that they had settled and found peace in Abyssinia, and that the Negus had given them the finest lodgings near him — they agreed to select two men known for shrewdness and eloquence and send them to the Negus. They chose ʿAmr ibn al-ʿAs and ʿAmmara ibn al-Walid, gathered a great deal of wealth, purchased many gifts from the Hijaz, and sent them along to the king of Abyssinia and to all of his courtiers and advisors.

When the gifts had been received, the patriarchs were given their share(2) and went in to the Negus, to whom the messengers had been sent. The patriarchs mentioned them to the Negus, and he brought them in. They said to him: "O king, there have come into your land foolish slaves from among us who have abandoned

(1) A: "Generosity" — and what is established is from "B", which accords with what is in Virtues and Defects.
(2) An increase from "B".
(3) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 131 -->
the religion of their people and have not entered into your religion either — instead they have invented a new teaching that neither you nor we recognize. The nobles of our people, including their fathers, uncles, and clan leaders, have sent us to you to have them returned."

The patriarchs then said, once their gifts had reached them: "Truly, O [51/f] king — their own people know them best. Return them to those who sent for them."

The king was angered and said: "I will not hand over to them a people who have come near me and chosen me over all others, until I invite them and hear what they have to say."

[Al-Najashi brings the emigrants before him, questions them about their religion, and receives their answer]

Al-Najashi sent for the companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family). When they came before him, he summoned the bishops, who spread their scriptures around him, and then he asked the companions of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him): "What is this religion for which you have abandoned your own people, and yet have not entered into our religion or any other known faith?"

Jaʿfar ibn Abi Talib spoke on their behalf. He said to him: "O king, we were a people of ignorance: we worshipped idols, ate carrion, committed immorality, severed the bonds of kinship, and wronged our neighbors — the strong among us devoured the weak. We remained in this state until Allah sent us a messenger whose lineage, truthfulness, trustworthiness, and chastity were well known to us. He called us to monotheism — to worship Allah alone, to abandon what we and our fathers had worshipped alongside Him of stones and idols — and he commanded us to speak truthfully, to fulfill our trusts, to maintain kinship ties, to be good neighbors, and to refrain from

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 336/1.
(2) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 132 -->
[51/z] unlawful intercourse and bloodshed; to shun immorality, falsehood, and the consuming of orphans' wealth and of that which is fortified against us; and He commanded us to worship Allah alone,(1) associating nothing with Him. He commanded us to pray, give zakat, and fast, and he laid out for us the obligations of Islam.

So we believed in him and followed him in what he brought us from Allah. We did not associate anything with Him;(2) we forbade what He forbade us, and made lawful what He made lawful for us. Then our people turned against us,(3) punished us, and tried to seduce us away from our religion — to draw us back from the worship of Allah to the worship of idols, and to make permissible for us again the evils we used to commit.(4) When they overpowered us(5) and wronged us and placed themselves between us and our religion, we departed to your country. We chose you above all others, sought your protection, and hoped that we would suffer no injustice in your presence, O king."

Al-Najashi said to him: "Do you have anything of what he has brought from Allah?" Jaʿfar ibn Abi Talib said: "Yes." Al-Najashi said: "Then read it to me." So he recited a passage from

(Maryam: 1).

The Negus and his bishops wept until their beards were wet.(6)

He said: "This and what Jesus brought have come from the same lamp."

(1) An increase in the biography of the Prophet.
(2) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(3) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(4) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(5) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(6) An increase from "B".


<!-- page 133 -->
When they left his presence, 'Amr ibn al-'As said: "Tomorrow I will bring him something against them that will uproot them."

The next day he returned to the Negus and said: "They make a grievous claim about 'Isa ibn Maryam." The Negus sent for the Muslims and asked them: "What do you say about 'Isa ibn Maryam?" They gathered together, [68] and said to one another: "What shall we say?"

Ja'far ibn Abi Talib spoke on their behalf and said: "We say that he is what our Prophet brought to us — a slave of God, His Messenger, His Spirit, and His Word, which He conveyed to the Virgin Mary."

At that, the Negus struck the ground with his hand, picked up a stick from it, and declared: "What separates 'Isa ibn Maryam from what you have just said is no more than this stick."
