And from Banu 'Amr ibn 'Awf ibn Malik ibn al-Aws: 'Uwaym ibn Sa'idah.

[The Messenger of Allah Sent a Teacher with the Delegation of al-Aqaba]

When the delegation departed from the Messenger of Allah, he sent with them Mus'ab ibn 'Umayr ibn Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf ibn 'Abd al-Dar ibn Qusai. He instructed him to recite the Quran to them, to teach them Islam, and to instruct them in the religion. He thus became the first to be called one of the "reciters" in Medina. He would lead the Ansar in prayer, call them to Allah and His Messenger, and it was through his hands that Sa'd ibn Mu'adh and Usayd ibn Hudayr came to Islam — and through them, all of Banu 'Abd al-Ashhal. After their conversion, not a single man or woman remained in the household of Banu 'Abd al-Ashhal [72/z] except as a Muslim.

[Mention of the Second Pledge at al-Aqaba]

Mus'ab ibn 'Umayr then returned to Mecca, and the Ansar who had become Muslim set out for the pilgrimage season along with the polytheists of their people. The Messenger of Allah appointed a meeting with them at al-'Aqaba during the middle of the days of Tashreeq, for he desired his dignity, the victory of his Prophet, the honor of Islam and its people, and the humiliation

(1) Addition from the Prophet's biography 433/2.
(2) Addition from the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 175 -->
of shirk and its people.

With the Messenger of Allah were 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, his uncle Hamza, and al-'Abbas — the sons of 'Abd al-Muttalib. Al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib said to the gathering: "O assembly of the Khazraj, Muhammad is from us, as you well know, and we have protected him from our people. He lives in honor among his people and in security in his land. He has, however, resolved to join you and attach himself to you. If you are prepared to be faithful to what you promise and to stand firm against those who oppose him, then so be it — but if you think you may abandon him and surrender him after he has come to you, then leave him now, for he is in a position of strength and protection among his people and his country."

The Ansar replied: "We have heard what you said. Speak now, O Messenger of Allah, and take for yourself what you wish."

[The Prophet's Covenant with the Ansar]

The Messenger of Allah spoke, recited the Quran, and called to Allah; then he said: "I take your pledge on the condition that you protect me from what you protect your women and children." Al-Bara' ibn Ma'rur — one of the masters of the Ansar — took his hand and said: "Yes, by the One who sent you with the truth — we will protect you from all that we protect ourselves from. So take our pledge, O Messenger of Allah. By God, we are the sons of war and the people of the shield; we have inherited that from one generation to the next."

They then pledged allegiance to the Messenger of Allah at al-'Aqaba on the terms of Islam: that they would protect him as they protected their women and children, and that they would stand firmly by him.

<!-- page 176 -->
[The Twelve Chieftains of al-Aqaba]

The Messenger of Allah appointed twelve chieftains from among them. They were: As'ad ibn Zurara, 'Abdullah ibn Rawaha, Sa'd ibn al-Rabi', Rafi' ibn Malik, al-Bara' ibn Ma'rur, 'Abdullah ibn 'Amr <ibn Haram> (1), 'Ubadah ibn al-Samit, Sa'd ibn 'Ubadah, al-Mundhir [73/z] ibn 'Amr, Usayd ibn Hudayr, Sa'd ibn Khayithama, and Abu al-Haytham ibn al-Tayhan.

He said to the chieftains: "You are the guarantors over your people — as the disciples of 'Isa ibn Maryam were guarantors for him — and I am the guarantor for those among my own people who have believed in me." They said: "Yes, O Messenger of Allah." Then they set out for the city.

[The Poetry of Ka'b ibn Malik in Praise of the Chieftains]

Ka'b ibn Malik recited the following, remembering them and addressing the polytheists of Quraysh:

Tomorrow has come upon the people and is now a reality —
He told my father that good sense had slipped away from him.
Keeping watch over the affairs of the people, alert and attentive —
O God, do not let yourself be destroyed;
With Ahmad, a light from Allah's guidance shines forth.
He told Abu Sufyan that he had appeared before us —
You cannot crowd out something that has taken its place.
Gather whatever force you are able to gather.
Father, you will prove a fool if you pursue this path,
Know well that we have broken our covenants with you.

(1) Addition from "B."
(2) See: ibid., 178–179.

<!-- page 177 -->
For your nose, if you dare attempt that brave act —
[74/f]
Its venom seeps in without a remedy.
As for Ibn Rawaha — he will not yield it to him,
But will honor it and raise its standing,
With a Muslim who neither covets nor is greedy.
And what of Ibn al-Rabi' when you deal with his covenant?
He strives to fulfill what he has bound himself to —
Meeting every pledge he has given.
Abu al-Haytham also fulfilled his pledge in full —
Are you so foolish as to think of nullifying this dispute?
And what of Ibn Hudayr when you come against him —
Wounds, when I tested him, did not weigh on me.
And Sa'd, the brother of 'Amr ibn 'Awf, is
One upon whom the night closes in around you.
Your foremost ones — do not tire yourself of them.
His father al-Bara' and Ibn 'Amr, both of them —
Awsad, his father al-Sa'di, and al-Mundhir —
Fulfillment personified — and al-Qawqali ibn Samit.

<!-- page 178 -->
[The Migration of the Prophet and His Companions]

Islam had by now taken firm root among the Ansar, and the Prophet granted his companions permission to migrate to Medina. They emigrated in groups, settling in the homes of the Ansar, who sheltered them, welcomed them, shared their dwellings with them, and gave them preference over themselves. It was concerning them that Allah revealed: *those who had settled in the city and embraced faith before them love those who emigrated to them, finding in their own hearts no desire for what has been given to the emigrants, but preferring them over themselves even when they are in need — whoever is saved from the greed of his own soul, it is they who are the prosperous* [al-Hashr: 9].

[Mention of the Quraysh's Gathering in Dar al-Nadwa]

The Messenger of Allah remained in Mecca after his companions had emigrated; no one was left behind in Mecca except those who were imprisoned or otherwise unable to leave, apart from his cousin 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) and Abu Bakr ibn Abi Quhafa.

When the disbelievers of Quraysh saw this, they realized that the Messenger of Allah had gained a party and supporters, and they feared that his authority would grow formidable against them. They assembled in the house of the council — Dar al-Nadwa. Present among them, according to some accounts, was the Devil — may Allah curse him — in the form of an elderly shaykh. They were suspicious of him and asked: "Who are you?" He said: "I am a man from the people of Najd. I have been informed of what you have assembled to discuss and I have come to attend with you; you will not lose any counsel from me that may benefit you."

They conferred and said: "The people of Yathrib have believed Muhammad, and they have drawn to themselves those who have come to them."

(1) Addition from the accounts of virtues and defects.


<!-- page 179 -->
The companions had migrated to join him, and the Quraysh feared that his standing among them would grow, that his authority would increase, and that his supporters would multiply. They worried that he would fight them with those who had already followed him, and they doubted that the Arabs would resist him the way the people of Yathrib had not. So they asked one another: What should be done?

Some of them said, "Arrest him, bind him, and throw him in prison." Shaykh al-Nahdi said, "What kind of opinion is that? If you do so, his people will come to free him — you know them well." They agreed the Shaykh was right.

Another said, "Banish him from our midst — let him go wherever he will, and we shall have peace." Shaykh al-Nahdi said, "That would only scatter his influence further and set you to chasing after him."

Then another said, "We kill him." The Shaykh said, "Now that is the best counsel you have offered. But here is how it must be done: take one man from each clan and arm each with a sword. Let them strike him together as one, so that his blood falls on every branch of Quraysh alike. Then the Banu Hashim cannot demand satisfaction from any single tribe — the blood-guilt would be too diffuse to pursue." They said, "This is the plan." They agreed upon it and readied themselves for the night they would come to him.

**[The Prophet's departure and 'Ali's vigil in his bed]**

Jibril came to the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and informed him of their plot. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) then instructed his cousin 'Ali (peace be upon him) — having told him what the Quraysh had conspired — to wrap himself in the Prophet's cloak and sleep in his bed, so that anyone who came would see what appeared to be the Prophet still at rest. He informed 'Ali of his emigration, told him to remain behind with the family, and charged him to

<!-- page 180 -->
discharge the trusts and debts that people had left with him, and then to follow him afterward.

He also instructed 'Ali to remain composed even as the men surrounded the house. Outside, someone was saying to the crowd: "Muhammad claims that if you follow him you will rule the Arabs and the non-Arabs alike, and that when you die you will enter a garden like the Garden of Jordan — but if you refuse and he kills you, you go to a fire and burn in it. So hasten and be done with it."

When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) heard the commotion outside, he commanded 'Ali (peace be upon him) to lie down on his bed and drew the green Hadrami mantle — the one in which he slept — over him. Then he recited Surah Ya-Sin, took a handful of dust, scattered it in the faces of those who had gathered, and walked out among them unseen, for Allah had veiled their sight from him.

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) then went to the cave, after first arranging with Abu Bakr, 'Amir ibn Fahira, and 'Abdullah ibn Arqat to accompany him and guide him to Medina, carrying what he needed for the journey.

Meanwhile, those watching the house peered through the open door and saw a figure lying in the Prophet's bed, wrapped in his cold cloak. They had no doubt it was him. When the full group had assembled and steeled themselves to act, a man who had not been with them from the start came and said, "What are you waiting for here?"

They said, "We mean to kill Muhammad."

He said, "Allah has confounded you! By Allah, Muhammad has already passed out among you — not one of those present when he left has been spared seeing him — and the dust has already settled on your heads."

<!-- page 181 -->
They reached up and found dust on the heads of most of them. Then they looked toward 'Ali (peace be upon him) through the gap of the door and saw him lying there in the Prophet's cloak, as if he were the Messenger of Allah himself at rest. They said, "That is Muhammad." They burst in with their swords, and when 'Ali (peace be upon him) sensed them he seized the swords from their hands. When they saw who it was and recognized him, they held back and said, "We have no quarrel with you, O son of Abi Talib." And they said among themselves, "Killing him would bring us nothing but blood-feud." So they left.

This was the first act of jihad that 'Ali (peace be upon him) carried out in the service of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) — offering himself as a shield against death without the Prophet present, standing in the face of a large company of men who sought the Prophet's life, doing so at a young age and in the earliest days of his standing in Islam.

**[The descent of Gabriel and Mika'il to guard 'Ali from all harm]**

Muhammad ibn 'Isa al-Nakha'i narrated with his chain of transmission from Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali ibn al-Husayn (peace be upon him), who said:

"When Allah Most High commanded His Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) to emigrate, and informed him of what the polytheists had conspired in order to kill him, He commanded 'Ali (peace be upon him) to lie down in his bed — and he did so. Then Allah the Exalted revealed to Gabriel and Mika'il (peace be upon them): 'I have established a brotherhood between you, and I shall take the soul of one of you. Choose

<!-- page 182 -->
which of you it shall be.' Both loved life and were reluctant to face death. So Allah the Exalted revealed to them: 'Neither of you shows the same selfless devotion to his companion that 'Ali has shown to Muhammad. Go, therefore, and guard him from every harm of My enemy until dawn.' "

So they descended, and one sat at his head and the other at his feet, and they said, "Well done, O 'Ali — the beloved one who gives himself as ransom."

**['Ali's vigil in the cave]**

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) entered the cave, and Abu Bakr entered with him. The polytheists searched for him until they reached the cave's mouth, but Allah turned them away. Abu Bakr grew frightened when the searchers drew near the entrance, and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) calmed his fear and reassured him of what Allah had promised regarding His victory and the completion of His command.

This episode is cited by some as a virtue of Abu Bakr — that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) took him as a companion in the cave. The scholars of the Shia, however, have said that it points more toward a failing than a virtue, for he did not bear the situation with patience alongside the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), nor did he place trust in what Allah had promised to send down. Rather, he was distressed and anxious. And Allah Most High rebuked him when He said: *"When he said to his companion, 'Do not grieve, for Allah is with us'"* — and then *"Allah sent down His tranquility upon him and supported him with soldiers you could not see"* [al-Tawba: 40]. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family were the ones upon whom tranquility truly rested.

If the honor of being granted tranquility by Allah were a virtue belonging to Abu Bakr, as it is to the believers in

<!-- page 183 -->
another verse — where Allah says: *"Then Allah sent down His tranquility upon His Messenger and upon the believers, and bound them to the word of piety, for they were most deserving of it and worthy of it"* [al-Fath: 26] — then mere companionship proves nothing on its own. A believer may travel alongside a disbeliever, a righteous man alongside a sinner. Consider what Allah Most High has said: *"His companion said to him, while he was speaking with him, 'Do you disbelieve in the One who created you from dust, then from a drop of fluid, then fashioned you into a man? But as for me — He is Allah, my Lord, and I associate no one with my Lord'"* [al-Kahf: 37–38].

The true distinction belongs to the one who ransomed the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) with his own life — who slept in his bed and made himself a willing sacrifice for the sake of Allah, and waged jihad in the path of the Prophet while the Prophet was absent from him. The discussion on this point is long and wide-ranging, but we have aimed for brevity.

**[The first year of the Prophet's Hijra]**

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) then migrated to Medina. He entered it on a Monday, twelve nights into the month of Rabi' al-Awwal. He was fifty-three years of age when he entered Medina, having resided in Mecca for thirteen years after Allah sent him on a Monday, when he was forty years old.

'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) remained in Mecca for three days after the Prophet's departure, fulfilling his obligations, then followed him to Medina and descended with him at the home of Kulthum ibn Haddam in Quba'. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) did not set out until 'Ali (peace be upon him) arrived, which was one or two nights after his arrival. The Prophet's stay there

<!-- page 184 -->
was on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday. Then on Friday he led the prayer at Bani Salim ibn 'Awf — that was the first Friday prayer he led in Medina.

**[The tribes offered him hospitality and wished him to stay among them]**

As he traveled, groups of men would gather around him and say, "O Messenger of Allah, stay with us — we are many and strong." They would try to halt his camel, and he would say, "Leave her and do not hold her back — she is under command." So they would step aside and she would move on.

Whenever he passed by the Ansar, they invited him to stop, and they would seize the camel's halter. He would say, "Let her go — she is commanded."

She went on until she arrived at the area of Bani Malik ibn al-Najjar, and there she knelt at the door of what is now his mosque — a plot that belonged to two young orphan slaves of Banu al-Najjar, kept as a threshing floor in the stonefield of Mu'adh ibn 'Afra'. When she knelt there, the Prophet of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) did not dismount from her back immediately. She rose and walked a short distance, then he turned her around and she came back to the spot where she had first knelt, lowered herself there once more, settled firmly, and he dismounted from her.

<!-- page 185 -->
The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) ordered that his belongings be carried into the house of Khalid ibn Zayd — that is, Abu Ayyub — and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) lodged with him until the mosque was finished. He then asked about the plot of land where the camel had knelt, learned whose it was, and bought it from the orphans.

**[The building of the Medina mosque]**

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) ordered the mosque to be built on that very site. He himself worked with his own hands, and the Muhajirun and the Ansar worked alongside him. One of the Muslims composed these verses:

*If we sit while the one who works toils on,*
*then we have no part in the work of prayer.*

And the Muslims labored cheerfully as they built, chanting:

*There is no life but the life of the Hereafter —*
*O Allah, have mercy on the Ansar and the Muhajirun.*

And the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) took up the words and said: "There is no true life but the life of the Hereafter. O Allah, have mercy on the emigrants and the Ansar."

**['Ali's devotion in building the mosque]**

'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) and his companions were the most diligent of all those who worked. He labored on and chanted:

<!-- page 186 -->
*The one who builds mosques and toils within them,*
*standing and sitting alike, is not the same*
*as one who merely passes by, turning aside from the dust —*
*while the dust still clings, barren and unsettled.*

Then 'Ammar ibn Yasir (may Allah be pleased with him) took up the chant and worked with great energy alongside him, exerting himself tremendously.

'Uthman ibn 'Affan stood to one side. When he saw what was being offered of him in terms of labor, he said to 'Ammar: "O son of Sumayyah, I will press this stick against your nose!" — and he had a stick in his hand.

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) heard 'Uthman's words and was angered. He said: "Their wealth calls them to Paradise, and they call him to Hell." And he said (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family): "'Ammar's place is between my eyes and my nose" — meaning that 'Ammar was as dear to him as his own face. "These are your companions — go with them if you wish."

Work on the mosque of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) continued until the building was complete. He then ordered his houses to be built nearby, and his companions settled around his mosque. Each of them was given a door opening into the mosque, and they lived this way for years — in the house of every one of the companions of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) was a door leading into the mosque. Then Allah Most High revealed to His Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) that he should command them to close their doors — all except the door of 'Ali (peace be upon him).


<!-- page 187 -->
[The Best Virtues of the Commander of the Faithful — peace be upon him]

It has been narrated that some people came to the Commander of the Faithful (peace be upon him) and said: "O Commander of the Faithful, tell us of your greatest virtues." He said:

"I prefer my distinction to be one I did not make for myself." They asked: "And what is that, O Commander of the Faithful?"

He said: "When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) arrived in Madinah, he ordered the construction of the mosque. No one was left who had not been assigned a door opening into it — until Gabriel came and commanded him to close all those doors except mine. So he sent a messenger to them. He sent Mu'adh ibn Jabal to Abu Bakr and ordered him to block his door. Abu Bakr said, 'I hear and obey,' and his door was closed. Then he sent word to 'Umar and ordered him to close his door. 'Umar came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and said: 'O Messenger of Allah, let me keep it open just enough that I may look at you with my eyes.' The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) refused. So his door was closed. Then he sent word to Talhah, al-Zubayr, 'Uthman, 'Abd al-Rahman, Sa'd, Hamzah, and al-'Abbas, ordering them all to close their doors, and they heard and obeyed. Yet Hamzah and some others said: 'He orders us to close our doors while he leaves the door of Ali open.'

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: 'I have been told what you said about the closing of the doors. By Allah, I did not do this of my own accord — Allah did it. Allah revealed to Moses to take a house of purification in which none would dwell except him, Aaron, and his sons.'"

(1) See: Pillars of Islam 16/1–17, Explanation 150, in which it says: "In the book of Hammad ibn 'Issa, Hurayz ibn 'Abd Allah narrated from Zurara ibn A'yan, from Abu 'Abd (peace be upon him) that he said:

<!-- page 188 -->
[Abu Ja'far said:] He meant that no one else was to enter into it, and Allah revealed to me likewise: to take this house as pure, so that none may dwell in it save me, Ali, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn. By Allah, I did not order your doors closed, nor did I leave Ali's door open — it was Allah who commanded me to do so."

This account has been summarized from a longer narration, and it has been transmitted with a chain of narrators through well-established channels.

[The First Sermon Delivered by the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him]

The first sermon delivered by the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was at the first Friday prayer gathered in Madinah. As Ibn Hisham narrated, he praised Allah and extolled Him as He deserves, then said:

"As for what follows — O people, send forth provision for yourselves. By Allah, one of you will surely be struck unconscious with no shepherd and no interpreter and no intercessor between him and his Lord. Then his Lord will say to him: 'Did My Messenger not come to you and convey the message? I gave you wealth and favored you — what have you put forward for yourself?' He will look to his right and see nothing, then to his left and see nothing, then before him and see nothing but the Fire. Whoever can shield his face from the Fire, even with half a date, let him do so. And whoever cannot find even that, let him do so with a good word, for a good deed is rewarded ten times over —"

(1) Added for clarity.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography.
(3) An addition from the Prophet's biography.
(4) See: Biography of the Prophet, 2/500–501.
(5) An addition from the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 189 -->
"— up to seven hundred times over. And peace be upon you, and the mercy and blessings of Allah."

[His Second Sermon — may Allah bless him and his family]

He (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then delivered another sermon, in which he said:

"In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

Praise be to Allah — we praise Him, seek His help, and take refuge in Allah from the evils of our own souls and the evils of our deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leaves to stray, none can guide. I testify that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. The finest of speech is the Book of Allah — blessed and exalted is He. Whoever Allah has adorned with it in his heart, and who has entered Islam after disbelief, choosing it over all other discourse, has found the best of guidance. Love what Allah loves, and love Allah with all your hearts. Do not grow weary of the word of Allah and His remembrance, and do not let your hearts harden against Him, for Allah selects from all things the best of deeds, the best of servants, and the most righteous of speech. Observe what Allah has made lawful and forbidden for people; worship Allah and associate nothing with Him; fear Allah as He truly deserves to be feared; be sincere to Allah in what you profess with your mouths; love one another in the spirit of Allah. Allah is angered when His covenant is broken. And peace be upon you."

[Brotherhood Between the Muhajireen and the Ansar]

The Messenger of Allah (may peace be upon him) concluded a pact with the Jews, made peace between the Aws and the Khazraj, and abolished the blood feuds of the pre-Islamic era.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 2/501.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 190 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then established bonds of brotherhood among his companions. He made Hamzah (peace be upon him) and Zayd ibn Haritha brothers, and Ja'far ibn Abi Talib — who was at that time in the land of Abyssinia — was paired with Mu'adh ibn Jabal. He made Abu Bakr and 'Umar brothers, and in the same way established brotherhood between all of his companions, pairing each man with another.

Then he took the hand of Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) and said: "This is my brother, my guardian and the fulfillment of my pledge, and my successor after me."

[The Verses of Sarmah ibn Abi Anas]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had settled, and Allah's religion had spread and taken root in the city of his dwelling, Abu Fais Sarmah ibn Abi Anas — a brother of the Banu 'Adi ibn al-Najjar — composed verses commemorating the honor Allah had bestowed upon his people through Islam, and the distinction of having the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) descend among them:

He recalls when he found a true and loyal friend —
how few such supporters were among Quraysh.
He found none to shelter him and none to heed his call,
though he declared himself openly before the people of the seasons.
He arrived content, and we received him gladly —
when he came to us, Allah made His religion manifest.
He was granted Allah's help through our hands,
and found two true friends; the city was set at peace by him.
He tells us what Moses said when he answered the caller,
and what Noah said to his people —
swift in proclamation, fearless of those far away,
until he was no longer reckoned among the rejected.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet (peace be upon him), 2/512.

<!-- page 191 -->
We gave him our wealth and our very selves in times of hardship and adversity, knowing that there is no god but Allah — and you know that Allah is the best of guides.

All of them — even the most beloved — if you bear enmity toward what people hold as ordinary, you have come to your calling.

I say, when I call out to you in every pledge of allegiance:

Do not expose yourself to your enemies.
I say, when you cross fearsome ground
and have no one to guard you —
he treads an open plain whose edges spread wide;
had Allah not appointed him a protector,
the young man would not know how to remain pious.
If you become arrogant and turn rebellious,
like the palm trees that defy their Lord —

[The Account of Salman al-Farsi]

Salman al-Farsi — may Allah be pleased with him — reached the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and embraced Islam. He was from Persia. He had come to love the Christian religion and abandoned his father's faith, for his father followed the religion of the Magi. He traveled with caravans to the Levant and served righteous monks one after another. The last of them, as he was dying, counseled him and said: "You will find that a prophet sent by Allah — the best of prophets — whose time has now come is near at hand; he accepts gifts but does not accept charity, and he bears the seal of prophethood between his shoulders."

Salman (may Allah be pleased with him) had been sold by compulsion into the ownership of a Jewish man. He thus came to the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and put him to the test: he brought him charity, but the Prophet did not eat from it and distributed it among his companions. Then Salman brought him a gift,

(1) An addition from manuscript "B".

<!-- page 192 -->
and the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) ate from it and shared it with them. Salman then looked at the seal of prophethood between his shoulders, recognized the signs, and submitted to Islam.

Salman went on to become one of the closest companions to the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). The Prophet would greet him warmly, draw him near, and speak highly of him. He said of him: "Salman is of us, the Ahl al-Bayt." He would pray for him and send him greetings.

Salman was among the Shi'a of Ali (peace be upon him). Ali (peace be upon him) had followers during the lifetime of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) who were known for their belief in his guardianship and were celebrated for it. Among them were: Salman al-Farsi, 'Ammar, Abu Dhar, al-Miqdad, 'Abd Allah ibn Mas'ud, 'Abd Allah ibn 'Abbas, and others. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) would remind people of their virtue and call them the Shi'a of Ali.

This is a well-known matter, familiar to both the learned and the general public, and the narrations that have come down concerning it are too numerous to be contained within this book.

[The Appearance of the Hypocrites]

Many Jews converted to Islam, as did the remaining members of the Aws and Khazraj — some of them sincerely, while others concealed hypocrisy when they saw that Islam had grown strong and was taking hold.

(1) An addition from manuscript "B".
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), 3/224.
(3) An addition from manuscript "B".

<!-- page 193 -->
Among the hypocrites were 'Abd Allah ibn Ubayy ibn Salul and others — many in number and well known by name. They would say one thing to the believers and another among themselves: ⟪ar:وَإِذَا لَقُوا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا قَالُوا آمَنَّا وَإِذَا خَلَوْا إِلَىٰ شَيَاطِينِهِمْ قَالُوا إِنَّا مَعَكُمْ إِنَّمَا نَحْنُ مُسْتَهْزِئُونَ⟫ [al-Baqarah: 14]. The Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) accepted from them what they outwardly displayed of Islam and left them to themselves, because verses had been revealed exposing their hypocrisy and making clear that even if they said they believed with their mouths, their hearts had not believed [al-Ma'idah: 41].

The Ansar said: "We used to recognize the hypocrites at the time of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) by their hatred of Ali ibn Abi Talib — peace be upon him." This is further confirmed by the Prophet's own words to Ali (peace be upon him): "No one hates you, O Ali, except a hypocrite" — or, in some narrations: "except one of illegitimate birth," or "except one whose mother conceived him while she was menstruating," or "except one who was conceived in transgression."

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had established himself in Madinah, news reached the polytheists of Quraysh of the unity between the Muhajireen and the Ansar and the growing strength of the Muslim community. When the religion became manifest, this filled them with revulsion and overwhelming grief. They yearned for the death of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), yet found themselves powerless to bring it about.

[Hamzah's Secret Mission to the Shore of the Sea]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) dispatched his uncle Hamzah (may Allah be pleased with him)

<!-- page 194 -->
to the coastal region near al-'Ais with thirty riders from among the Muhajireen. He established a covenant with the people there. The first banner ever raised in Islam was that of Hamzah ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib, the uncle of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

When the news reached Quraysh, they dispatched three hundred riders against him under the command of Abu Jahl ibn Hisham. The two forces nearly met in battle, but Majdi ibn 'Amr al-Juhani stepped between them as an intermediary and the two sides parted without fighting. Hamzah (peace be upon him) and those who accompanied him from among the Muhajireen withdrew.

Hamzah (may Allah be pleased with him) said in verse:

Because of the weakness of men's judgment and their reason —
are they not my people who dream idle dreams?
They hold a sanctity from fasting or from people,
and the Rakina with her grievances has not taken a step.
They possess nothing but an order of chastity and justice;
it is as though we drench them yet they drench us not.
And what descends from them is like mockery —
they have been commanded to accept Islam and refused.
For them, wherever comfort and ease beckon, they seek it;
so they did not depart until I was assigned the raid against them —
a force the like of which I had never seen.
The Messenger of Allah gave the first command;
Allah is too noble to countenance anything but the finest deed.
A force to whom victory comes from the One of Might —
on the eve they marched, crowded together, all of us present;
their cauldron seething with the rage of their companions,
their mounts and their resolve fixed upon their purpose.
When we saw them, they were prudent.

(1) See: Dictionary of Countries, 3/753.
(2) An addition from manuscript "B".
(3) The Prophet's Biography, 2/596–597.


<!-- page 195 -->
The Migration of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him)

And you have no rope to misguidance.
We said to them: "The rope of God is our support."
Abu Jahl arose there like a stray
So he was disappointed, and God turned back the schemes of Abu Jahl.
And they are dead after one boon,
While we number no more than thirty travelers.
O elephants of Lu'ayy, do not yield to your temptation —
They came to Islam and the easy path.
Torment, so you pray in regret, every one of you;
I fear it will pour down upon you.

**[The Battle of Buwat]**

Then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) led a raid against Quraysh after a quarter of a month. It was the first expedition in which both the Muhajirun and the Ansar went out with him. The Messenger of Allah (may peace be upon him) reached Buwat, in the vicinity of Radwa, then returned to Medina without engaging the enemy. The remainder of that month was Rabi' al-Akhir, and some of it fell in Jumada al-Awwal.

**[The Battle of al-'Ushayrah]**

He then led a raid against Quraysh until he reached the tribe at the heart of Yanbua', and he encamped among them for a time — this was during the nights of Jumada al-Akhirah. He then returned to the city without encountering any fighting.

During that expedition, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) came upon 'Ali (peace be upon him) and 'Ammar ibn Yasir sleeping on a mound of dust, their bodies covered in it. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to 'Ali (peace be upon him): "What is the matter with you, O Abu Turab?" Then he said: "Shall I not tell you who is the most wretched of all people?" He said:(1) "Uhaymir Thamud, who hamstrung the camel,(2) and the most wicked of the later ones, who

(1) "A" and "B": "The most wretched of the first two," and what is established here is an addition found in the Prophet's biography.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 196 -->
will strike you, O 'Ali, on this" — and he placed his hand on his shoulder — "until this becomes wet from it." And he took him by the beard.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then returned to Medina without any fighting having taken place.

**[The Secret Mission of Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas to al-Kharrar]**

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) dispatched Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas with eight of the Muhajirun. They set out until they reached al-Kharrar,(1) in the land of the Hijaz, then turned back without any fighting.

**[The First Battle of Badr]**

The Messenger of Allah (may peace be upon him) went out in pursuit of Kurz ibn Jabir al-Fihri,

who had raided the grazing lands of Medina. He pressed on until he reached Badr, but Kurz ibn Jabir had already escaped. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) returned to Medina, and this expedition became known as the First Battle of Badr.

**[The Secret Mission of 'Abdullah ibn Jahsh]**

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) dispatched 'Abdullah ibn Jahsh with eight men and said to him: "Travel until you descend upon a palm grove between Mecca and Ta'if, and keep watch over Quraysh." So 'Abdullah set out with those who were with him. When he had traveled two days, he said to his companions: "I am going to disclose what the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) has charged me with."

(1) See: Dictionary of Countries, 408/2–409.
(2) See: Dictionary of Countries, 769/4.

<!-- page 197 -->
"Whoever wishes to be a witness and aspires to it, let him be glad; and whoever is unwilling(1) may return." So Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas and 'Utbah ibn Ghazwan turned back,(2) and 'Abdullah marched on with those who remained.

**[What Passed Between the Two Groups]**

When he descended upon the palm grove where the Messenger of Allah had commanded him, he came upon a caravan of Quraysh carrying goods. 'Abdullah ibn Jahsh and his companions(3) fell upon those who were with the caravan. From among the polytheists they took captive: 'Uthman ibn 'Abd Allah and al-Hakam ibn Kaysan. They killed 'Amr ibn al-Hadrami, seized the caravan, and brought it to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him). The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) held back the caravan and the captives.

The Quraysh sent to ransom 'Uthman ibn 'Abd Allah and al-Hakam ibn Kaysan. As for al-Hakam ibn Kaysan, he accepted Islam and remained with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) until the day he was killed at the well of Ma'unah. As for 'Uthman ibn 'Abd Allah, he returned to Mecca and died as a disbeliever.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) allocated to 'Abdullah ibn Jahsh what remained after taking the fifth, and distributed four-fifths of that gain among the people of that expedition.

(1) "A" and "B": "Otherwise," and what is established here is an addition from the Prophet's biography.
(2) Ibn Hisham said: "Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas and 'Utbah ibn Ghazwan lost their way while following him, and so they fell behind on his mission." Biography of the Prophet 602/2.
(3) An addition from "B."

<!-- page 198 -->
'Amr ibn al-Hadrami was the first man killed by the Muslims, and that occurred

during the Sacred Month, concerning which Allah says: "They ask you about the Sacred Month — about fighting during it." [Al-Baqarah: 217], to the end of the verse.

The Quraysh said: Muhammad and his companions have violated the sanctity of the Sacred Month and shed blood in it.

**[The Poetry of 'Abdullah ibn Jahsh Concerning This Secret Mission]**

'Abdullah ibn Jahsh said:

You count it a grave sin during the forbidden month —
Your judgments against what Muhammad says —
Yet he disbelieved in him, and God is ever-watchful.
Lest he see in the House those who prostrate to God,
And expel from the mosque of God its people.
Tremble with Islam, and be consumed by envy —
Even if you shame us with the killing of him —
With a lance when I kindle the war and it blazes.
We watered our spears from the son of al-Hadrami,
Who contests it with the stubbornness of old.
Blood — and the son of 'Abd Allah, 'Uthman, is our prize.

(1) The Prophet's Biography 605/2–606.

<!-- page 199 -->
The Great Battle of Badr

Then came the Great Battle of Badr.

This is because the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) received word that Abu Sufyan ibn Harb was returning from the Levant with a great merchant caravan belonging to Quraysh — carrying their wealth and trade goods — accompanied by thirty or forty men of Quraysh. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) urged his companions forward, saying: "This is the caravan of Quraysh with their wealth — go out against it, and perhaps Allah will grant it to you as spoils." The people readied themselves, though some were reluctant, supposing that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) would not actually face a battle.

Abu Sufyan received word that Muhammad had roused his companions against his caravan. He hired Damdam ibn 'Amr al-Ghifari, sent him to Mecca, and ordered him to call Quraysh to arms in defense of their property.

**[The Vision of 'Atikah bint 'Abd al-Muttalib]**

'Atikah bint 'Abd al-Muttalib had a vision three days before Damdam arrived in Mecca. It terrified her. She saw a rider approaching on a camel until he halted, then called out at the top of his voice: "Hasten to your fallen warriors — in three days!" He then entered the mosque, and the people followed him. He made his camel kneel against the back of the Ka'bah and cried out in the same way: "Hasten to your fallen warriors — in three days!" Then he led his camel to the summit of Abu Qubays, called out as before, took a rock, and hurled it from the mountain. It crashed to the ground below, and as it rolled to the foot of the mountain it shattered, until there was not a single house or dwelling in Mecca that was not struck by a fragment of that rock.

<!-- page 200 -->
She told her brother 'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib about this, and al-'Abbas in turn mentioned it to al-Walid ibn 'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah, who spread the news. Abu Jahl then said: "Since when has this prophetess arisen among you? Is it not enough that your men lay claim to prophethood — must your women do so as well?"

Al-'Abbas denied that she had seen anything, and the Quraysh weighed in heavily on this, none more so than Abu Jahl ibn Hisham.

Al-'Abbas (may Allah be pleased with him) said: The women of Banu 'Abd al-Muttalib said to me: "Abu Jahl is belittling your men with insults, and will not be satisfied until he turns to insulting your women!" So I went out in anger, intending to confront Abu Jahl with something he would detest. I entered the mosque and saw him, and moved toward him to expose him. He retreated from some of what he had been saying, and I cornered him — for he was a lean man with a face like iron, a tongue like iron, and a gaze like iron. When(1) he moved toward the door of the mosque, I pressed him harder. I said to myself: "What does the curse of Allah matter — would this change anything for me if I were to curse him?"

Then something distracted me from him: the voice of Damdam ibn 'Amr al-Ghifari shouting from the belly of the valley, standing on his camel with his shirt torn, saying: "O people of Quraysh — the caravan! The caravan! Your wealth with Abu Sufyan has been intercepted by Muhammad and his companions — rescue it, rescue it!" And so that drew me away from Abu Jahl, and it drew him away from the matter between us.

**[Quraysh Prepare to Depart]**

The people prepared swiftly, saying: "Does Muhammad and his companions think this will be like the affair of Ibn

(1) An addition from the biography of the Prophet.

<!-- page 201 -->
al-Hadrami? No, by God, it will not." Not one of the nobles of Quraysh stayed behind, except that Abu Lahab remained and sent in his place al-'As ibn Hisham ibn al-Mughirah.

Quraysh took out al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib and Talib ibn Abi Talib and 'Aqil ibn Abi Talib with them to fight the Messenger of Allah, though they went out reluctantly. There arose a dispute between them and a companion of theirs, so they turned him back and said: "We know that your heart is with Muhammad." So Talib returned, and composed the following verses about it:

Oh Lord, whether they went forth with a companion
In a league of sworn warriors
In one of these positions(2) —
So make them the losers, not the victors,
And let them be the despoiled, not the ones who despoil.

Talib had in fact accepted Islam, and it is he who says of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him)(3):

The place of sleep and flowers —
The King's messenger, for a time.
The glory of Banu Hashim has come,
And pure Banu Hashim: Ahmad.

(1) Al-Rajaz in: Sirat al-Nabawiyyah 619/2, Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 1/121, Ansab al-Ashraf 42/2, Tarikh al-Tabari 439/2, Sharh al-Akhbar 237/3, Al-Manaqib wa al-Mathalib [folio 41/f], Songs 134/4.
(2) An addition from "B."
(3) The verses in: Sharh al-Akhbar 235/3, Al-Manaqib wa al-Mathalib [folio 41/f–41/z], and attributed to Abu Talib; see: The Faith of Abu Talib by Shaykh al-Mufid, p. 35.

<!-- page 202 -->
Bold of heart, of clear voice —
The great and honorable, the light of the land.
If the one who sets limits ponders his power —
Generous of aspect, fair as Lebanon.
Pure in conduct, both inwardly and outwardly,
Chaste, pious, immaculate in character.
From the neighborhood of Raba and from Zahra —
Bountiful, springlike, noble, noble of birth.
War brings rebuke upon those who deserve rebuke,
And he is like a lion — relentless, unspent.
How many of his cries have raised the fallen?
Long moaning gives way to relief.

**[The Messenger of Allah Sets Out]**

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) set out in the early nights of Ramadan with his companions — it is said on the eighth. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had two black banners: one he gave to 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, and the other to the Ansar.

The companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had seventy camels, which they rode in turns. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and 'Ali ibn Abi Talib shared one camel between them in turn, and the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) walked alongside the Muhajirun and the Ansar until they descended upon Sajjaj — which is the well of al-Ruh — then continued until, drawing close to al-Safra',(2) he sent two men ahead to gather news of Abu Sufyan ibn Harb.

(1) See: Mu'jam al-Buldan 724/3.
(2) See: Dictionary of Countries, 399/3.

<!-- page 203 -->
He pressed on until he came to a valley called Dhafran,(1) where he made camp. There the news reached him that Quraysh had marched out to defend their honor. He consulted the people, and they responded well and gave good counsel. He said: "Advance and be of good cheer, for Allah has promised me one of the two parties, and by Allah, it is as though I can see right now where the enemy will fall."

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then departed from Dhafran and soon descended upon Badr. When he had nearly arrived, he sent 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) with a group of Muslims to scout for news of Quraysh at the waters of Badr. They found two servants of Quraysh there: Aslam, a slave of Banu al-Hajjaj, and 'Arid, the slave of Abu Yasar of Banu al-'As ibn Sa'id.(3) They captured them and brought them to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him). He said: "Tell me about Quraysh." They said: "They are behind that great dune you see — the Dune of 'Aqanqal."

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to them: "How many(2) are the people?" They said: "Many." He said: "What is their number?"(4) They said: "We do not know their count." He said: "How many camels do they slaughter each day?" They said: "Nine one day and ten another." He said: "Then the people number between nine hundred and a thousand." He asked them: "Who among them are the nobles?"

(1) See: Dictionary of Countries, 720/2–721.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography.
(3) An addition from the Prophet's biography.
(4) An addition from the Prophet's biography.


<!-- page 204 -->
The Quraysh recalled him. He said to his companions: "This is Makkah — she has cast her liver-pieces before you."

As for Abu Sufyan, he turned his camel away from the road and left Badr on his left. Abu Sufyan had learned that al-'Ir was safe, and he sent word to the Quraysh ordering them to turn back, but they refused.

[115] This is the Quraysh: after falling into dispute among themselves, they pressed on until they had advanced as far as their most bitter enemies. From the valley, behind the Aqqul and the belly of the valley — that night — between Badr and the Aqqal, the dune was left behind by the Quraysh, and hearts lay full and exposed in the depths of the night, from the belly of the valley toward the city.

[The Advance of the Quraysh]

The Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) moved until he reached the nearest water of the people, and he descended there. He then commanded that a basin be built at the water source where he had descended, and it was filled with water.

When the Quraysh drew near, they were let through. When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) saw them advancing from al-'Aqqul, and from the dune through which they entered the valley, he said: "O Allah, here are the Quraysh — they have come in their arrogance and deceit, to challenge You and to give the lie to Your Messenger. O Allah, grant me the victory You have promised me. I am the one who stands against them."

(1) An increase in the biography of the Prophet.
(2) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(3) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 205 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) caught sight of 'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah among the Quraysh ranks and said: "If there is any good in any one of those people, it is in the rider of the red camel — if they follow him, they will be rightly guided." Yet 'Utbah was among those slain at Badr, and no good came of it.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) spoke truly, for Allah says: ⟪ar:وَمَا يَنطِقُ عَنِ الْهَوَىٰ⟫ [Al-Najm: 3]. Rather, it was with those who were from his children — the followers among the Imams of the family of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

[The Quraysh Consult on Whether to Turn Back]

When the Quraysh had settled their position, they sent 'Umayr ibn Wahb al-Jumahi and said: "Assess Muhammad's companions for us." He rode his horse around the Muslim army and then returned to them and said: "About three hundred men, give or take — but let me look more carefully to see whether they have an ambush or reinforcements waiting." He swept through the valley as far as he could and found nothing, so he returned to them [116] and said: "I found nothing, but I have seen, O assembly of Quraysh, calamities that carry their fates. Yathrib has sent you soaking death: a people who have no shield and no refuge except their swords. By Allah, I do not see that a single one of them will be killed without first killing one of you in return.

<!-- page 206 -->
If they kill among you a number equal to their own, what good is life after that?"

'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah then rose, pulled away from the Quraysh, and called on them to abandon the fighting. But Abu Jahl said: "He has swelled with cowardice, by God — his resolve has bewitched him." When he saw Muhammad and his companions, he said: "No, by God, we shall not turn back until God judges between us and Muhammad. Nothing stands before us but the sight of Muhammad and his companions — like the eaters of slaughter-camels — including his own son; perhaps you fear him." When word of 'Utbah's speech reached Abu Jahl, he replied: "He who is yellow with cowardice — that is he, not I. He will know which of us is swollen with magic." Then 'Utbah sought a helmet to place on his head but could find none to fit his large skull. Seeing this, he wrapped his head with a cloth.

[The Killing of al-Aswad al-Makhzumi]

Al-Aswad ibn 'Abd al-Asad al-Makhzumi came forward and declared: "I will drink from Muhammad's basin, or I will destroy it, or die without having done so." Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib (may Allah be pleased with him) went out to meet him. When they clashed, Hamza struck him and severed half of his leg — cutting it clean — and he fell on his back with his leg streaming blood. He then dragged himself toward the basin and plunged into it, intending to fulfill his oath, as he claimed. Hamza (peace be upon him) followed him and struck him again in the basin until he was killed.

(1) See: Other Proverbs on Deeds, 160.

<!-- page 207 -->
[The Call to Single Combat]

Then 'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah came forward, flanked by his brother Shaybah ibn Rabi'ah and his son al-Waleed ibn 'Utbah. When he stepped out from the line and called for single combat, three men came out to meet them: 'Awf and Mu'awwidh, the sons of al-Harith — their mother being 'Afra' — and another man; some say this third man was 'Abdullah ibn Rawaha. The Quraysh asked: "Who are you?" They replied: "Men of the Ansar." The Quraysh said: "We have no need of you."

A spokesman called out: "O Muhammad, send out to us men who are our equals from among our own people." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Rise, O 'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith; rise, O Hamzah; rise, O 'Ali."

[Pg 117]

When they rose and were asked who they were, they identified themselves and were recognized as worthy and honorable opponents.

'Ubaydah — the eldest of the three — faced 'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah. [90/f] Hamza (peace be upon him) faced Shaybah ibn Rabi'ah. And 'Ali (peace be upon him) faced al-Waleed ibn 'Utbah. 'Ali (peace be upon him) was the youngest of the three — eighteen years old; some say he had not yet reached twenty. Al-Waleed wasted no time before 'Ali killed him, and Hamza (peace be upon him) wasted no time before he killed Shaybah. As for 'Ubaydah, he and 'Utbah exchanged two blows; each man struck the other severely, and 'Ubaydah had his leg severed. 'Ali and Hamza (peace be upon them) then turned their swords on 'Utbah and finished him, then carried

(1) An increase in the biography of the Prophet.

<!-- page 208 -->
'Ubaydah back to the Muslim army.

[The Two Armies Meet]

Then the two sides advanced and engaged one another. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) commanded his companions not to charge until he gave the order, saying: "If the enemy surrounds you, drive them back with arrows."

The Muslims left the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) in the field shelter. They said: "If we are victorious, we will remain by your side." Abu Bakr stayed with him and did not fight.

It is narrated from al-Baqir Muhammad ibn 'Ali (peace be upon him) that he said: The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) arranged the ranks of his companions on the day of Badr and ordered them [90/p.m.] that no one should leave his position. If anyone was pressed by the polytheists, he was to fall back to his place and regroup with his fellows.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to his companions: "Rejoice, for here is Gabriel, leading a horse by the halter, fully armed."

[Urging the Muslims to Fight]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) went among the people and urged them on. He said: "By Him in Whose hand is Muhammad's soul, no man who fights them this day, killing patiently in God's cause, will fail to enter Paradise."

[Pg 118]

(1) See: The Biography of the Prophet, 626/2.

<!-- page 209 -->
The Muslims held their positions and stood firm. Then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) took a handful of pebbles, flung them toward the Quraysh, and said: "May those faces be disfigured." Then he gave the order to advance, saying to his companions: "Press forward." And the rout came.

'Ali and Hamza (may Allah be pleased with them) fought magnificently. The people witnessed 'Ali (peace be upon him) enduring hardship and difficulty, holding firm against adversity, in a manner they could scarcely believe came from any human being, given his age.

The polytheists were routed, and Allah granted the Muslims victory through slaying and capture.

[Those Slain at Badr from Among the Polytheists]

'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) slew: al-'Aas ibn Sa'id ibn al-'Aas ibn Umayyah, and 'Uqbah ibn Abi Mu'ayt [91/f] of Banu Umayyah ibn 'Abd Shams. And 'Amir ibn 'Abd Allah, of Banu Annar ibn Baghidh, an ally of theirs. And Ta'imah ibn 'Adi ibn Nawfal. Nuqal ibn Huwaylid ibn Asad — he is the son of al-'Adawiyyah of 'Adi Khuza'ah — and he was the one who had bound Abu Bakr ibn Abi Quhafa and Talhah ibn 'Ubayd Allah when they became Muslim, binding them together with a rope. He was one of the devils of Quraysh. Al-'Arr ibn al-Harith of Banu 'Abd al-Dar — 'Ali killed him in single combat before the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). And 'Umayr ibn 'Uthman ibn Tayyim ibn Murrah.

(1) An increase in the biography of the Prophet.

<!-- page 210 -->
And Harmalah ibn 'Amr, an ally of Banu Makhzum. And Mas'ud ibn Abi Umayya ibn al-Mughirah al-Makhzumi. And Abu Qays ibn al-Fakah ibn al-Mughirah, of Banu Makhzum. And 'Abd Allah ibn al-Mundhir ibn Abi Rifa'ah. And Hajib ibn al-Sa'ib.

[Pg 119]

And Abu al-'As ibn Umayya ibn al-Hajjaj al-Sahmi. And Aws ibn Mu'ir ibn Luthan ibn Jumaah. And Mu'awiya ibn 'Amir, of 'Abd al-Qays, an ally of theirs.

We have already mentioned that al-Waleed ibn 'Utbah was slain in single combat — he is counted among those killed specifically by 'Ali (peace be upon him). [91/z] 'Ali and Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib (peace be upon them) shared in the killing of 'Utbah ibn Rabi'ah — as has been noted — together with 'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith (may Allah be pleased with him).

Hamza and 'Ali (peace be upon them) and Zayd ibn Haritha shared in the killing of Hanzalah ibn Abi Sufyan. Zama'ah ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muttalib shared with 'Ali and Hamza (peace be upon them) in killing his man.

<!-- page 211 -->
'Aqeel ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muttalib also shared with 'Ali and Hamza (peace be upon them) in a killing.

Shaybah ibn Rabi'ah, as we have mentioned, was killed by Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib (peace be upon him) in single combat.

Abu Qays ibn al-Waleed ibn al-Mughirah was killed by Hamza (peace be upon him); some say by 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him).

Al-Aswad ibn 'Abd al-Asad al-Makhzumi was killed by Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib (peace be upon him).

Nabeeh ibn al-Hajjaj ibn 'Amir was killed by Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib (peace be upon him); some say that Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas shared in killing him.

Al-Harith ibn al-Hadrami was killed by 'Ammar ibn Yasir (may Allah be pleased with him).

'Umayr ibn Abi 'Umayr and his son — two mawali of Banu 'Abd Shams — were killed by Salim, the mawla of Abu Hudhayfa.

'Ubaydah ibn Sa'id ibn al-'As ibn Umayyah was killed [92/f] by al-Zubayr ibn al-'Awwam.

Al-Harith ibn 'Amir ibn Nawfal ibn 'Abd Manaf was killed by Khubaib ibn Issaf, brother of Banu al-Harith ibn al-Khazraj.

Al-Harith ibn Zama'ah ibn al-Aswad was killed by 'Ammar ibn Yasir.

<!-- page 212 -->
Abu al-Bakhtari — who was al-'As ibn Hisham — was killed by al-Mujdhir ibn Dhiyad al-Balawi.

Uzayd ibn Malis was killed by Bilal ibn Rabah, along with Zayd, an ally of Banu 'Abd al-Dar; some say al-Miqdad ibn al-Aswad killed him.

'Uthman ibn Malik ibn Ka'b was killed by Suhayb ibn Sinan.

Abu Jahl ibn Hisham — whose name was 'Amr ibn Hisham ibn al-Mughirah ibn 'Abd Allah ibn 'Amr ibn Makhzum — was killed jointly by Mu'adh ibn 'Amr ibn al-Jamuh, Mu'awwidh ibn 'Afra', and 'Abd Allah ibn Mas'ud.

[Pg 120]

Al-'As ibn Hisham was killed by 'Umar ibn al-Khattab.

Yazid ibn 'Abd Allah, an ally of Banu Makhzum who was known for his bravery, was killed by 'Ammar ibn Yasir.

Abu Musafi' al-Ash'ari, an ally of theirs, was killed by Abu Dajana al-Sa'idi.

Rifa'ah ibn Abi Rifa'ah al-Makhzumi was killed by Sa'd ibn al-Rabi', brother of Banu al-Harith ibn al-Khazraj.

Al-Mundhir ibn Abi Rifa'ah was killed by Ma'an [92/z] ibn 'Adi, an ally of Ibn 'Ubayd Allah ibn Zayd ibn Malik ibn 'Amr ibn 'Awf.

Al-Sa'ib ibn Abi al-Sa'ib al-Makhzumi was killed by al-Zubayr ibn al-'Awwam.

And 'Uwaymar ibn al-Sa'ib was killed by al-Nu'man ibn Malik al-Qawqali.

(1) Ibn Hisham [al-Sira, 711/2] said: "Al-'As ibn Hisham ... was killed by 'Umar ibn al-Khattab."
(2) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 213 -->
'Amr ibn Sufyan was killed by Yazid ibn Ruqaysh.

Jabir ibn Sufyan was killed by Abu Burdah ibn Nayyar.

Munabbih ibn al-Hajjaj ibn 'Amir was killed by Abu al-Yusr, brother of Banu Salamah.

And his son al-'As ibn Munabbih ibn al-Hajjaj was killed by Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib, with Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas sharing in the killing.

Abu al-'As ibn Qays ibn 'Adi ibn Sa'd ibn Sahm was killed by 'Ali ibn Abi Talib; some say by al-Nu'man ibn Malik al-Qawqali, and some say by Abu Dajana.

'Asim ibn Abi 'Awf ibn Dhubayrah, of Banu Sahm, was also killed by Abu al-Yusr.

Umayya ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb, of Banu Jumaah, was killed by Mu'adh ibn 'Afra', with Kharijah ibn Zayd and Khubaib ibn Issaf sharing in the killing.

His son 'Ali ibn Umayya was killed by 'Ammar ibn Yasir.

Abu Ma'bad ibn Wahb, an ally of theirs from Banu Kalb, was killed by Khalid and Iyas ibn al-Bukayr; some say by Abu Dajana.

[Pg 121]

The total count of those killed at Badr from among the polytheists was fifty men. Some say the dead of Badr were seventy men, and likewise the captives were seventy — this is the opinion of Ibn 'Abbas and Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib.

Allah the Exalted says in His Book: ⟪ar:أَوَلَمَّا أَصَابَتْكُم مُّصِيبَةٌ قَدْ أَصَبْتُم مِّثْلَيْهَا⟫ [Āl 'Imrān: 165]. And that was on a Sunday — he was [93/f] martyred on a Sunday.

(1) An increase in the biography of the Prophet.
(2) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

The Muslims numbered seventy men, and Allah says: "You have struck the like of them on the day of Badr." On Sunday, seventy were killed and seventy taken prisoner.

Ka'b ibn Malik (1) sang:
Seventy of them were struck down by the stinging snake, and the lions.

[Naming of those martyred at Badr]

On the day of Badr, fourteen Muslims were martyred, six of whom were Muhajireen. They are:

'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith, whose leg was severed and who died of the wound.
Amir ibn Abi Waqqas, brother of Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas. (2)
Dhu al-Shamalayn [Umayr] ibn 'Abd 'Amr ibn Nadlah, an ally of Banu Zahra from Khara'ah, then from Bani Ghabshan.

And from Banu 'Adi ibn Ka'b ibn Lu'ay: 'Aqil ibn [Abi] (3) al-Bakir, who was an ally of theirs, and the freedman of 'Umar ibn al-Khattab. And Safwan ibn Bayda from Bani Fahr.

Eight of the Ansar:
From Banu Amr ibn 'Awf: Sa'd ibn Khaythama, and Mubashir ibn 'Abd al-Mundhir ibn Zanbar.

187
The Great Battle of Badr

From Banu al-Harith ibn al-Khazraj: Yazid ibn al-Harith and 'Umayr ibn al-Hammam. [122]
[93/z] Rafi' ibn al-Mu'alla, Haritha ibn Saraqa, 'Awf and Mu'awdh ibn al-Harith ibn Rifa'ah — these last two being the sons of Afra' — may Allah have mercy on them all.

[Martyrdom of 'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith]

After the battle, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) distributed the spoils among his companions and entrusted the captives to a guard. When he reached al-Safra', he went to 'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hashim ibn 'Abd Manaf, who had already been struck before this. He was in great pain. He asked about the wound to his leg, and 'Ubaydah said: "O Messenger of Allah, Abu Talib believed," citing his words: (1)

*And we hand him over until we insist around him, and we marvel at our sons and the lawful.*

"If Abu Talib had seen this, he would have kept it to himself." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) praised him, and 'Ali ibn Abi Talib was with him. 'Ubaydah died — may Allah have mercy on him and be pleased with him — of his wound.

[The Killing of al-Nadir and 'Uqbah]

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) ordered the execution of al-Nadir ibn al-Harith. While at the valley of the doe, he also ordered the execution of 'Uqbah ibn Abi Mu'ayt. When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) ordered his killing, 'Uqbah said: "Who will look after my children?" The Prophet said — may Allah's prayers

(1) See: Diwan, 74.

<!-- page 216 -->
188 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

be upon him and his family — "The Fire." So 'Ali (peace be upon him) struck his neck. It was also said that the one who struck him was 'Asim ibn Thabit [ibn] (1) [Abi] (2) al-Aqlah.

[His Return from Badr to Madinah]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) returned to Madinah with the captives under guard. Some of the captives were ordered to ransom themselves. Among those taken prisoner on the day of Badr were al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib, the uncle of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), and 'Aqil ibn Abi Talib. The Quraysh had compelled them to march out against their will, and the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to the Muslims: "Whoever among the Banu Hashim you are able to capture, do not kill them, for they were brought out against their will."

So al-'Abbas and 'Aqil ibn Abi Talib were captured. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to al-'Abbas: "Ransom yourself and your nephew, for he has no money." Al-'Abbas said: "I have nothing." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to him: "Where is the money you left with Umm al-Fadl on the day you departed from Mecca? You said to her: 'If I am killed, then such-and-such goes to 'Abdullah, and such-and-such to al-Fadl.'" He described to him exactly what he had said to her. Al-'Abbas said: "No one knew of this except Allah, and so I bear witness that there is no god but Allah and that you are the Messenger of Allah." 'Aqil testified to the same, and al-'Abbas ransomed himself and 'Aqil.

(1) An addition from "B".
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography, 2/644.

189
The Great Battle of Badr

[News of the Losses at Badr Reaches Their Families in Mecca]

Abu Rafi', the freedman of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), said:

"I was a slave of al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib. Islam had entered our household: al-'Abbas had embraced it, and Umm al-Fadl had become Muslim. Yet al-'Abbas feared his people, was reluctant to oppose them openly, and so concealed his Islam. He had considerable wealth distributed among his people.

Abu Lahab had stayed behind from Badr and sent al-'Asi ibn Hisham ibn al-Mughirah in his place. When news arrived in Mecca of the Quraysh's defeat at Badr, Allah humbled Abu Lahab and shamed him, and we felt a sense of strength and pride within ourselves.

I was carving goblets in the room of Zamzam. Umm al-Fadl, wife of al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib, was with me, and we were delighted by the news that had reached us. Then Abu Lahab came dragging his feet and sat down on the edge of the room, his back against mine. The people said: 'Here comes Abu Sufyan ibn al-Harith ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib.' Abu Lahab called out: 'Come to me — you have news.' He sat down, while the other people stood around him, and Abu Lahab said: 'O nephew, tell me how it went.' He replied: 'By Allah, we had barely met the enemy when they turned on us and did with us as they pleased — they cut us down, they led us captive at will. And yet, I tell you, between heaven and earth we saw white horsemen, and there was nothing we could do against them.'

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 2/646–647.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 218 -->
190 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

Nothing could be done against them, and nothing availed."

Abu Rafi' said: "I raised the support-pole of the room with my hand, and then (1) I said: 'Those, by Allah, were angels.'" [124] He said: "Abu Lahab raised his hand and struck me hard across the face. I fought back against him, but he overpowered me, threw me to the ground, and then knelt on my chest. Umm al-Fadl seized one of the room's pillars and struck him with it, splitting his head open, saying: 'You bully him because his master is away?' He got up and left. He lived only seven more nights before Allah struck him with a pestilence that killed him, and the people were rid of him."

[Verses of Poetry on the Day of Badr]

'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) recited on the day of Badr: (3)

*A noble scourge, with power and grace —*
*Did you not see that Allah has fulfilled His Messenger's promise?*
*When the disbelievers were brought low in humiliation,*
*They found awaiting them captivity and slaughter.*
*The Messenger of Allah was sent with justice —*
*And the Messenger of Allah was granted his victory.*
*What signs these are for those who possess understanding.*
*He brought a house from God,*
*And people believed in that and were guided.*
*So they were reunited — praise be to Allah —*
*Their vain one, against the Throne, in ruin.*
*The nations that deny have broken hearts.*

[95/p.m]

[125]

*By now, the reasons for the matter have become plain.*
*Did he not reproach what was among the wonders of the ages?*
*They grew angry and did what they considered best,*
*Holding in their hands what they clung to.*
*Yet you met it with clarity and resolve.*
*How many of the young men of Dhi Hama were left behind,*
*Crying out — and among them an old man.*
*The eyes of mourning women linger over them,*
*Turning with wailing and lamentation.*
*An old man mourns, and mourns Abu Jahl;*
*Nawaih mourns the threshold of al-Ghayy and his son.*
*The obelisk of Hurra, tarnished.*
*Al-Dahl mourns, and Ibn Haddan among them.*
*It was in their power on the day of the full moon, before his prophet's time,*
*A few of them in the midst of the gathering.*
*People tested in wars and in the fray,*
*Whose mighty bonds of kinship were dissolved.*
*He called for the dissolution of those who had prayed, and he was answered,*
*Over the people, in the most consequential of affairs.*
*So they were left isolated at the house of the Humvees.*

Hamza ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib said on the day of Badr: (1)

[96/f]

*They marched against us and we met at a fated hour,*
*And we would have sought no other outcome.*
*Now they lie in disobedience and disbelief,*
*Mortgaged to destruction from Badr.*
*On the eve of their departure they gathered toward Badr,*
*And we had no recourse but to strike with the dark blade.*
*When we met, she did not hesitate.*
*It was only that some men gave them benefit,*
*And struck with a pulse that concealed the inspiration.*
*We left the threshold, canceling their pride,*
*Displaying the color of manifest proof.*
*An old man among the dead, dragged into the pits,*
*The pockets of the mourners opened for 'Amr.*
*And 'Amr Thawa is more than their mother-in-law.*

*Noble men turned aside from Fahr,*
*And let a commander not perish for the sake of victory —*
*Feel them, for treachery is treacherous.*
*I swear to you I have no patience today.*

And he said to them, when the matter became clear:

*I fear Allah's punishment — by Allah, it is a compulsion.*
*I see what you do not see,*
*And what the people have not yet heard is news.*
*So he pressed them until they became embroiled,*
*Three times like a blossoming.*
*So they became a thousand on the day after the storm, and we gathered —*
*They in one place, then the matter clarified.*
*Among us are the soldiers of Allah, when He grants us*
*A trial in which their wishes are tested.*
*So Jibril led them beneath our banner,*
*The women's households of Luayy ibn Ghalib.*
*Those are the ones who err in their transgression —*
*A misguided brigade, led by Satan to his own.*

'Ubaydah ibn al-Harith ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib said, when he was carried away from Badr to al-Bifur: (1) [96/z]

*The people of Mecca have informed us of a calamity — those who were far from it felt it keenly,*
*And there was no Bakr in it who was satisfied.*
*I ask from it life from Allah, close at hand,*
*With the highest of heaven for those who were noble.*
*I struck until the lowest were lost,*
*And sold that life and came to know clarity.*
*With a garment of Islam that covered all alike,*
*The next day the capable called out to whoever would preach.*
*With a threshold when he is gone, and an old man after him.*
*If my leg is cut off, then I am still a Muslim*
*[126] Among the sincere, like a swaying palm.*
*The Merciful has honored me with His bounty,*

*The Messenger of Allah (may peace be upon him) was called a sorcerer,*

Hasan ibn Thabit (1) said:

*You heal what is right with a cold, bright smile,*

*And the night distributes my dreams through it,*

*I swore to forget her and leave off her mention,*
*And I have defied my own desires for my reproach.*
*O you who blame me for my foolishness —*
*I was raised from a spell after the curse,*
*And a convergence of the incidents of days,*
