He called upon the Arab tribes around him, but many of them held back. He proceeded — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — with those who accompanied him from the Muhajireen, the Ansar, and those Arabs who had pledged themselves to him. He brought the sacrificial animals with him and entered the state of ihram, so that the people would know he had come only as a visitor to the Sacred House — a great mark of honor for it. The sacrificial animals sent by the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) numbered seventy head.

Jabir ibn 'Abdullah al-Ansari used to say: "We, the companions of al-Hudaybiyah, were fourteen hundred."

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) entered ihram at Dhu al-Hulayfah. He assumed the state of consecration, his companions followed him into it, and they garlanded the sacrificial animals and marked them to distinguish them.

[The Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and Bishr ibn Sufyan al-Ka'bi]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) reached 'Usfan, he was met by Bishr ibn Sufyan al-Ka'bi — also referred to as "Busr" — who said: "O Messenger of Allah, Quraysh has heard of your march. They have gone out with their women, wearing animal skins, and have sworn a covenant with Allah that you will never enter upon them. Khalid ibn al-Walid is now at the head of their cavalry, encamped at the shepherd's plateau."

<!-- page 326 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) said: "Woe to Quraysh — war has consumed them. What would it cost them to leave me to the rest of the Arabs? If they were to strike me down, that would be what they sought — and if Allah were to grant me victory over them, they would enter Islam in great numbers. And if they do not, they will fight and be destroyed. What do the Quraysh think? By Allah, I will never cease striving for what Allah has sent me to accomplish until He causes it to prevail — or until this neck is severed." He pointed to the front of his neck. Then he said: "Is there anyone who can lead us by another road so that we avoid encountering Quraysh?"

[The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) takes a different route to avoid the Quraysh]

A man who had accepted Islam said: "I am, O Messenger of Allah." He led them along a rough and difficult road. When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and his companions reached the descent toward al-Hudaybiyah, his she-camel knelt and refused to move. His companions said: "She is spent, O Messenger of Allah." He said: "She is not spent, nor is that her nature. What has restrained her is the same force that restrained the Elephant from Mecca. Today I will not refuse any plan by which Quraysh asks me to honor the ties of kinship." He then said to them: "Dismount." They replied: "O Messenger of Allah, there is no water in this valley on which we can camp." He took an arrow from his quiver and gave it to one of his companions — said to be a prince among them —

<!-- page 327 -->
from among the believers. He plunged it into the bed of that hollow, digging it into the earth,

and the water welled up. The people drank and watered their animals.

['Urwah ibn Mas'ud as a messenger from Quraysh to the Messenger of Allah]

Envoys moved back and forth between the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and Quraysh. Quraysh sent 'Urwah ibn Mas'ud al-Thaqafi to speak with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) told him that he had not come to fight, but only to honor the Sacred House. 'Urwah returned to Quraysh, having witnessed how the companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) revered him: whenever he performed ablution, they rushed to use the water he had used; whenever he spat, they caught it; and whenever a hair fell from him, they took it. He went back to Quraysh and said: "O people, I have seen Khosrow in his kingdom, and Caesar in his kingdom, and the Negus in his kingdom — and by Allah, I have never seen any king among his people as Muhammad is among his companions. Do not provoke them."

[The raiding party sent by Quraysh against the Muslim camp]

Quraysh sent fifty men to circle around the army of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and take prisoners. They were themselves captured. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) pardoned them and released them. Yet they pelted the camp of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) with arrows and stones. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) sent envoys to Quraysh, who imprisoned them and acted in bad faith.

<!-- page 328 -->
[The Pledge of al-Ridwan]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) learned that his envoys had been seized and killed, his companions pledged allegiance to him — this is the Pledge of al-Ridwan. Some say the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) took their pledge unto death. But Jabir ibn 'Abdullah used to say: "The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) did not take our pledge unto death; he took our pledge that we would not flee."

[The order of the truce]

The Quraysh eventually sent Suhayl ibn 'Amr, brother of Banu 'Amir ibn Lu'ayy, to treat with the Prophet. When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) saw him coming, he said: "The people want peace." The two men negotiated and reached an agreement with relative ease, and a settlement was near — nothing remained but the written document. At this point 'Umar ibn al-Khattab rose in protest and addressed the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family): "O Messenger of Allah, are you not the Messenger of Allah?" He said: "Yes." He said: "Are we not Muslims?" He said: "Yes." He said: "Are they not polytheists?" He said: "Yes." He said: "Then why should we concede anything of our religion?" The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) said: "I am the servant of Allah and His Messenger; I will not disobey His command, and He will not forsake me."

['Ali (peace be upon him) writes the terms of the truce]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) then called upon 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) to write the document. He said: "Write: In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful." Suhayl said: "I do not recognize this; write instead: In Your name, O God." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) said:


<!-- page 329 -->
As the document was being drafted, 'Ali wrote down: "In the name of Allah." Then he wrote: "This is what Muhammad, the Messenger of Allah, has agreed upon with Suhail ibn 'Amr." Suhail objected: "If I acknowledged you as the Messenger of Allah, I would not have fought you — write your name and your father's name instead." The Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said to 'Ali: "Write: This is what Muhammad ibn 'Abd Allah has agreed upon." The Commander of the Faithful said: "O Messenger of Allah, I cannot erase your name." He replied: "Write it — you will one day be called to do something similar." That day came in the time of Mu'awiyah, and we shall recount it in its place.

[Copying the covenant of peace with the Quraysh in the year of Hudaybiyyah]

'Ali (peace be upon him) wrote the following, at the dictation of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him):

"In the name of Allah. This is what Muhammad ibn 'Abd Allah and Suhail ibn 'Amr have agreed upon. They have agreed to lay down the state of war between the two peoples for ten years, during which all people shall be safe and refrain from attacking one another. Whoever comes to Muhammad from the Quraysh without his guardian's permission shall be returned to them. Whoever comes to the Quraysh from those with Muhammad, they shall not be returned. There shall be no treachery and no breach of faith. Whoever wishes to enter into the covenant and alliance of Muhammad may do so, and whoever wishes to enter into the covenant and alliance of the Quraysh may do so."

(1) "2" and "B": "I was", and the reading I have adopted is from the Biography of the Prophet (Sīrah) 3/317.
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 3/317–318.
(3) An addition from the Prophet's biography.
(4) See: Majma' al-Amthāl 1/67.

<!-- page 330 -->
[Khuzā'ah entered the covenant of Muhammad and Banū Bakr entered the covenant of the Quraysh]

Khuzā'ah stepped forward and declared: "We are in the covenant and alliance of Muhammad." Banū Bakr likewise stepped forward and declared: "We are in the covenant and alliance of the Quraysh." The Quraysh then said: "You must turn back from us this year and not enter Mecca. But when a year has passed, we shall withdraw and you shall enter with your companions and remain there for three days. You may carry only the weapons of a traveller — swords in their scabbards — and bring nothing more."

[The completion of the treaty document and the words of Abū Jandal]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had nearly finished drafting the treaty with Suhail ibn 'Amr when Abū Jandal ibn Suhail ibn 'Amr appeared, shuffling toward them in his shackles. He had accepted Islam, and his father Suhail had imprisoned him on that account. He had now escaped and made his way to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him). His father said to the Prophet: "Has not the agreement between us already been settled, before this man arrived?" The Prophet said: "Yes." Suhail then rose, seized his son, and dragged him back toward the Quraysh, while Abū Jandal cried out at the top of his voice: "O community of Muslims — am I to be handed back to the polytheists, who will persecute me for my religion?"

The Muslims were deeply shaken.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "O Abū Jandal, be patient and persevere. Allah will surely provide relief and a way out for you and for those like you who are oppressed. We have made peace with these people and have given them the covenant of Allah. We cannot act in bad faith toward them."

When the document was completed, several Muslim men witnessed the reconciliation.

<!-- page 331 -->
"Your people."(1)

[The gathering of detainees around Abū Basīr]

Abū Basīr was escorted back with the polytheists, and when they reached Dhū al-Ḥulayfa, Abū Basīr killed one of his two escorts. He then made his way until he encamped at al-'Īṣ, on the shores of the sea along the road that the Quraysh used to travel to the Levant. When the news reached the Muslims who were still imprisoned in Mecca, they began slipping away one by one to join him. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had said of Abū Basīr: "Woe to his mother — what a kindler of war he would be, if only he had men behind him!" And indeed, some seventy men eventually gathered around him at al-'Īṣ. They waged relentless warfare against the Quraysh, not letting any of their caravans pass without attacking them and killing those they could. This continued until the Quraysh wrote to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), appealing to him by ties of kinship to grant Abū Basīr and his companions shelter in Medina, saying they no longer had any need for the treaty clause requiring their return. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) granted them shelter, and they came to him in Medina.

Abū Basīr was from Thaqīf.

[The condition of the emigrant women after the truce]

Around that time, Kulthūm bint 'Uqbah ibn Abī Mu'ayṭ emigrated to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), and her brothers 'Amāra and al-Walīd came after her, asking for her return.

Then Allah (Exalted be He) revealed to His Messenger (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him):

(1) An addition from the Prophet's biography 3/323.

<!-- page 332 -->
The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was troubled, and so he went to his sacrificial animal and slaughtered it, then sat down. He shaved his head, and when the Muslims saw that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had slaughtered and shaved, they rose and did likewise — some shaving their heads completely, others cutting their hair short. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "May Allah have mercy on those who shave." They said: "And those who cut short, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "May Allah have mercy on those who shave." They said again: "And those who cut short, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "May Allah have mercy on those who shave — and on those who cut short." On the third repetition, he included them as well.

[Sūrat al-Fatḥ is revealed]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) returned with his caravan, and while he was between Mecca and Medina, the Sūrat al-Fatḥ was revealed to him.

[What happened to some of the weak after the reconciliation]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) arrived in Medina, Abū Basīr — whose full name was 'Utbah ibn Usayd ibn Jāriyah — came to him. He was one of those who had been imprisoned in Mecca. He was brought to the Prophet by a man from the polytheists, who carried a letter from the Quraysh saying: "Did you not promise us that anyone who came to you from our side would be returned?" The Prophet said: "Yes." He then said: "O Abū Basīr, we have given these people a binding pledge, and treachery has no place in our religion. Allah will grant you and those like you relief and a way out."

<!-- page 333 -->
"O you who believe, when believing women come to you as emigrants, examine them — Allah knows best concerning their faith. If you find them to be believers, do not return them to the disbelievers. They are not lawful for the disbelievers, nor are the disbelievers lawful for them. Give the disbelievers what they have spent, and there is no blame on you if you marry them when you have given them their bridal gifts. And do not hold to the marriage bonds of disbelieving women." [60:10]

So Allah (Exalted be He) commanded that these women not be returned to the disbelievers.

<!-- page 334 -->
[Mention of the march to Khaybar]

After returning from al-Ḥudaybiyyah, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) remained in Medina through Dhū al-Ḥijjah and into part of Muḥarram — during which time the polytheists held custody of the sacred pilgrimage. He then set out for Khaybar in the remaining days of Muḥarram of the seventh year, taking with him the Muhājirūn, the Anṣār, and the most distinguished of his companions. He gave the banner to 'Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib (peace be upon him), and it was white.

['Āmir ibn al-Akwa' chants his battle verses and is martyred]

Ibn al-Akwa' went out ahead of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), striking the ground with his feet and chanting:(1)

*By Allah, had it not been for Allah, we would not have been guided,*
*nor given charity, nor performed our prayers.*
*If they attack us*
*and seek to sow strife — where shall it go?*
*Send down upon us steadfastness,*
*and make our feet firm when we meet them.*

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "May Allah have mercy on you." He was killed on the day of Khaybar as a martyr — his own sword glanced back against him while he was fighting fiercely, and he died from the wound. May Allah have mercy on him.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 3/328–329.

<!-- page 335 -->
The Muslims fell into doubt about him and began saying that he had killed himself with his own weapon — until the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) declared: "He is a martyr." The Muslims then prayed over him.

[The supplication of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) when he overlooked Khaybar]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) arrived within sight of Khaybar, he said to his companions: "Halt." Then he said: "O Allah, Lord of the seven heavens and all they shade, Lord of the seven earths and all they carry, Lord of the devils and those they lead astray, Lord of the winds and all they scatter — we ask You for the good of this settlement, the good of its people, and the good of what lies within it. We seek refuge in You from its evil, the evil of its people, and the evil of what lies within it. In the name of Allah." He would say this upon approaching every settlement he intended to enter.

[The people of Khaybar fled when they saw the Messenger (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him)]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) arrived at Khaybar under cover of night, so that by morning the Muslims were mounted and in formation around him. The people of Khaybar came out carrying their tools to begin their day's work, and when they caught sight of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his army, they cried out: "Muhammad! Muhammad and his army!" They turned and fled. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allahu Akbar! Khaybar is destroyed! When we descend into the courtyard of a people, wretched is the morning of those who have been warned."

[The halting places of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) on his way to Khaybar]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) departed from Medina on this expedition, he stopped at [155/f] 'Aṣr,(1) where he had a mosque. Then at

(1) See: Mu'jam al-Buldān, 3/603.

<!-- page 336 -->
al-Ṣahbā',(1) after which he (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) pressed on with his army until he descended into a valley known as al-Rajā', lying between the lands of Khaybar and Ghaṭafān, so as to cut off any support from Ghaṭafān.

[The affair of 'Alī (peace be upon him) on the day of Khaybar]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) first gave the banner to Abū Bakr — it was white — and sent him to fight the people of Khaybar. He returned without victory. The next day he sent 'Umar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, and he too returned without conquering anything. The Prophet then said: "Tomorrow I shall give the banner to a man who loves Allah and His Messenger, and whom Allah and His Messenger love. He does not flee — Allah will open Khaybar at his hands."

Everyone eagerly hoped to be that man. When morning came, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Where is my brother 'Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib?"

He was summoned. The Prophet spat into his eyes and said: "O Allah, remove from him the discomfort of heat and cold." From that day on, 'Alī (peace be upon him) was never again troubled by heat or cold. The Prophet gave him the banner and said: "Go forward with it, until Allah opens victory at your hands."

He set out at a swift pace, the Muslims following behind him, until he planted the banner against a rock at the foot of the fortress. A Jew looked down at him from the top of the fortification and called: "Who are you?" He replied: "I am 'Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib." The Jew said to those around him: "By what was revealed to Moses son of 'Imrān, you have been defeated!" And indeed, 'Alī did not depart until Allah had opened Khaybar at his hands.(3)

(1) An addition from the Prophet's biography 3/437.
(2) An addition from the Prophet's biography 3/334.
(3) An addition from the Prophet's biography 3/335.

<!-- page 337 -->
[Marḥab the Jew is killed]

A Jewish warrior came out from their fortress, fully armed and bristling with weapons, trembling with battle-fury. He chanted:(1)

*Khaybar knows well that I am Marḥab —*
*a proven warrior, fully armed, a hero of battle.*
*Sometimes I thrust with the spear, sometimes I strike with the sword,*
*when the young man steps forward to fight.*
*None may come near my protected ground.*

'Alī (peace be upon him) answered him, saying:

*I am the one whose mother named him Ḥayḍara —*
*I shall feed you the blade of the sword, full measure.*

Marḥab launched his attack against the Commander of the Faithful, and 'Alī met his blow with his shield, deflecting it. 'Alī (peace be upon him) then seized a door from the fortress gate and used it as a shield, protecting himself with it. Swinging it as a weapon, he struck Marḥab(3) and shattered his shield, bringing down upon him a blow of mighty, resounding force.

He continued to fight with that door in his hand until he had slain a number of the Jewish champions.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 3/333.
(2) See: Dīwānuh, 67.
(3) An addition from "B".


<!-- page 338 -->
310 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

God opened his hands, and he flung the door away, after which eight men gathered together to move it. It was said that there were more, but however many they were, they turned the door over and could not lift it.

[The Trial of Ali (peace be upon him) at Khaybar]

Among what is reported of Ali (peace be upon him) is that he said:

"When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) led the campaign against Khaybar, its people met us — the Jews — like mountains [156] of horses and weapons. They held the most fortified of strongholds and were the greatest in number, each man calling out to challenge anyone who would face him, and not one of the Muslims who approached them was left unkilled.

Until I pressed forward, drawn into combat, every man occupied with himself. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family came toward their position, and not one of their men appeared before me without my killing him, and no horseman among them stood against me without my running him through. Then I drove at them with the full ferocity of a lion upon its prey, and thrust them deep into their city until they scattered among themselves.

I came to the city gate and found it shut against them, so I tore it out with my hands and entered their city alone, killing every man of theirs who appeared before me and taking captive every woman I found [in] [2] among their wives. I opened it alone, and I had no helper except Allah alone."

And from Isma'il ibn Muhammad al-Kufi, with his isnad, from Jabir ibn Abd-Allah al-Ansari, who said [3]:

(1) See: Sharh al-Akhbar 301/1.
(2) Addition from the explanation of the news.
(3) See: Sharh al-Akhbar 381/2–382, Manaqib Amir al-Mu'minin 249/1–251, Manaqib Ali by Ibn al-Maghazali 237–239, Virtues of Flowers 133–134.

<!-- page 339 -->
311.
Battle of Khaybar

When Ali (peace be upon him) came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family, having opened the gate of Khaybar, he (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said:

"Were it not that a group among my community would say of you what the Christians say of Christ, [207] I would have said to you [today] (1) a word (2) such that you would not pass by a gathering of people without them taking from the dust under your feet [3] and the water of your purification as a means of healing.

But it is enough for you that you stand to me as Aaron stood to Moses — except that there is no prophet after me. You will acquit my obligations, and you will fight for my Sunnah. In the Hereafter, you will be the closest of all people to me, and you will be [tomorrow] at the basin as my successor. You will be the first to come to me at the basin because you were the first to believe in me, the first to be clothed by me, (6) and the first of my community to enter Paradise.

And when your Shia stand on pulpits of radiant light, their faces bright [around me], (2) I will intercede for them so that they may be my neighbors in Heaven. Whoever fights you fights me, and whoever makes peace with you makes peace with me. Your bond of kinship is as my bond of kinship. You are born of my son and fulfill my obligations, and if

(1) Addition from the explanation of the news.
(2) "Word" in the explanation of the news.
(3) An addition in the Virtues of Ali by Ibn al-Maghazali.
(4) An addition in the Virtues of Ali by Ibn al-Maghazali.

<!-- page 340 -->
312 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

the truth is on your tongue and in your heart, before your hands and before your eyes, and faith is mingled with your flesh and blood just as it is mingled with mine, then no one who hates you will reach the Basin, and no lover of yours will fail to find you there with him."

He said: At that, Ali (peace be upon him) prostrated and said: Praise be to Allah, who blessed me with Islam, taught me the Quran, made me beloved to the best of the wilderness, and set upon me the seal of the Prophets and the Messengers — [and the purity of God in all the worlds] (2) — as a grace from Him to me and a distinction He conferred upon me.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Were it not for you, O Ali, the believers would not have recognized [me] (4) after me."

[The Remainder of the Khaybar Affair]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then opened Khaybar and seized its fortresses. He put to death whomever he willed among the men, granted safety to whomever he willed, took captives from their descendants, and chose for himself Safiyyah bint Hayy ibn Akhtab.

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) forbade four things on the day of Khaybar: taking ropes from captives, eating domestic donkey, eating every creature possessing a tusk from

[Pg 208]

(1) Addition from the explanation of the news.
(2) An addition in the Virtues of Ali by Ibn al-Maghazali.
(3) Addition from the explanation of the news.
(4) Addition from the explanation of the news.

<!-- page 341 -->
313.
Battle of Khaybar

the predators, and selling spoils before they are divided.

Jabir ibn Abd-Allah (may Allah be pleased with him) reported that the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) — God — [when] (1) forbade the people from eating the meat of donkeys and permitted them to eat the meat of horses.

[What the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) Forbade on the Day of Khaybar]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) delivered a sermon at Khaybar, and the substance of his sermon (2) was:

"It is not lawful for a man who believes in Allah and the Last Day to water his crops using the irrigation of another man — meaning the tied labor of the captives. It is not lawful for a man who believes in Allah and the Last Day to approach a captive woman until she has been declared free. It is not lawful for a woman who believes in Allah [157/z] and the Last Day to sell her share until she has taken her oath and been released. Nor is it lawful for a man who believes in Allah and the Last Day to ride a mount belonging to the Muslims and then return it worn out and ruined. And it is not lawful for a man who believes in Allah and the Last Day to wear a garment belonging to the Muslims and return it to them threadbare and destroyed."

[The Prophet's (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) Reconciliation with the People of Khaybar]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family made peace with the people of Khaybar, the people of Fadak, and the people of al-Awali, on the condition that they cultivate the land and retain half of whatever it produced, and that whenever he willed he could expel them from it.

(1) An addition from the biography of the Prophet.
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 331/3–332.

<!-- page 342 -->
314 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

[The Story of the Poisoned Sheep]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had settled and felt secure, Zaynab the daughter of al-Harith, wife of Salam ibn Mishkam, presented him with a gift of a roasted sheep. She had first inquired which part of the sheep was most beloved to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family, and was told: the foreleg. She then brought it and placed it before the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family. He took the foreleg, chewed it, but could not swallow it, and called out to those present: [209] I have been poisoned. Bishr ibn al-Bara' ibn Ma'arur had also eaten from it, as had the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family — but as for the Messenger, he was the more righteous of the two. He spat it out and said: "This bone is telling me that it is [158/f] poisoned." He then summoned her and she confessed. He said: "What drove you to do this?" She said: "If you are a king, we shall be rid of you; and if you are a prophet, you will be told."

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) pardoned her, but the man who had eaten from it died from what he had consumed.

It was said that Umm Bishr bint al-Bara' ibn Ma'roor came to the Messenger of Allah (may peace be upon him) during his final illness, and he said to her: "O mother of Bishr, [this] is the time (2) — I find [in it] the severing effect of that food which I ate with your brother at Khaybar."

(1) An addition from the biography of the Prophet, 338/3.
(2) "2" and "b": "now." What has been established is from the biography of the Prophet.
(3) An addition from the biography of the Prophet, 338/3.

<!-- page 343 -->
316 The Eyes of News and the Arts of Antiquities - by Imad al-Din Idris ibn Hassan (The First Seven)

He said: "I am able to speak, O Messenger of Allah," and he said: "Then speak."

So the pilgrims came to Mecca and found the people of Mecca agitated by the news, questioning the travelers about the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family and the progress of his journey, which had reached them leading to Khaybar. They knew it to be the great stronghold of the Hijaz, fortified and well-manned. When the pilgrims arrived at Mecca, they said: This is the man with the news. Not knowing of his Islam, they said: Tell us, O Abu Muhammad — we have been informed that the one who cut ties has marched against Khaybar, which is a land of Jews and a stronghold of the Hijaz.

He said: I have heard of this and I have news that will please you. They said: What is it, O pilgrim? He said: He suffered a defeat the like of which you have never heard, his companions were slaughtered as never before, and Muhammad himself was taken captive. They said: We shall not kill him until we send word to the people of Mecca so they may kill him too. They stood up and shouted throughout Mecca: We are waiting for Muhammad to be brought in as a prisoner so we may kill him together with those who were harmed by our men. The pilgrim said: Help me collect my wealth in Mecca and among my assets — I wish to go to Khaybar, for the people of Muhammad and his companions may have gotten there ahead of me as merchants [to what you know.] (1) So they gathered everything he had for him.

[Al-Abbas Verifies the Pilgrim's News and Rebukes the Quraysh]

When al-Abbas ibn Abd al-Muttalib heard the news, he came to the pilgrim and said: "What is this news?" He said: "Guard what I entrust to you, O Abu al-Fadl, for I gathered their money using it, and I fear the consequences three times over." He said: "I shall." He said: "I swear by God, you have left your nephew as a groom to the daughter of their king."

(1) An addition from the biography of the Prophet.

<!-- page 344 -->
315
Battle of Khaybar

[The Return of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) to Madinah]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had finished at Khaybar, he proceeded to Wadi al-Qura, besieged its people for several nights, then returned to Madinah.

[The Poetry of Ibn Luqeem al-Absi on the Conquest of Khaybar]

Ibn Luqeem al-Absi said regarding the conquest of Khaybar, which took place in Safar (1):

Martyrs with ranks and poverty
I threw a jumper from the Messenger with a legion.
And I was sure of humiliation when it was rumored
And men who are Muslim in the middle of it and forgive
And the split darkened his family in a day
I became the son of Amr ibn Zar'a tomorrow
Except the chickens are screaming in the mornings
She ran with her tails and didn't let her

[Pg 158]

From a slave of Ashhal or the sons of fire
And for every fortress there is a concern of their horses
Above the strangers they intend to flee
And immigrants have learned their faces
And where are they to be zeros?
And I have learned that Ya'alban Muhammad
Under the Blindfold
The Jews fled that day in the wilderness.

[Pg 210]

When Khaybar was conquered, al-Hajjaj ibn Alaat al-Sulami came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family and said: O Messenger of Allah, I have money in Makkah with Umm Shaybah bint Abi Talha, who is his wife, and my wealth is scattered among the merchants of the people of Makkah, so grant me leave, O Messenger of Allah. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) gave him leave and said: You must —

/ [The Affair of al-Hajjaj ibn Alaat al-Sulami]

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 341/3.

<!-- page 345 -->
317.
Battle of Khaybar

ibn Akhtab — Khaybar was conquered and all that was in it became his and his companions'. He said: Is this truly what you are saying, Hajjaj? [He said:] (1) I said, "O God, keep me quiet — I accepted Islam and came only to recover my wealth, fearing that most of it [159/z] would be lost. Three days have now passed since I set out, so make your affair known, for it is God who brings about what He loves." On the third day after the pilgrim's departure, al-Aas ibn Abd al-Muttalib put on his finest garment, took up his staff, and went out until he reached the Kaaba and circumambulated it. When they saw him, they said to him: "O Abu al-Fadl, this is how one bears a calamity." He said: "No, by Allah — Muhammad conquered it by force and took the daughter of their king, Hayy ibn Akhtab, as his bride, and their wealth and all that it contained became his and his companions'." They said: Who brought you this news? He said: The same one who brought you what he brought you — and he has now become a Muslim, so he took his wealth and set out to join Muhammad and his companions and remain with them. They said: O servants of Allah! The enemy of God has slipped away — had God willed, he would have been of use to us and to them. [He said:] (2) It was not long before the news reached them.

[The Poetry of Hassan on the Day of Khaybar]

Hassan ibn Thabit said on the day of Khaybar (3):

I didn't fight Khaybar Uncle
Collected from farms and palms
And they established the act of the servile lemon.
They hated death, so their father-in-law took refuge

(1) An addition from the biography of the Prophet.
(2) An addition from the biography of the Prophet.
(3) See: Diwana 369/1.


<!-- page 346 -->
Ka'b ibn Malik said on the day of Khaybar (1):

With every naked boy the manger
And we received Khybera and loans
Bold on enemies in every scene
A horse with ends, not weak strengths
I will call the supervisor Almohannad
Great is the ashes of destiny in every winter
From God they hope for her and win Ahmed
He sees killing as praise if he hits a martyrdom
And he pushes him with the tongue and the hand
Defend and protect Dhamar Mohammed
He turns with the same without the soul of Mohammed

[212] And He will help him from every matter that He shows
He wants to win and glory tomorrow
He believes in the news in the unseen, sincere

[Mention of the arrival of Ja'far ibn Abi Talib]

Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) arrived on the day of the conquest of Khaybar, and the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — came and said: (2) "I do not know which of the two brings me greater joy: the opening of Khaybar or the coming of Ja'far." He was accompanied by his wife, Asmaa bint Umays, and his son Abdullah ibn Ja'far, who had been born in the land of Abyssinia, along with a group of Muslims who had emigrated to Abyssinia.

(1) See: Diwana 164.
(2) See: Sira al-Nabawiyyah 359/4, Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 34/4-35, Ansab al-Ashraf 223/1, Sharh al-Akhbar 204/3, Al-Khasal 77, Muqatil al-Talibin 30.

<!-- page 347 -->
[Umrah of the Qada' in Dhu al-Qa'dah in the year seven]

After returning from Khaybar, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — remained in Madinah through the months of Rabi', the two Jamadas, Rajab, Sha'ban, Ramadan, and Shawwal. He then set out to perform the Umrah — the same Umrah that the polytheists had previously turned him away from. The Muslims went out with him, and when the people of Makkah heard of his approach they withdrew from his path. The Quraysh said among themselves that Muhammad and his companions were worn down by hardship and exhaustion. They therefore gathered at the arcade of the sanctuary to observe him and his companions. When the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — entered, he wrapped his robe under his right arm, baring it, and said: "May God have mercy on the man who shows them his strength today." He then touched the corner and set off at a brisk trot, with his companions trotting alongside him, until the house was between him and the crowd; then he took hold of the Yemeni corner and walked the rest. He ran the first three circuits in this manner, and walked the remaining ones.

The Muslims came to understand that the Prophet had done this only because the Quraysh were watching, wishing to display strength. When he performed the Farewell Pilgrimage he continued the practice, and it became an established Sunnah.

[The rajaz of Ibn Rawahah driving the Prophet's camel]

It is said that when the Prophet — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — entered Makkah during that Umrah, Abdullah ibn Rawahah took hold of his camel's halter and said:

Clear the way, sons of the unbelievers, from his path

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 371/4.

<!-- page 348 -->
Clear away — in his Messenger is all that is good

O God, I believe in him

[0/161]

I scoop God's right to accept it

The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — remained in Makkah for three days, after which the Quraysh urged him to depart.

[What was revealed from the Qur'an during 'Umrah al-Qada']

It is said that during that Umrah the following verse was revealed to him: "Allah has fulfilled the vision of His Messenger in truth: you shall enter the Sacred Mosque, God willing, in security, with your heads shaved and hair cut short, without fear — for He knew what you did not know, and He has appointed a near victory besides that" [al-Fath: 27].

The "near victory" mentioned here refers to the conquest of Khaybar.

<!-- page 349 -->
[Mention of the Battle of Mu'tah in Jumada al-Awwal in the year eight]

After returning from Umrah, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — remained in Madinah through Dhu al-Hijjah — while the polytheists oversaw that pilgrimage season — and then through Muharram, Safar, and the months of Rabi'.

The Battle of Mu'tah took place in Jumada al-Awwal of the year eight. The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — dispatched an army of Muslims to Mu'tah and appointed his cousin Ja'far ibn Abi Talib to command them, saying: "If Ja'far is struck down, then Zayd ibn Haritha shall lead the people; if Zayd is struck down, then 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah shall lead."

[The poetry of Abdullah ibn Rawaha bidding farewell to the Prophet]

And fate has swept it before him —
You are the Prophet, who could forbid his prayers?
Among the messengers and a victory like those who triumphed —
May God grant you all the good that came to you.
A countenance with which whoever looks upon you is blessed —
I have been graced with good through you.

These verses are from a longer poem of his.

The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — came out to see Ja'far ibn Abi Talib and his companions off. When he had bidden them farewell and they were departing, 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah said:

Behind peace be upon a man I have called in the gathering — the finest and most radiant of men.

[The people's fear at the news of Heraclius]

They marched on until they halted at Ma'an, in the land of the Levant, where news reached them that Heraclius had

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 374/4.

<!-- page 350 -->
descended upon Ma'ab, in the land of Balqa', with a hundred thousand Romans, and that he had been joined by a hundred thousand men from the Arab tribes of Lakhm, Judham, Bahra', and Bali.

When this news reached the Muslims, they remained at Ma'an for two nights deliberating over their situation. Some said: "Let us write to the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — and inform him of the numbers they face, so that he may either send us reinforcements or give us his command, and we shall follow it."

[Abdullah ibn Rawaha urges the people to advance]

But Ja'far ibn Abi Talib and 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah encouraged the people, saying: "We did not march out seeking martyrdom in order to shy away from it when the enemy outnumbers us. We do not fight by force of numbers, nor by strength or equipment — we fight with this religion by which God has honoured us. [5/162] So advance, for one of two good outcomes awaits us: either victory or martyrdom." And the people set out.

'Abdullah ibn Rawahah (1) said:

We brought the horses from Aga and Branch
She is tempted by hashish for her
We followed her example from the conservation
Remove as if his page is eternal
She spent two nights on Ma'an
After a while, he followed a crowd.
Breathe poison in her nostrils
Our joy and neutrality are bad
And if it had Arabs and Romans
Neither nor my father, let us come to her.
Awabs and dust have a breem
So we filled it and it came.

[Pg 215]

If its bows protrude
It is as if the eggs are in it

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 375/4-376.

<!-- page 351 -->
The woman content with living has divorced her and remarried.

[Mention of the encounter with the Romans]

The army pressed forward until they were near Balqa', where the forces of Heraclius — Romans and Arabs together — met them in one of the villages of Balqa', said to be called Masharif. The enemy then drew near, and the Muslims withdrew to a village called Mu'tah and formed their battle ranks. On the right wing they placed a man from Banu 'Udhra, known as Qutbah ibn Qatadah, and on the left wing a man from the Ansar known as 'Abayah ibn Malik.

[Ja'far's command and his death]

The armies met and battle was joined. Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) took command of the troops — it is also said he took the banner — and when the fighting grew fierce around him and he feared for his horse, he dismounted and hamstrung it, then fought on foot. As he fought, he recited:

How good is Paradise drawing near,
Cold its drink and sweet its fragrance,
While Rome faces punishment —
A nation of unbelievers, far removed.

He fought on — peace be upon him — bearing the standard on his right, until his right hand was cut off; he then took it up with his left, until that too was cut off, and he clasped the banner to his chest with his arms until he fell as a martyr — peace be upon him. He was thirty-three years old, and God rewarded him with two wings with which he flies in Paradise as He wills.

It is also said that a man from Rome struck him a single blow and cut him in two.

(1) An increase from "B".
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 378/4.

<!-- page 352 -->
[The command of Abdullah ibn Rawahah and his death]

When Ja'far (peace be upon him) was martyred, Zayd ibn Haritha took up the banner and fought with it.

[Pg 216]

The spears of the enemy pressed in upon him until he was killed — may God have mercy on him. When he fell, 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah took it up. He rode forward on his horse, and then hesitated for a moment. Then he said (1):

I swear to you, O soul, you shall not be disgraced —
Dismount, or what you hate will come.
The people have tightened their reins for battle —
I see you loathe nothing but the cut of a blade.
How long have you dwelt at ease in this frame?
Are you not merely a drop in its vessel?

[Pg 163/f]

O soul — unless you are killed, you die.

He also said (2):

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 379/4.
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 379/4.

<!-- page 353 -->
[Pg 217]

This is the dove of death — I have wooed her,
And what I longed for, I have been given.
If you do as they did, you shall be guided —

— meaning his companions Zayd and Ja'far. Then he pressed forward and fought until he was killed — may Allah be pleased with him. (1)

Thabit ibn Akram, of the Banu 'Ajlan, then took the banner and handed it to Khalid ibn al-Walid, who accepted it and rallied the troops until the enemy withdrew.

[The Prophet's foreknowledge of what befell the Muslims]

When the army suffered its losses, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — said to his companions: (2) "[Zayd ibn Haritha took the banner and fought with it until he was killed as a martyr. Then] (3) Ja'far ibn Abi Talib [took it] (4) and held it until he too was killed. Then 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah took the banner of Zayd ibn Haritha and [held it] (6) until he too was killed as a martyr." Then the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — fell silent, and the faces of the Ansar changed,

(1) An increase from "B".
(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 380/4.
(3) (2) and (b): "In" — what is confirmed is from the Prophet's biography.
(4) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(5) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(6) An increase in the Prophet's biography.

<!-- page 354 -->
for they feared that 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah had done something they would find blameworthy. Then the Prophet said: "Abdullah ibn Rawahah took it and bore it until he was killed as a martyr." Then he said: "They were raised up, and I beheld them reclining on couches of gold. I saw the bed of 'Abdullah ibn Rawahah set apart from the beds of his two companions — and I asked, 'What is this?' I was told: 'Abdullah had hesitated for a moment, and then went forward.'"

[The Prophet mourns Ja'far and takes care of his family]

When the news of Ja'far and his companions arrived, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — went to Asmaa bint Umays and said: "Bring me the sons of Ja'far." She brought them to him, and his eyes filled with tears. She said: "By my father and my mother, O Messenger of Allah — what makes you weep? Have you received news of Ja'far and his companions?" He said: "Yes — they were struck down today."

Asmaa cried out in grief, and the women gathered around her.

The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — went out to his family and said: "Do not neglect the family of Ja'far — make food for them, for they are occupied with a grief that has overwhelmed them."

When the news of his death came from Mu'tah, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — said: "He walks among a company of angels, on two white wings with which he flies wherever God wills."

The Prophet was deeply grieved for him, and he loved him dearly, and he said:

(1) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 39/4, Maghazi al-Waqidi 762/2, Al-Manaqib wa al-Muqalab [leaf 40/f], Understanding in the Knowledge of Companions 242/1.

<!-- page 355 -->
[Pg 218]

(1): "People were created from different trees, but Ja'far and I were created from a single tree."

[He said in another hadith (2): "We, the family of 'Abd al-Muttalib, are from one tree. Ja'far and I are from one of its branches — his nature is like my nature, and his character is my character."]

[News of Qutbah ibn Qatadah]

Qutbah ibn Qatadah al-'Adhri, who had been stationed on the right wing of the Muslims, had charged at Malik [5/164] ibn Zafilah and killed him while he was before him. He was among the commanders of the disbelievers on the day of Mu'tah. Qutbah ibn Qatadah (3) al-'Adhri (4) said:

I thrust at Ibn Zafilah ibn al-Irash
With a spear that pierced him through and then shattered —
And money is like the tip of a rung of a ladder.
I called out at the good of his strike.
The next day came the market of blessings,
And we gave the women of his kin to drink.

[From what Asmaa bint Umays said in mourning Ja'far (peace be upon him)]

Asmaa bint Umays, weeping for Ja'far (peace be upon him), said:

O Ja'far — the pilot, finest of horsemen on the day of din and clamour.

(1) See: Muqatil al-Talibin 34, Characteristics of the Commander of the Faithful 221-232, Sharh al-Akhbar 305/3, History of the City of Damascus (vol. 93) 158.
(2) See: Tabaqat Ibn Sa'd 36/4, al-Isti'ab 243/1.
(3) An increase in the Prophet's biography.
(4) See: Biography of the Prophet, 381/4.
(5) See: Sharh al-Ahbar 3/207, Tahzeeb al-Kamal 5/63, and verses (2-5) attributed to Fatima bint al-Azam al-Khara'iyyah. See: Amali al-Qali 1/2, al-Bashir al-Bashir 67/2-671.


<!-- page 356 -->
So you left me to walk a barren road.
I had a mountain whose shade I sheltered in —
I was a fever, and no life remained in me.
I walked on, wings folded, and then spread them wide.
And if the Qamaria prays for her, they will plead on her behalf,
one day in the hour of my morning prayer.
Today I bow at the tail end and make myself pure
from him, and I will repay my oppressors with the wind.

[Hassan's poetry mourning the dead of Mu'tah]

Hassan ibn Thabit said, mourning him and his companions who fell at Mu'tah(1):

While people slept, they kept watch —
you repent of me, drinking through the night with the left hand.
Foothills and reasons enough for a man to weep,
for the memory of Hotep has erased a lesson in me.
How many generous souls are struck down and yet remain patient —
yes, the loss of a beloved is a true disaster.
Peoples and those who come after them are delayed.
I saw the finest of the Muslims brought low
by a death that gave Ja'far two wings.
So may God not keep the dead far from His mercy —
all of them, and the causes of their deaths are proclaimed.

[Pg 219] [164/z] And Zayd and 'Abd-Allah, when they followed
death — an auspicious captain, radiant as the dawn.
The next day they went forth with the believers leading them.
My father — if the darkness is worn out with grief,
I am drawn, like the light of the full moon, from the family of Hashim,
in a battle where the spear shafts break.
He struck until even unguarded wealth was taken.
Silk and gardens, green and wrapped around him —
so he joined the martyrs and found his reward.
Loyalty and firm command when he gives the order.
We used to see Ja'far as one of Muhammad's own.

(1) See: Diwan, 98/1–99.

<!-- page 357 -->
[Pg 165/f]

Battle of Mu'tah

The pillars of pride stand proud still,
he remains in Islam, from the family of Hashim.
Content with an expulsion that pleases and conquers —
they are the core of Islam and the people who surround them.
Ali, and among them Ahmad al-Mukhtar.
Many among them are Ja'far and his mother's son,
'Aqeel — and the water of the oud drawn from where it is pressed.
Hamza and 'Abbas are of them, and they release
the Alawis in every predicament.
Even when people have exhausted their sources,
they are the guardians of God, upon whom He sends down His wisdom,
and among them is the Purified Book.

[Ka'b's poetry mourning the dead of Mu'tah]

Ka'b ibn Malik said in this regard(1):

Let eyes sleep while their tears neglect the sky, as the doctors have stopped treating the sting.
In a night her worries returned to me — tender at first, then restless.
I was long acquainted with grief, and so I became like one carried in a coffin, the fish committed to the deep.
From what Shihab has entered —
it is as if between the creatures and the stuffing there is no space.
One day they supported him and did not flinch,
and they found the people who would follow.
Their bones were watered by the clouds that poured down.
May God pray upon them — among the finest of sons,
who warned the repugnant and feared the punishment that would come.
They were patient in surrendering their souls to God;
the iron was brought down upon them.
So they went forth at the head of the Muslims as though
guided by Ja'far and his brigade.
At the front of the vanguard — how fine a vanguard it was —
where they met the enemy and pressed through the ranks,
until the ranks were overwhelmed, and Ja'far among them.

(1) See: Mad, 205–206.

<!-- page 358 -->

The sun was shy and nearly faded away;
[220] the moon changed on account of his loss.
Qurm Ola is built by Hashim —
I breathe in a branch and repeat what has been transmitted.
Upon them the Book came down into their house;
God raised them up through His infallibility as His servants.
Their forbearance sheathed those who were ignorant,
preferring life that is hard yet generous.
See how their betrothed is truly divorced —
they do not release their love to the foolish.
You cry out when the pomegranate is sold.
The Divine — you see the palms of their hands —
and their grandfather is the victory of the sent Prophet.
I give them the satisfaction of God for His creation.

A Muslim man who returned from the Battle of Mu'tah [165/] said:

And Zayd and 'Abd-Allah — in sorrow they loom larger than ever.
Enough grief that I returned, while Ja'far did not.
I left the calamity behind, covered in dust.
They died when they set out on their own paths
toward a fate they had long hated — red with death.
Three brave souls stepped forward and advanced.

[Naming those who were martyred on the day of Mu'tah]

Those Muslims who were martyred at Mu'tah:

Ja'far ibn Abi Talib — peace be upon him — cousin of the Messenger of Allah — peace be upon him and his family.

And Zayd ibn Haritha, the mawla of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family.

And Mas'ud ibn al-Aswad, from Banu 'Adi ibn Ka'b.

Wahab ibn Sa'd [ibn Abi Sarh], from Banu Malik ibn Hassal. These

(2) See: Biography of the Prophet, 388/4.

<!-- page 359 -->

Battle of Mu'tah

[Pg 221]

four are from the Emigrants.

Among the Ansar:

'Abd-Allah ibn Rawaha.
And 'Abbad ibn Qays.
Al-Harith ibn al-Nu'man.
And Saraqa ibn 'Amr.
Abu Kulayb and Jabir, sons of 'Amr ibn Zayd, who shared both father and mother.
Or 'Amr and 'Amir, sons of Sa'd.

These are twelve men in total.

<!-- page 360 -->

[Mention of the reasons for the march to Mecca and its conquest in the month of Ramadan in the year eight]

The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — remained(1) after the army had been sent to Mu'tah throughout Jumada al-Akhirah and Rajab.

Then the matter arose of nullifying the treaty between him and the Quraysh, because Banu Bakr ibn 'Abd Manat ibn Kinana had attacked Khuza'a.

Khuza'ah had entered into a treaty [with the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — and his covenant](2), and Banu Bakr had entered the era of the Quraysh [and their covenant].

There had been(3) old feuds and fighting between them from the pre-Islamic era, but Islam had held them in check. They were bound by the truce of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family.

When [166/f] Banu al-Dail of Banu Bakr seized upon the truce to attack Khuza'a and sought to take revenge against them — Khuza'ah being encamped at al-Watir, a watering place of theirs — they fell upon them, wounding some of their men. Fighting broke out and men were killed. The Quraysh supplied Banu Bakr with weapons, and some of the Quraysh even joined the fighting secretly under cover of night, until one party took a position inside the Sacred Precinct.

Lufl ibn Mu'awiya al-Dili, of Banu Bakr, said:
"We have entered the Haram — your God, your God!"
He replied: "There is no god for Him today, O Banu Bakr — take your revenge! For by my life, do you not steal even within the Haram? Will you not take your revenge there?"

(1) An addition from "B".
(2) Between the two passages: Biography of the Prophet, 38/4.
(3) "B": "Felonies."

<!-- page 361 -->

Conquest of Mecca

So Khuza'ah took refuge in the house of Badil ibn Warqa', and in the house of their master, whose name was said to be Rafi', after the Quraysh and Banu Bakr had killed their men.

[The poetry of 'Amr ibn Salim al-Khuza'i to the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him and his family — seeking his support]

When Banu Bakr and the Quraysh moved against Khuza'a and wounded some of them, breaking the covenant between themselves and the Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings be upon him — and violating the covenant that gave Khuza'ah his protection and placed them under his treaty, 'Amr ibn [222] Salim al-Khuza'i set out until he reached the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — in [166] Medina, while he was sitting in his mosque among the Emigrants and the Ansar, and he said:

O Lord, I call upon Muhammad
by the oath that is bound and firm.
You were the young ones and we were the elders,
then we submitted, and she did not withdraw her hand.
So God granted you victory —
let the servants of God come to our aid.
The Messenger of Allah has been stripped bare by them;
if he is left weak, his face will be brought low.
Come with a host like the surging, foaming sea.
The Quraysh have betrayed you at the appointed time.

(1) See: Biography of the Prophet, 394/4–395.

<!-- page 362 -->

They have revoked your firmly confirmed covenant
and left me in a state of bewilderment.
They claimed that I call upon no one —
they are humiliated and few in number.
They seek to make us falter,
and they have killed us while we were kneeling and prostrating.
So grant us victory — may God guide you with a decisive, conquering victory.

He said: "O 'Amr ibn Salim, you have been answered."

Then — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — a cloud appeared in the sky, and he said: "This cloud heralds the victory of Bani Ka'b."

[Badil ibn Warqa's journey to the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him and his family — as a complainant]

[223] Then Badil ibn Warqa' set out with a group of Khuza'a until they reached the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — in Medina. They told him what had been done to them and the attack that the Quraysh and Banu Bakr had launched against them. Then they turned back to Mecca, and the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — said to those around him: [167/f] "It is as if Abu Sufyan has come to you to tighten the covenant and extend its term."

Badil ibn Warqa' and his companions went on until they encountered Abu Sufyan ibn Harb in 'Asfan, for the Quraysh had sent him to the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — to reinforce the treaty, extend its term, and reassure those who had broken it.

<!-- page 363 -->

Conquest of Mecca

[Abu Sufyan's journey to Medina for reconciliation and his failure]

Abu Sufyan came [until he reached the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family](1) in Medina. He entered upon his daughter, Umm Habiba bint Abi Sufyan, wife of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him. When he moved to sit on the bed of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — she folded it away from beneath him. He said: "O daughter, I do not know whether you found me unworthy of this bed, or whether you found the bed unworthy of me." She said: "It is the bed of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — and you are a polytheist and impure. I did not wish you to sit on the bed of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him." He said: "By Allah, evil has come upon you, O daughter, since leaving me."

Umm Habiba bint Abi Sufyan had emigrated to Abyssinia with her husband, who died there. The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — then sent word to the Negus to betroth her to him, and she thereafter presented herself before the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family. She became one of his wives.

When Abu Sufyan went out, he came to the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — and spoke to him, but he received no reply.

So he came to Abu Bakr and asked him to speak to [him](2) on his behalf. The Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — said: "I shall not do so."

(1) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 396/4.
(2) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 396/4.

<!-- page 364 -->

Then 'Umar ibn al-Khattab came, and Abu Sufyan appealed to him in the same way. 'Umar answered: "We cannot approach the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — with what he dislikes."

He went out to 'Ali ibn Abi Talib — peace be upon him — who was with Fatima, daughter of

[Pg 224]

the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — and al-Hasan ibn 'Ali was there as a small boy. He said: "O 'Ali, have mercy on me yesterday's guest, for I am in need. Let me not return as I came, empty-handed. Intercede for me with the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family."

He said, rubbing his hands: "O Abu Sufyan! By Allah, the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — has resolved upon something, and nothing we could say will change his mind."

He turned to Fatima — peace be upon her — and said: "O daughter of Muhammad, can you command your son — meaning al-Hasan, peace be upon him — to extend his protection to the people, so that he becomes a master of the Arabs until the end of time?"

She said: "By Allah, my son has not yet reached the point of extending protection over the people, and no one can intercede against the decision of the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family."

He said: "O Abu al-Hasan, I see that matters have grown difficult for me — give me your counsel."

(1) Ibn Hisham said: "Then 'Umar ibn al-Khattab came and spoke to him, and said: 'Should I intercede for you with the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family? By God, if I had nothing but a seed's worth, I would use it against you. We cannot bring the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — what he dislikes.'" The resources, however, were not in his hands.
(2) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 396/4.

<!-- page 365 -->

Conquest of Mecca

He said: "By Allah, I know of nothing that will serve you — yet you are the master of Banu Kinana. So stand among the people and extend your protection, and then make your way back to your land."

He said: "Do you think that [168/f] this will avail me anything?"

He said: "No, by Allah, I do not think so — but I can find nothing else for you."

Abu Sufyan stood up in the mosque among the people and said: "O people, I hereby extend my protection among all people." Then he mounted his camel and set off. When he arrived in Mecca to his people, they asked: "What news do you bring?" He said: "Muhammad spoke not a word to me, and by Allah, he gave me no answer. Then I went to Ibn al-Khattab and found him the most hostile of enemies."](1) Then I came to 'Ali and found him the most gentle of the people, and [he] pointed me toward something I did — though I do not know whether it will avail anything or not." They said: "And what did he order you?" He said: "He ordered me to extend my protection among the people, and I did so." [They said: Did Muhammad sanction that?(3)] They said: "Woe to you! By God, he has done nothing but play with your mind." He said: "By Allah, I found nothing better than that."

[The Prophet's — peace and blessings be upon him and his family — preparations for the conquest of Mecca]

Then the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family — informed the people that he was setting out for [225] Mecca. He ordered them to prepare and make ready, and he said: "O Allah, take from the eyes

(1) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 396/4.
(2) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 397/4.
(3) An addition from the Biography of the Prophet, 397/4.


<!-- page 366 -->
And news of the Quraysh and those with them, until they proclaimed it in their own country." So the people were prepared.

[Hassan's Poetry Inciting the People]

Hassan ibn Thabit spoke up, inciting the people and recalling the wounded men of Khuza'ah:

I have not seen the men of Bani Ka'b in the presence of Mecca,
In the hands of men who did not draw their swords,
And many people did not wear their armour.
If only my hair could win you my victory?
Suhail ibn 'Umar and her punishment —
This is the time of war; he drew out its sinews.
And Safwan — we long for the edge of his blade.
If it is purely milked and I shave its fangs,
Then do not trust us, son of Umm Mahalid.
She has a fall in death that opens her door,
And do not fear us — for our swords are ready.

[A Letter Written to the Quraysh, and the Prophet Learning of It]

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) assembled his forces to march on Mecca, Hatib ibn Abi Balta'ah wrote a letter to the Quraysh informing them of what the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) had resolved to do — namely, to march against them. He entrusted the letter to a woman — said to be a freed slave of some of the Banu 'Abd al-Muttalib — on the condition that she deliver it to the Quraysh. She concealed it in her hair, twisting her locks around it, and set out.

Then news of what Hatib had done came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) from heaven. He sent 'Ali ibn Abi Talib and al-Zubayr ibn al-'Awwam, saying: "Go — you will find a woman who has

<!-- page 367 -->
with her a letter that Hatib ibn Abi Balta'ah wrote to the Quraysh, warning them of what we are gathering against them."

They set out and overtook her at a place known as the grove of Bani Abi Ahmad. They dismounted her from her mount, searched her baggage, and found nothing. Then 'Ali (peace be upon him) said to her: "By Allah, I have not lied and I have not been misled — bring out this letter, or we will strip your clothing." When she saw the resolve in him, she said, "I will show it." She turned away, loosened her braids, and drew out the letter from within them. She handed it over, and it was brought to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

He summoned Hatib and said, "What drove you to do this?"

Hatib said: "O Messenger of Allah, I am a believer in Allah and His Messenger — I have not changed nor wavered. But I was a man among the Quraysh who had no family or clan to protect his people and children there, so I sought to earn their favour through this."

'Umar ibn al-Khattab said: "O Messenger of Allah, let me strike his neck — he has committed treason." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "And what do you know, O 'Umar? Perhaps Allah has already looked upon the people of Badr and said: 'Do as you wish, for I have forgiven you.'"

Then Allah revealed concerning Hatib: *"O you who believe, do not take*

<!-- page 368 -->
*My enemy and your enemy as allies, offering them affection, when they have disbelieved in what has come to you of the truth, driving out the Messenger and yourselves because you believe in Allah, your Lord — if you have gone forth to strive in My cause and to seek My pleasure. You confide to them affection, while I know best what you have concealed and what you have revealed"* — up to the words of the Almighty: *"You have had a good example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people: 'We are innocent of you and of what you worship besides Allah. We disbelieve in you, and there has appeared between us and you enmity and hatred forever, until you believe in Allah alone'"* [al-Mumtahanah: 1–4], and so on to the end of the passage.

Then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) set out to conquer Mecca when ten nights of Ramadan had passed.

[Fasting Mentioned in This Context]

We narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq, from his father, from his forefathers (peace be upon them): "The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) travelled in the month of Ramadan, and he broke his fast and commanded those with him to break their fast. Some people, however, refused to break their fast, and so they were called the disobedient — because Allah (may He be exalted) had commanded them through His Prophet, and they did not obey his command."

[On Shortening the Prayer While Travelling]

Narrated to us from Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace be upon him), from his father, from 'Ali ibn

<!-- page 369 -->
Abu Talib (peace be upon him), who said that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah Almighty has given my nation a gift that He did not give to any nation before them — an honour from Him, may He be glorified and exalted." They asked: "And what is that, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "Breaking the fast and shortening the prayer while travelling. Whoever does not do so has rejected Allah's gift."

It was also narrated from 'Ali (peace be upon him) that he said: "Whoever shortens his prayer when travelling and breaks his fast has accepted the relief Allah has extended to him."

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali al-Baqir said: "Whoever completes his prayer while travelling has done wrong."

He was asked about prayer on a journey — how much it is and how much is required. He said: "Allah the Almighty says: *'And when you travel in the land, there is no blame on you if you shorten the prayer'* [al-Nisa': 101]." He said: "Shortening on a journey is obligatory, just as completing the prayer in one's city is obligatory."

It was said to him: "O son of the Messenger of Allah, Allah said: 'there is no blame on you' — which implies permission, not obligation. How then do you say it is obligatory?" He said: "Did not Allah say: *'Indeed, al-Safa and al-Marwah are among the rituals of Allah, so whoever performs Hajj or 'Umrah — there is no blame on him if he goes back and forth between them'* [al-Baqarah: 158]? Do you not see that walking between them is obligatory? For Allah mentioned them in His Book, and the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) enacted it as a practice."

<!-- page 370 -->
In the same way, shortening the prayer on a journey was mentioned by Allah and enacted by the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

From 'Ali (peace be upon him): "The Messenger of Allah forbade completing the full prayer while travelling."

And Ja'far ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq (peace be upon him) said: "I disown those who pray four rak'ahs on a journey."

This discussion could go on at length, but we have included it here because there is a genuine difference of opinion over whether shortening the prayer on a journey is obligatory — notwithstanding the consensus that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) shortened his prayer on journeys, and that this is his practice and his example.

[Return: The Prophet's Departure in Ramadan]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then set out toward Mecca with ten thousand Muslims. With him were the Muhajirun and the Ansar, and no one of consequence had remained behind. Sulaym and other Arab tribes had embraced Islam, and so all the Muslims gathered in full force with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

[The Islam of Abu Sufyan ibn al-Harith and 'Abdullah ibn Abi Umayyah]

Abu Sufyan ibn al-Harith ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib and 'Abdullah ibn Abi Umayyah ibn

<!-- page 371 -->
al-Mughirah met the Prophet at a waypoint between Medina and Mecca and sought permission to enter his presence, but he did not grant it. Umm Salamah spoke to him on their behalf and said: "O Messenger of Allah — your cousin and the son of your aunt, and your brother-in-law."

He said: "I have no need of them. As for my cousin, he violated my honour, and as for my cousin — he is the one who said what he said in Mecca."

When this response reached them, Abu Sufyan said: "By Allah, he must give me permission, or I will take this son of mine by the hand and we will wander the land until we die of thirst and hunger."

When word reached the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) of what he had said, he relented and granted them permission to enter. They came before him and greeted him, and he returned their greeting.

[Abu Sufyan's Apology in Verse for His Past]

Abu Sufyan ibn al-Harith composed verses marking his conversion to Islam and expressing his remorse for his former conduct. He said:

For the swiftness of Muhammad's horses' reins,
I shall never carry a flag against them again.
These are the vessels — I have been guided and shown the way.
The messenger of confusion is the darkest of nights.

<!-- page 372 -->
By God, who drove away every exile,
He guided me — this changed me and set me right.
And I pray, though I was not affiliated with Muhammad;
I had turned away and kept my distance from Muhammad.
And if this is an opinion that will gather and bring tomorrow,
They are the ones who held back from professing their love.
Among the people — unless I be guided in every gathering —
I seek to please them, and I am no scoundrel.

I say to you: I have no desire to fight it. I tell you plainly: I pledge to another.
It was not from the picking of my tongue or my hands.
I was not in the arena that claimed 'Amr.
Disputes that came from arrows and accounts —
She said it came from several lands.

[The Story of Abu Sufyan ibn Harb's Islam at the Hands of al-'Abbas]

Al-'Abbas ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib encountered the Muslim army at al-Juhfah, emigrating with his family — having previously been responsible for supplying water to pilgrims — and the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was pleased with him.

When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) descended at Marr al-Zahran, al-'Abbas said to himself: "Woe to the Quraysh! By Allah, if the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) enters Mecca by force before they come out to him and seek his pledge of security, it will be the destruction of the Quraysh for all time."

He mounted the mule of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and rode out until he reached the Arak trees. He said: "Perhaps I will find a woodcutter, or someone with a milk animal, or a pilgrim heading to Mecca who can tell them where the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) is, so they can go out to him and seek his security before he enters by force."

<!-- page 373 -->
On that same night, Abu Sufyan ibn Harb had left Mecca together with Hakim ibn Hizam and Badil ibn Warqa', scouting for news and trying to learn what was happening — for news of the Muslim forces had been kept from the Quraysh, and nothing had reached them about the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family).

Al-'Abbas said: By Allah, I had not gone far on the mule of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family), seeking what I had gone out for, when I heard the voices of Abu Sufyan and Badil ibn Warqa' as they argued. Abu Sufyan was saying: "I have never seen anything like tonight — there is no fire nor army visible."

Badil said to him: "This must be Khuza'ah, stirred up by the war."

Abu Sufyan said: "Khuza'ah is too few and too lowly to account for all these fires and that army."

Al-'Abbas said: I recognised Abu Sufyan's voice and called out: "O Abu Hanzalah!" He recognised me and said: "Abu al-Fadl?" I said, "Yes." He said: "What has brought you? May my father and mother be your ransom!"

I said: "O Abu Sufyan — this is the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) with his army, and the morning of the Quraysh will be the worst morning they have ever known, by Allah!" He said: "What is to be done? May my father and mother be your ransom!"

Al-'Abbas said: I told him: "By Allah, if they seize you they will kill you. Ride behind me on this mule and go to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) so that I may seek his pledge of security for you."

He mounted behind me, and his two companions turned back. I brought him along, and whenever we passed by a fire

<!-- page 374 -->
from among the fires of the Muslims, they would ask: "Who is this?" Then they would see the mule of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and me upon it, and they would say: "The uncle of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) on his mule." And so they let us pass — until we came to the fire of 'Umar ibn al-Khattab, who called out: "Who is this?" and rose to his feet. When he saw Abu Sufyan on the back of the mule, he rushed forward.

'Umar said: "O Messenger of Allah, this is Abu Sufyan, whom Allah has delivered into our hands without any covenant or pledge — let me strike his neck!" Al-'Abbas said: "I have given him my protection, O Messenger of Allah." Then I said: "'Umar, if he were a man of Banu 'Uday ibn Ka'b, you would not have said this — but you know well that he is a man of Banu 'Abd Manaf."

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Take him to your quarters, O 'Abbas, and if morning comes, bring him to me."

Al-'Abbas said: So I took him to my lodging and he spent the night with me. In the morning, I brought him to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him). When the Prophet saw him, he said: "Woe to you, O Abu Sufyan — do you not yet know that there is no god but Allah?" He replied: "By my father and my mother —


<!-- page 375 -->
